# OT1997 Term Continuity Note

**Status:** Cumulative replacement after OT_1997CHUNK5: 60 completed scheduled matters through March 23, 1998, represented by 61 Court-action Records, plus one admitted noncase source. Chunk 5 completes 10 matters in 11 event Records; 2 matters are stopped. Hetzel has distinct same-date grant and summary-merits Records. All earlier public law and published positions are retained. Records remain authoritative over this projection.

## Scope and Chronology Cursor

**October Term:** OT1997. **Chunk covered:** OT_1997CHUNK5. **Current completed chronology cursor:** March 23, 1998. **Next scheduled item:** Cohen v. de la Cruz, No. 96-1923, March 24, 1998, OT_1997CHUNK6. 60 scheduled Court events are completed and 101 scheduled items remain open or stopped. Completed filing actions leave any underlying petitions pending only at the stages actually recorded. The five opening carryovers retain their established stages. The divergence remains October 7, 1991.

The opening baseline consists of the governing versions of [Holdings](../../../state/HOLDINGS.md), [Standards and Tests](../../../state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md), and [Standing State](../../../state/STANDING_STATE.md): OT1996 completed through September 15, 1997, with 153 Court events and three admitted source events. The final prior event was Harrison v. Eddy Potash, Inc., Tenth Circuit Nos. 96-2045 and 96-2065. The synchronized [doctrinal Holdings volumes](../../../state/holdings/) remain the preferred full-law reading source. Those identified opening publications govern with the dated current-term public holdings below; this projection does not replace their qualifications, precedent treatment, or recorded authority.

The [current manifest](manifest.md) covers all 161 scheduled matters and the five separate opening carryovers. This replacement incorporates completed public actions for 60 scheduled events spanning October 20, 1997–March 23, 1998; 101 scheduled events remain open or stopped. Jefferson is completed as a jurisdictional dismissal. Brown's filing application is completed while its certiorari petition remains pending as a distinct stage. The completed Court events supply no roster or allotment change. Completion status follows the public actions below; prospective inventory labels do not establish outcomes. The subsequently admitted [Pub. L. 105-119, §123](../records/plra_section_123_amendments_admitted_source_1997-11-26.md#public-projection) separately changes §3626 from November 26, 1997; its admission leaves the completed chunk-1 adjudications intact, and Rouse applies the amended pending-case preservation condition on January 15, 1998. The term therefore contains 62 completed Records: 61 Court-action Records and this one admitted source. The scheduled inventory counts 60 completed matters; Hetzel has separate grant and summary-merits actions on March 23.

Events sharing a date use a common pre-group law unless a dependency is established. The November 4, November 17, December 2, December 9, and December 15, 1997 groups, the January 13, 1998 Fink–Baker group, the January 21 Buchanan–LaChance/McManus–Lunding group, and the January 26 Allentown Mack–Brogan–Oubre–Yankton Sioux Tribe group have no established within-group dependency; reading or recording one peer first does not supply law to another. Earlier effective events may enter later events: Richenberg expressly addresses the November 17 Jeffries holding within its stated limits. Unscheduled carryovers keep their existing stage until a supported date and action are supplied.

## Current Law

The identified opening Holdings and Standards continue in full except as precisely changed below. Each proposition becomes operative on its own decision date. Albrecht's vertical maximum-price per se rule is replaced on November 4; the other specified statute-specific constructions and applications operate within their stated scopes. The §3626 amendments described below are effective from November 26, 1997. Controlling authority is proposition-specific. Fields, decided February 9, 1998, has distinct majority propositions and separate judgment-supporting application rationales; no unified controlling search or entry application is supplied by combining those rationales. Its complete public blocks below preserve the direct majorities, plurality, concurrence, dissent and limits on a Marks synthesis. Clanton leaves Albright's incompatible rationales without a controlling synthesis. Published separate positions appear in the next block and supply no controlling rule.

### October 20, 1997 — Brown v. Williams

[Public Projection](../records/brown_v_williams_filing_order_1997-10-20.md#public-projection). The per curiam Court applies existing filing-access law. Rule 39 financial eligibility and Rule 39.8 screening remain separate: denial requires a supported finding that the particular submission is frivolous or malicious. Eight prior unsuccessful petitions and an earlier denial do not establish that predicate for the present paper. Access allegations require examination but create no privileged claim category; none is found true. A prospective noncriminal-certiorari fee-and-form condition independently requires demonstrated applicant-specific abuse, fit to that category, and support for the inadequacy of individual-paper treatment. Keeping paid and criminal channels open limits a condition's reach but does not justify it.

Martin, Demos, In re Vey, Vey v. Clinton, Zatko, Day, Sassower, and the applicant-specific cases retain their distinct scopes. McDonald and Sindram's extraordinary-writ authority does not automatically justify controls over another category. No numerical threshold, universal warning requirement, exhaustion of every conceivable alternative, new presumption, or categorical prohibition on supported restrictions is created. Financial leave permits processing only: no certiorari ruling, access violation, entitlement to briefing or counsel, reversal of the Sixth Circuit, or relief from lower-court default follows. No payment deadline, refiling obligation, or future restriction is imposed.

### November 4, 1997 — Bates v. United States

[Public Projection](../records/bates_v_united_states_merits_1997-11-04.md#public-projection). Ginsburg's unanimous Court opinion holds that the version of 20 U.S.C. §1097(a) governing the alleged 1990 conduct requires knowing and willful culpable misapplication, without an additional purpose to injure or defraud. An unpaid refund, accounting mistake, insolvency, or breached funding condition is insufficient by itself. The indictment's deliberate-diversion allegations remain unproved; affirmance allows the reinstated twelve-count prosecution to proceed.

Wells governs fidelity to actual enacted elements; Ratzlaf's contextual willfulness and independently sufficient residual-lenity ground, and Staples, Morissette, and Liparota's protection of offense-specific fault remain intact. Text, context, and the distinct bank-misapplication history leave no residual ambiguity requiring the proposed additional purpose here. The complete meaning of willfulness, including knowledge of illegality, every conversion theory, aiding-and-abetting questions, and independent indictment objections are not decided. No universal rule about statutory silence or mental state is adopted; the later refund-specific amendment is not applied retroactively. Guilt, sufficiency, instructions, and sentence remain outside this pretrial construction ruling.

### November 4, 1997 — State Oil Co. v. Khan

[Public Projection](../records/state_oil_co_v_khan_merits_1997-11-04.md#public-projection). Scalia's unanimous Court opinion replaces Albrecht's per se condemnation of vertical maximum resale-price agreements with rule-of-reason review, effective this date. Albrecht is overruled only to that extent. Per se condemnation requires a sound basis for treating the category as predictably harmful; a ceiling may constrain dealer markups and improve interbrand competition as well as produce harms requiring proof. State Oil's rebate obligation is an actual price agreement, not a unilateral suggestion.

Sylvania and Business Electronics inform the vertical-restraint distinction; Atlantic Richfield and Brunswick preserve independent antitrust injury; Kodak and Brooke Group retain their distinct proof and competitive-harm rules. Minimum resale-price and horizontal price-fixing rules remain unchanged. Harmful service suppression, exclusion, exploitation of dependence, disguised minimum pricing, and proved horizontal arrangements retain their governing rules. Dealer lost margin alone is not antitrust injury. The Court decides no market power, actual harm, damages, expert-evidence outcome, or ultimate liability, and creates no general stare decisis checklist from Hubbard's divided rationales. Lower courts properly followed Albrecht before this overruling. The antitrust component is vacated for rule-of-reason proceedings; the district court's antitrust summary judgment is not automatically restored, all evidence or injury issues are not automatically reopened, and the separate contract dismissal stands.

### November 17, 1997 — City of Monroe v. United States

[Public Projection](../records/city_of_monroe_v_united_states_summary_merits_1997-11-17.md#public-projection). Per curiam Part I, joined by all nine Justices, permits an enforcement court to determine the actual scope and applicability of Attorney General preclearance while forbidding substantive preclearance or reconsideration of approval's wisdom; actual changes must be fairly identified. Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, and Ginsburg, holds that the specifically submitted and approved 1968 statewide majority default independently governs Monroe's silent charter. The unprecleared 1966 local amendment does not negate that independent authority. The conflicting plurality injunction is reversed to that limited extent.

City of Rome is distinguished because its charter expressly required plurality; its rejection of implied clearance for local amendments remains binding. McCain and Clark preserve specific submission and the submitting authority's responsibility for ambiguity. Lopez, Young, and Foreman reject clearance by association, long use, or state compulsion. No exemption for state-compelled administration, blanket approval of local implementation, or clearance of an approved delegation's distinct local choices follows. An ambiguous submission would not establish the same approval. The 1966 amendment and other charter revisions remain unapproved; genuinely distinct changes require ordinary coverage and preclearance treatment. Bossier's substantive rules and independent §2 discrimination claims remain separate. No damages, completed-election consequences, replacement-system design, automatic new approval request, or automatic evidentiary hearing is decided.

### November 17, 1997 — Jeffries v. Harleston

[Public Projection](../records/jeffries_v_harleston_merits_1997-11-17.md#public-projection). Souter's Parts I and III command five direct votes (Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer); Part II commands those five plus Stone-Zsela. Six Justices support vacatur; the controlling five-vote mandate does not require a new trial.

Part I rejects final defense judgment on the panel's employment balance. Pickering and Connick require attention to the actual speech, service relationship, and institutional needs. Retained tenure and lack of entitlement to a longer chair term do not categorically defeat retaliation; a reasonable forecast matters but does not alone decide protection. The public-concern address was off campus at an unaffiliated event and was not assigned or controlled as official duty; introduction by the chair title did not establish sponsorship. Real administrative duties and recruitment, fundraising, faculty-interaction, and community interests remain relevant, but no special policymaking, confidential, or additional public-contact role was established. Both jury findings—no actual disruption and reasonable expected disruption—stand. Prevention need not await actual harm. Waters protects a genuinely relied-upon reasonable account only if it describes legally disciplinable speech; no complete investigation code is adopted. Perry, Umbehr, and O'Hare preserve the distinction between benefit entitlement and retaliation. No categorical protection of the entire address, actual-loss requirement, quantified institutional-damages requirement, or wholesale NTEU prospective-ban burden follows; Settle remains confined to its student-curriculum setting.

Part II requires each defendant's actual causal responsibility and a real counterfactual under Mt. Healthy, Umbehr, O'Hare, Kimberlin, and Crawford-El. Once protected speech is a substantial or motivating cause, the same-decision defense requires proof by a preponderance, not a conceivable lawful reason. Nine successful one-year votes and differentiated animus or punitive findings do not automatically establish an independent lawful Board decision. A causally effective proposal may matter, but procurement alone proves no liability; nonvoting proposals, defeated votes, and abstention neither establish nor eliminate other supported causal contributions. Competent direct and circumstantial proof applies without a heightened motive burden. A genuine independent lawful decision or supported personal defense may still dispose of a claim; Bernstein's discontinued claims stay discontinued.

Part III leaves actual verdict harmonization and consequences, sustainable individual defenses, capacity attribution, and relief to ordinary adjudication. Harlow and Anderson v. Creighton require personal-damages immunity at the late-October 1991 conduct date and, for any preserved March 1992 claim, its distinct date; later law supplies no retrospective notice. Punitive answers do not replace protection, causation, or immunity. Prospective relief requires a live remedy; elapsed time alone proves neither mootness nor continuing eligibility. Vacatur reinstates neither the two-year chair injunction nor punitive awards and establishes no final liability, immunity, damages, chair entitlement, new March 1992 claim, or universal retrial requirement. Each controlling part has a direct majority; no combined plurality or Marks rule is necessary.

### November 24, 1997 — United States v. Rybar

[Public Projection](../records/united_states_v_rybar_merits_1997-11-24.md#public-projection). Kennedy's Parts I–II command all nine votes; Part III commands eight, with Stone-Zsela dissenting from remand. Part I applies Hale's anti-evasion/material-impairment rule to uphold §922(o)'s possession component as a necessary part of Congress's direct prohibition on additions to the post-1986 civilian machinegun market; Rybar adds no new Commerce doctrine and does not enlarge Hale. The two completed submachine guns and consecutive gun-show sales make the market relation concrete and place these possessions at the core of that mechanism, although the convictions are for possession. There is no transaction-specific interstate-crossing element. Under Lopez's controlling sequence the regulated class and interstate mechanism come first; marketability, remote interstate history, generalized gun violence, or presumed foreign or military provenance alone is not the ground. No universal legislative-findings requirement or general police power follows. Government channels and lawful pre-May 19, 1986 possession retain their statutory scope; manufacture date alone proves no exception.

Part II permits personal invocation of the Second Amendment without militia enrollment or service. Military usefulness neither excludes an arm nor establishes immunity; commerce does not categorically erase the right, and necessary acquisition connects to protected keeping. Love, Hale, and Miller require separate attention to the actual weapons, conduct, and restriction. Commerce authority and registration compliance do not resolve that inquiry; Staples supplies statutory culpability, not a constitutional weapon classification.

Part III vacates the affirmance of Counts I and III because the militia prerequisite was material and the lower court's additional discussion was not an adequate independent weapon-and-restriction application. The known automatic characteristics strengthen the regulation argument but do not alone resolve the possession restriction. Hale's registration holding does not validate this prohibition. Existing evidence or genuinely necessary ordinary proceedings may supply the application; no empirical study, automatic hearing, new scrutiny tier, comprehensive catalogue, categorical automatic-weapons exclusion, general danger exception, or universal remand rule is required. Ultimate constitutional validity remains open. The Court orders no acquittal, release, weapon return, trial, or resentencing. Dismissed NFA transfer Counts II and IV stay outside the judgment; receivers, conversion parts, accessories, ordinary semiautomatic arms, and every other §922(o) application are not resolved.

### November 26, 1997 — §3626 statutory amendments

[Pub. L. 105-119, §123](../records/plra_section_123_amendments_admitted_source_1997-11-26.md#public-projection). Section 123(a)(2) changes §3626(b)(3) from “current or ongoing” to “current and ongoing”; subsection (b) makes the amendments effective on enactment and applicable to pending cases. The enactment also changes identified release-order and automatic-stay provisions; its exact terms control. The June 23 Plyler decision retains its original statutory premise and precise holding. No pending-case violation, stay, deadline, constitutional ruling, or outcome is supplied by admission. The six completed chunk-1 events on or after enactment do not apply prospective prison-conditions relief, so none changes. Rouse applies the amended current-and-ongoing condition on January 15, 1998, within the separate holding and limits below.

### December 1, 1997 — United States v. Parker

[Public Projection](../records/united_states_v_parker_merits_1997-12-01.md#public-projection). O'Connor's unanimous opinion applies Mussari/Schroeder to the original §228: Congress may punish willful nonpayment of a valid, legally fixed support obligation between a Florida obligor and children in Pennsylvania. Different-state residence is essential; nonpayment longer than one year or greater than $5,000 is an alternative threshold, not a substitute for the interstate relation. No completed payment, travel, flight, wire, voluntary crossing, or evasion motive is added. The required cross-border payment, rather than attenuated poverty or welfare costs, supplies the commerce ground under Lopez.

The law regulates the private obligor and does not commandeer a State. New York and Printz/Mack retain their independent constraints; absence of commandeering alone creates no commerce power. Federal prosecution accepts the valid state order and authorizes no creation or revision of support, custody, parentage, or domestic status, or disregard of a lawful state modification. Proper challenges to the predicate's existence and continuing force remain available. Only willful failures after October 25, 1992 constitute the offense; earlier arrears may establish background or past-due status. Every actual element requires proof beyond reasonable doubt, with lawful ability-related and other defenses preserved. Rybar changes neither the direct-payment rule nor its limits. Affirmance reinstates prosecution only; guilt, sentence, restitution amount, and treatment of older arrears in any later restitution order remain undecided.

### December 2, 1997 — Foster v. Love

[Public Projection](../records/foster_v_love_merits_1997-12-02.md#public-projection). Ginsburg's unanimous opinion holds that Louisiana's conclusive October election of a congressional majority winner conflicts with then-operative 2 U.S.C. §§1 and 7 under Congress's Article I, §4 authority. Legal choice is consummated in October where no selection remains on the prescribed November day; calling the event a primary or one stage of a larger process does not cure that conflict. Section 8's vacancy and failure-to-elect provisions remain distinct; a successful final October majority is not failure to elect.

Voting Rights Coalition supports express federal election-administration authority; Thornton's administration/qualifications distinction and Freightliner's actual-conflict principle remain intact. Relief displaces only premature final congressional selection. Compatible preliminary contests, administration, and the open-ballot form remain available, including an October preliminary contest if operative selection occurs on the federal day. No replacement calendar or deadline, particular primary system, comprehensive rule for every preliminary step or later runoff, state-office regulation, presidential-timing ruling, completed-officeholding disruption, additional legislative-purpose holding, or field-preemption ground is adopted.

### December 2, 1997 — Salinas v. United States

[Public Projection](../records/salinas_v_united_states_merits_1997-12-02.md#public-projection). Thomas's unanimous opinion holds on §666 that a bribe need not demonstrably affect federal funds. It expressly reserves any distinct statutory connection between the bribe and expenditure of federal funds and outer constitutional applications; neither reservation creates a nexus test or burden. Actual agency, corrupt intent, acceptance of value to influence or reward, covered entity business or transactions worth at least $5,000, and qualifying federal benefits exceeding $10,000 in the relevant one-year period remain necessary. Section 666(c)'s bona fide compensation and ordinary-course expense exception remains operative. The $5,000 threshold measures covered business or transactions, not automatically the bribe, watches, or truck. Benefits status is assumed for this review; every federal receipt is not declared a benefit. No precise annual amount, intangible-benefit coverage, or reopened valuation is adjudicated.

On §1962(d), the same opinion requires a knowing agreement to further an undertaking that, if carried out, satisfies the substantive enterprise offense; personal commission or personal agreement to commit two predicates is unnecessary. The unlawful object must encompass substantive elements, including Reves's direction requirement, but each conspirator need not necessarily direct the enterprise personally. Mere association, knowledge, friendship, or employment is insufficient. Wells distinguishes actual statutory predicates from added conditions, Ratzlaf preserves contextual construction and residual lenity, and Shabani informs the agreement inquiry only within its scope; no universal conspiracy code follows. Reves remains the substantive rule and had reserved conspiracy.

The personal-two-act instruction objection fails, but the entire charge, an independent personal-direction instruction, general evidentiary sufficiency, harmlessness, general aiding-and-abetting liability, and any general overt-act rule are not decided. The substantive §1962(c) acquittal remains intact. Affirmance of the two bribery convictions and conspiracy conviction enlarges no conviction and directs no new trial, special remand, or independent sentence review.

### December 8, 1997 — Richenberg v. Cohen

[Public Projection](../records/richenberg_v_cohen_merits_1997-12-08.md#public-projection). O'Connor's five-Justice opinion, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, and Breyer, applies Thomasson's ordinary-rationality standard, conduct-related likelihood beyond abstract desire, and genuine individualized rebuttal requirement to sustain this discharge. Meaningful rebuttal does not require acceptance of every denial, proof of completed prohibited acts, or individual operational injury. Service and character evidence must be actually considered but does not automatically defeat the inference. Two hearings considered that evidence and Richenberg's denial; equivocal answers about future conduct permit the existing conduct-related assessment. Discounting general character testimony may reflect its probative reach rather than refusal to consider it.

Attraction alone remains insufficient and ordinary attraction is not a statutory act. Sparse reasons or a competing inference alone establish neither a constitutional violation nor a general requirement of additional findings. The supported reading requires no invented demeanor finding or additional agency reason; hypothetical appellate reasons cannot replace actual reasons. Section 654(b)(2) permits rebuttal by showing no engagement, attempt, propensity, or intent concerning homosexual acts. Section 654(e)'s permission to omit processing requires avoidance-or-termination intent and military-best-interest findings, neither established here, and creates no retention command.

Walmer's preliminary admitted-conduct case does not decide this statement challenge. Romer preserves ordinary rationality, reasonable prediction, and conceivable legitimate grounds tied to the actual disadvantage; Weiss gives military judgment substantial weight while retaining independent constitutional review. Jeffries's actual-ground requirement survives, but its civilian balance and retaliation same-decision rule do not replace Thomasson. No heightened scrutiny, actual-purpose burden, attraction-alone rule, or universal administrative checklist is adopted. Independent APA reviewability and relief, speech, due process, directive validity, substantive liberty, and other ungranted claims remain unresolved. The discharge stands; no new hearing, reinstatement, or compensation is ordered.

### December 9, 1997 — Jefferson v. City of Tarrant

[Public Projection](../records/jefferson_v_city_of_tarrant_jurisdictional_dismissal_1997-12-09.md#public-projection). Breyer's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg, dismisses for lack of a final state judgment under §1257(a). Cox permits bounded review of a finally resolved federal issue where proceedings are practically concluded; the issue necessarily survives every further outcome; effective later review is unavailable whatever the outcome; or reversal would preclude the relevant litigation and postponement seriously threaten federal policy. State certification and importance alone create no finality.

No route applies here. Unlike Richards, the petitioners' claims and disputed liability and causation remain for substantive adjudication; unlike Sullivan, the damages question need not survive every outcome; unlike Labron/Kilgore, effective later correction is available after an appropriate adverse final judgment; unlike Casarotto, reversal would permit damages litigation rather than protect against litigation itself. Ritchie, including note 7, does not require absolute impossibility of later appeal: a possible route did not make renewed presentation useful in its unusual setting, involving disclosure injury, prosecution outcomes, completed trial and appellate review, and an inadequate contempt alternative. Confidentiality alone was not decisive, these considerations are no universal checklist, and Ritchie creates no freestanding exception for every state-law-of-the-case issue. Here remaining adjudication has substantive purpose, later review can effectively correct the restriction, and no comparable destruction of a federal interest is established.

A late jurisdictional objection cannot enlarge statutory authority. The interlocutory state ruling remains in its existing posture; the federal merits are not affirmed. Sections 1983 and 1988, the Alabama damages restriction, discriminatory conduct, causation, municipal liability, survivorship, and damages remain unadjudicated. Robertson's borrowing principles and reserved death-caused-by-violation setting and Newport's municipal punitive-damages rule receive no alteration or new application. No vacatur, trial, merits remand, award, guaranteed later review, or precedent overruling follows.

### December 9, 1997 — Trest v. Cain

[Public Projection](../records/trest_v_cain_merits_1997-12-09.md#public-projection). Stevens's unanimous opinion holds that an appellate court has no mandatory duty to raise an omitted state procedural-default defense. Default is not a defect in federal subject-matter jurisdiction. The Court leaves separate whether judicial initiative is permitted, when it is proper, and whether it would be permissible here; nonjurisdictional classification alone does not resolve those questions.

Lambrix keeps default and nonretroactivity distinct and rejects inflexible sequencing; its remand involved a preserved defense, not a command to discover an omitted one. Granberry's exhaustion analogy shows that comity and federalism do not alone compel automatic appellate dismissal; exhaustion and default remain distinct. The Fifth Circuit treated predicted Mississippi untimeliness under an undiscussed Sones ground as compelling exclusion. Affirmance on discretion it did not express would replace its reasoning and assume undeveloped predicates. Finality interests may inform permissible discretion but do not create a mandatory duty.

Coleman's adequate-independent-ground requirements, supported anticipated default, and applicable cause/prejudice or miscarriage-of-justice exceptions remain intact; its stay disposition supplies no new default finding or jurisdictional classification. Schlup's innocence gateway remains distinct. Neither state silence nor this record proves intentional waiver, automatic forfeiture, actual valid default, cause and prejudice, or an innocence gateway. Gray supplies no universal forfeiture rule. Lindh excludes the covered chapter 153 amendments from this September 30, 1991 noncapital application while preserving separately directed provisions; amended §2254(b)(3)'s exhaustion-waiver rule supplies no default rule here.

The constitutional validity of the predicate Mississippi pleas and the route for challenging expired convictions remain reserved. Lawful later consideration must address the actual ground with an appropriate opportunity to respond, but no universal constitutional right to extra briefing on every unargued issue is announced. Vacatur grants no writ, invalidates no conviction, reduces no sentence, and orders no automatic hearing. Possible lawful default consideration, actual default and exceptions, remaining habeas issues, and ultimate entitlement remain separate inquiries; there is no categorical ban on judicial initiative.

### December 10, 1997 — Hudson v. United States

[Public Projection](../records/hudson_v_united_states_merits_1997-12-10.md#public-projection). Breyer's Parts I and III command seven votes: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Part II commands those six Associate Justices; Stone-Zsela does not join its independent Halper analysis. All nine support affirmance permitting the prosecution to proceed.

Part I applies Ward's civil-intent inquiry and requires the clearest proof to overcome civil classification through the sanctions' supported purpose and actual combined operation. Civil wording cannot immunize actual punishment; deterrence, severity, occupational burden, or criminal overlap is not individually conclusive. Ursery preserves the demanding override and Halper's distinct personal-penalty field; Hendricks requires meaningful operational examination; Mendoza-Martinez supplies indicators without a new exhaustive checklist. The original monetary authorization governs, not increased tiers enacted after the February 1989 notice; good-faith and gravity considerations affect amount without creating a universal willfulness element.

Part II retains and applies Halper's rational remedial relationship, including reasonable approximation. The actual $44,000 assessments bear the requisite relation to the District Court's $72,000 pursuit-cost finding; no additional accounting is required. The initial $200,000 demand is not the final obligation, alleged banking or FDIC losses are not proved government costs, and the agency's failure initially to calculate costs does not establish punishment. Deterrence may coexist with recovery of legitimate pursuit costs. No automatic ratio, below-cost safe harbor, universal compensation ceiling, or statutory-face replacement of Halper follows.

Part III considers the monetary and occupational burdens together. The banking restriction's written-regulatory-authorization exception and protective function in positions of trust distinguish Kurth Ranch's extraordinary arrest-linked contraband tax and Hendricks's prisonlike application; both precedents remain intact. The supported operation supplies no criminal punishment and hence no presented multiple-punishment bar. Materially different schemes or operations remain open. No Blockburger or retained Grady identity determination, attachment rule, Excessive Fines ruling, due-process ruling, settlement waiver, or guilt finding follows; Dixon, Felix, Austin, and the distinct identity and fines rules retain their force. Original administrative instruments, indictment particulars, and underlying cost-hearing materials are not reproduced. The lower opinion's individual-amount discrepancy does not alter its operative $44,000 total, and no additional statutory predicate or waiver is inferred.

### December 15, 1997 — Bay Area Laundry v. Ferbar

[Public Projection](../records/bay_area_laundry_v_ferbar_merits_1997-12-15.md#public-projection). Breyer's Parts I–II are unanimous; Part III has the eight Associate Justices, with Stone-Zsela agreeing only in its first-installment-exclusion judgment and filing no separate writing. Withdrawal creates §1381 liability, but collection accrues only after assessment, a schedule and demand, and an actually missed due installment. Section 1451(f) allows the longer of six years after accrual or three years after the plaintiff acquired or should have acquired actual knowledge of the cause; fraud or concealment permits suit within six years after discovery, without a finding of either here. Knowledge of withdrawal is not knowledge of an accrued collection cause.

Each missed installment has its own period unless a legally effective election accelerates the debt; the accelerated-debt period runs from that exercise, and a later aggregate demand revives no barred installment. Nonpayment default requires failure to cure within sixty days after written notice; an alternative plan-defined default must indicate substantial likelihood of inability to pay. Default, delinquency, and a cure warning do not themselves establish acceleration. Whether the February 1993 complaint's alternative full-debt request accelerated the debt remains open. The lower courts must identify actual due dates and effective acceleration without inventing a schedule.

Section 1399(b)(1)'s as-soon-as-practicable assessment-and-demand duty and proper delay objections survive. Scheduled payments commence no later than sixty days after demand notwithstanding review or appeal; no exhaustion rule postpones them. Part III excludes the first $345.50 claim because of the premise on which the Fund presented the controlling installment conflict for review. Receipt-based and sixty-day arguments do not undo that petition posture. No exact receipt date, universal February 1 maturity, new concession, or universal alternative-argument forfeiture rule is found.

Milwaukee distinguishes computational dates from actual demand, payment, default, and acceleration without previously deciding accrual. North Star's borrowing rule does not replace Congress's enacted period. Concrete Pipe's neutral adjudication and lawful factual and actuarial rebuttal burdens and Inter-Modal's rights/enforcement distinction survive. No assessment validity, equitable tolling, arbitration result, or entitlement to every remedy is established. Remand addresses timely later installments and properly remaining assessment, acceleration, and relief issues; it awards no money or full debt and orders no prospective injunction. Original instruments and pleadings are not reproduced, so the demand-receipt date, pre-suit acceleration, and uncompleted arbitration are not supplied.

### December 15, 1997 — General Electric Co. v. Joiner

[Public Projection](../records/general_electric_co_v_joiner_merits_1997-12-15.md#public-projection). Kennedy's Parts I–II are unanimous. Part III has Kennedy, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer; Stone-Zsela joins reversal but not its PCB-only application, and Stevens would vacate for appellate reconsideration. Reversal and bounded remand carry eight votes; the complete defense summary judgment is not restored.

Part I requires uniform abuse-of-discretion review of scientific expert admissions and exclusions: legal mistakes remain independently correctable, historical findings receive their proper protection, and permissible case-specific evidentiary judgments receive deference. Consequence for summary judgment creates no asymmetric or particularly stringent exclusion standard. Koon and the task-sensitive reasoning of Pierce and Cooter & Gell inform that allocation without transferring their other subject-specific rules; Ornelas's particular constitutional task remains distinct. Rule 56 protects disputed merits facts without removing antecedent admissibility from the judge.

Part II applies original Rules 104(a) and 702 and Daubert's preponderance foundation of reliable scientific knowledge fitting the actual question. The judge may examine the supported inferential connection to the offered conclusion; credentials, terminology, or a claimed weight-of-evidence method alone do not establish it. This screening is distinct from ultimate causation. A matching study or independent proof from each study is unnecessary: animal work, nonsignificant associations, competing causes, and individually inconclusive materials may contribute to a supported cumulative inference. The offered theory is promotion or acceleration, not necessarily sole causation. No later Rule 702 text, categorical significance requirement, or technical-specialty rule is adopted.

Part III sustains this PCB-only exclusion as permissible, without holding admission forbidden or PCB promotion of lung cancer impossible. Infant-mouse injections and adenomas differ from the adult's lower alleged occupational exposure and small-cell carcinoma; the human studies' nonsignificant excesses, unconnected mineral-oil findings, and mixed exposures bear on fit without creating automatic disqualifications. The experts' combined method and additional materials remain acknowledged; incomplete source reproduction does not prove the absence of an inferential bridge. The unchallenged furan/dioxin exposure ruling survives, and the District Court had not expressly assessed causation admissibility on that assumed exposure. Those questions remain for further proceedings.

Daubert's flexibility and distinction between admissibility and ultimate proof survive; Frye's compulsory general-acceptance prerequisite remains displaced, and Rules 403, 703, 706, and 56 retain separate functions. No mandatory expert assistance, new hearing, trial, admission, damages, collateral reopening, or alternative-theory outcome follows. Full studies, depositions, and the entire record are not reproduced; the reported observations and cumulative method supply no independent medical finding, invented data, or litigant default.

### December 15, 1997 — City of Chicago v. International College of Surgeons

[Public Projection](../records/city_of_chicago_v_international_college_of_surgeons_merits_1997-12-15.md#public-projection). Thomas's Part I commands seven votes: Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer. Part II's appellate mandate has those six Associate Justices; Stone-Zsela joins reversal with a separate district-court instruction. Stevens and Ginsburg would affirm the jurisdictional remand.

Substantial federal constitutional claims on these complaints' face supply §1331 jurisdiction and §1441(a) removal. Section 1367(a) reaches their related state constitutional and record-confined administrative-review claims because the property designations, permit denials, procedures, injury, and relief form the same Article III case. Gibbs informs the common-case relation; supplemental claims need no independent original-jurisdiction basis. Illinois's closed-record review and presumption favoring agency facts define the task rather than exclude it categorically from §1367. State review standards remain intact. These local administrative decisions are not state-court judgments; an action containing only a state administrative appeal and a diversity basis for one remain undecided.

Power does not compel retention. Section 1367(c) permits declining where state-law issues are novel or complex, state claims substantially predominate, all original-jurisdiction claims are dismissed, or exceptional circumstances present other compelling reasons. Economy, convenience, fairness, and comity inform discretion without replacing those conditions. Abstention retains independent predicates. Burford requires timely and adequate state review and either difficult state-law questions of transcendent public importance or disruption of coherent state policy on a matter of substantial public concern. Quackenbush limits outright Burford surrender to equitable or otherwise discretionary relief; an independent legal-damages claim may be stayed on a justified showing, but not simply relinquished on that ground. No abstention application is decided.

Part II returns to the Seventh Circuit for properly remaining retention and abstention arguments, without restoring the District Court's entire merits judgment. Peacock's later new-debtor action lacked an original federal anchor; its rule survives. Things Remembered's covered-remand review bar is unchanged because this review concerns an appellate judgment following a district merits judgment. Steel Co. retains jurisdiction-first analysis without adding power. No constitutional validity, taking, hardship, sufficiency, demolition entitlement, compulsory retention, categorical administrative-appeal jurisdiction, §1447(d) exception, or alteration of state standards follows. Lakefront's separate action remains outside the judgment. Original complaints, agency materials, and briefs are not reproduced; an asserted lack of state precedent remains a litigant contention rather than an exhaustive finding.

### December 22, 1997 — Tanford v. Brand

[Public Projection](../records/tanford_v_brand_merits_1997-12-22.md#public-projection). Stone-Zsela's Part I is unanimous; Parts II–III have Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas dissent from reversal. The continuing policy and Urbanski's concrete expectation of his 1999 graduation support prospective review under Lee, without perpetual-student assumptions or ideological objection alone. University selection of a religious leader and incorporation of actual invocations and benedictions in its official ceremony make the exercises governmental worship despite independent wording and an uplifting-message instruction.

Under Lemon as Agostini modified it, the University may not select and present these devotional prayers as official commencement components. Governmental organization of worship falls within the test's specific-prohibition route. Purpose, effects and entanglement are evidence within the single inquiry, not freestanding prongs or an alternative primary-effect test. Lee and Ingebretsen supply the prohibition applied to this previously unresolved university setting. A mature audience, sincere ceremonial purpose, brevity, nonsectarian language and genuine freedom to abstain do not cure it. The Court accepts optional attendance, seated abstention, ordinary movement and no penalty without finding adolescent pressure. No separate coercion, sham-purpose, denominational-exclusion or excessive-entanglement finding is necessary. Marsh establishes no permissible relationship for this university practice, and the resolved prohibition requires no residual attribution-and-function inquiry.

Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas accept the modified Lemon test they joined in Agostini but regard this particular voluntary adult ceremony as a permissible relationship, drawing on Marsh and historical ceremonial accommodation. They accept its official devotional character and dispute the Court's application of the worship prohibition; Kennedy retains his practical-pressure approach, while Scalia and Thomas retain their distinct compulsion account. O'Connor applies the Lemon test as updated in Agostini, which she joined. Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer apply the governing test's preserved official-worship prohibition despite their Agostini dissents. The holding remains confined to this official university prayer practice.

Marsh remains distinct legislative-prayer law, not a universal adult-ceremony exemption; Sherman distinguishes civic acknowledgment from actual prayer. Engel and Schempp retain their official-worship protection despite nonsectarian wording and freedom to abstain. Pinette, Rosenberger, Good News, and Mergens preserve genuine private expression and neutral access, which do not authorize governmental worship. Long history and solemnity receive weight without establishing permission for this devotional program.

Ingebretsen's official/private boundary governs whatever prospective relief the lower courts find warranted against the established official prayers, subject to the plaintiffs' continuing standing and the requirements for an injunction. Independent private prayer, lawful graduate remarks, genuine neutral forums, nondevotional remarks, historical acknowledgment, and moments of silence remain distinct, without deciding that unpresented arrangements are permissible. No new forum, new ceremony design, religious-viewpoint suppression, damages, fees ruling, automatic hearing, or general prohibition on religion at universities follows. The complete lower opinion and prayer footnote support the practice and expected exposure; differing references to the May 1995 ceremony date do not settle an exact date. The full complaint, depositions, and district entry are not reproduced, and no detailed University script, attendance penalty, or later enrollment fact is supplied.

### December 29, 1997 — Clanton v. Cooper

[Public Projection](../records/clanton_v_cooper_merits_1997-12-29.md#public-projection). Ginsburg's Part I and Part II-B are unanimous. Part II-A's complete knowing-or-reckless material-warrant-falsity rule has all eight Associate Justices; Stone-Zsela joins its deliberate-falsity application and subsidiary-theory limits. Part III's distinct confession remand has the eight Associate Justices; Stone-Zsela would affirm denial on that branch. Legal immunity rejection for the assumed deliberate warrant and later detention deception, dismissal of factual-appeal portions, and preservation of rejected theories and disposed claims are unanimous; no liability finding follows.

Johnson and Behrens permit interlocutory immunity review of separable legal defenses on properly assumed plaintiff-favorable historical facts, not participation, credibility, or factual sufficiency. Section 1254 does not enlarge the circuit's §1291 authority. Miller makes ultimate constitutional voluntariness distinct from historical facts; Ornelas supplies task-specific allocation rather than a universal mixed-question rule. Calling an entire coercion inquiry factual cannot exclude a real legal defense, and legal labels cannot secure factual review. The unavailable factual portions must be dismissed.

Franks and Malley supplied the pre-August-1993 rule against personally responsible knowing or reckless falsehoods material to probable cause; judicial issuance does not categorically insulate the officer. Culpable falsity, materiality, and responsibility remain necessary: a corrected truthful account may establish probable cause, and later recantation alone does not prove fabrication. The Court does not find affidavit contents or treat Bobby's derivative statement as independent probable cause. Sufficiently reliable hearsay remains usable; filed charges are not universally necessary before a warrant; and the federal grand-jury requirement does not govern state prosecutions. These subsidiary limits supply neither universal accomplice reliability nor federal liability for every state-law defect.

The same material-deception law obviously applies to the assumed personal communication of deliberate homicide and no-bond falsehoods actually causing otherwise unjustified additional unreasonable physical detention. A different custodian and electronic transmission require no identical NCIC precedent. The original arrest and later message's asserted detention extension remain distinct. The $5,000 Oklahoma bond establishes no federal release entitlement; Texas bond-setting authority establishes no inevitable independent detention. Falsehood, culpability, materiality, attributable cause, and an actual unreasonable seizure must be proved. Inaccuracy, bad motive, a state-law breach, or one-to-three days alone is insufficient. The same-lawful-detention and no-causal-role defenses survive, and no universal duration of Fourth Amendment coverage is decided.

Part III requires legal review of the distinct involuntary-third-party-statement theory on assumed historical facts, identifying Clanton's own protected interest, the particular use, legal voluntariness, and clear establishment at the relevant 1993 conduct date. The lower court discussed her own interest and notice; its error was the overbroad factual characterization, not complete omission. A statement involuntary but not knowingly false presents a distinct theory. Michael's personal self-incrimination privilege is not borrowed, but that does not categorically exclude Clanton's independent liberty injury. Albright supplies no controlling synthesis, universal Fourth Amendment displacement, or general state-remedy bar.

Jackson, Blackburn, Spano, Haynes, and Fulminante supply voluntariness background; Frazier makes an evidence lie nonconclusive, and Hutto does not make every promised benefit coercive. Threats, leniency, suggested narrative, and asserted physical evidence require the applicable totality inquiry. LaFrance, Merkt, Chiavola, Bradford, and relevant circuit law must be evaluated for their actual reach; third-party trial-use holdings do not automatically establish a particular August 1993 pretrial-detention damages right. Obvious coverage needs no identical case, but an abstract liberty principle or bad intent cannot establish an unsettled extension. Ultimate coercion, the independent due-process right, notice, and immunity on that branch remain open.

Harlow/Anderson remains the objective, particularized conduct-date defense. Elder requires all relevant pre-existing law; Lanier supplies a current clarity analogy without retroactive notice or merger of civil and criminal elements. No mandatory merits-first sequence precludes a dispositive notice ground. Crawford-El and Jeffries preserve ordinary competent proof, actual elements, personal responsibility, and causation without a heightened motive burden; their retaliation burden allocation is not universal fabrication law. Fletcher's function distinction supplies neither prosecutorial immunity to Cooper nor 1993 notice. The eight-Associate opinion adopts these limits; Stone-Zsela joins their deliberate-deception application and proposes a noncontrolling replacement defense below.

The existing district immunity denial remains subject to lawful claim-specific review. No new contextual defense, burden shift, general fabrication or pretrial due-process right, derivative Fifth Amendment right, automatic discovery, trial, hearing, or damages follows. The lower opinion supplies assumed facts; original affidavit, full transcript, pleadings, depositions, messages, and later state voluntariness ruling are not reproduced. Its release-year discrepancy and one-versus-three-day ambiguity supply no exact date or amount. Michael's plea and later ruling do not bind Clanton absent established preclusion predicates, and missing materials prove no litigant default.

### January 5, 1998 — Valot v. Southeast Local School District Board of Education

[Public Projection](../records/valot_v_southeast_local_school_district_board_of_education_merits_1998-01-05.md#public-projection). Souter's Parts I and IV have Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer; Part II has all eight Associate Justices, with Stone-Zsela agreeing in its judgments; Part III has Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. The count-1 categorical rejection is reversed and corresponding defense summary judgment vacated, 6–3. Count 2's protected administrative invocation, count 3's protected-right penalty branch, and count 5's state-claim nonretention return, 5–4. Rejection of count 3's independent economic equality theory and count 4's substantive due process is affirmed, 9–0. No procedural-due-process or renewal-property claim is revived.

A public employer may not deny continued employment solely because an employee lawfully and non-shamly uses this established governmental unemployment-benefit mechanism and imposes its prescribed benefit cost; public-concern content is unnecessary for this particular invocation. Perry separates employment entitlement from retaliation protection; California Motor Transport protects governmental redress, while Smith v. Arkansas State Highway Employees distinguishes protection from retaliation from an obligation to accept demands. Connick, Pickering, and Rankin retain employee-speech rules, but this external formal proceeding is not merely an informal workplace complaint. Neither every personal request or internal grievance nor a universal success condition is decided.

The Board admitted that applications and receipts caused nonrenewal. Its dollar-for-dollar cost was real but was the lawful expense of the very invocation punished; no separately established disruption or fraud justifies that action. Later reasonable-assurance practices prove neither summer 1993 assurance nor ineligibility. Eligibility contests, rejection of fraudulent or ineligible claims, neutral filing conditions, lawful prospective changes, and independent job-related decisions remain available. The program is not frozen and operational costs are not universally irrelevant.

Part II holds that known benefit applications rationally indicate possible recurring fiscal expense when considered independently of a protected-right penalty. Beach, Heller, and Romer retain their actual ordinary-equality limits; that conceivable fiscal basis establishes neither a valid rights penalty nor a Mt. Healthy same-decision defense. Collins does not convert voluntary employment or state-law expectations into a distinct fundamental renewal right or substantive deprivation. Annual contracts and an anticipated recommendation guaranteed no renewal; plaintiffs' express substantive-only limitation leaves procedural due process outside the case. Albright remains fractured, without a universal specific-source or state-remedy bar.

Part III returns count 2 because successful receipt does not foreclose retaliation for using the process; it creates no freestanding general access entitlement. The rights-dependent equality branch returns on the corrected protection premise without automatic strict scrutiny or independent liability. The Ohio claim's nonretention must be reconsidered because federal claims survive. Section 1367(c)(3) permits rather than compels declining after all original claims are dismissed and does not eliminate supplemental power; Chicago's power/discretion distinction governs. The state merits, compulsory retention, and other supported grounds for nonretention remain open.

Parts I and IV require ordinary proof of actual causation, personal responsibility, municipal attribution, injury, immunity, and relief. The Board's reason establishes neither each individual's participation nor all remedial predicates; the superintendent's favorable recommendation is not an adverse vote. Under Mt. Healthy, Umbehr, O'Hare, and Crawford-El, protected conduct must play a substantial or motivating role, and an actual same-decision defense requires a preponderance, not a conceivable lawful reason. Harlow/Anderson and Elder preserve particularized conduct-date notice and a dispositive defense without mandatory merits-first sequencing. Later San Filippo, Umbehr, O'Hare, Crawford-El, Jeffries, Clanton, and this decision cannot establish notice for the August 16, 1993 refusal; San Filippo is persuasive 1994 circuit authority, not Supreme Court law.

No immediate partial liability judgment, ultimate immunity disposition, reinstatement, backpay, damages, repayment, mandatory hearing, trial, or discovery follows. A denial resting solely on the corrected categorical ground remains subject to remand. Published lower-court recitations support the Board admission and claim limits; original pleadings, agency files, minutes, and briefs are not reproduced and establish no additional participation, policymaking predicate, concession, or default.

### January 12, 1998 — Smith v. Mattox

[Public Projection](../records/smith_v_mattox_merits_1998-01-12.md#public-projection). Scalia's Parts I–II are unanimous. Part III's complete retained-immunity framework has all eight Associate Justices; Stone-Zsela joins its disposition and ordinary-proceedings limits. All nine affirm the denial of qualified-immunity summary judgment on the presented legal question and proper assumed facts, without finding final constitutional liability.

Johnson, Behrens, and Clanton permit a separable legal immunity inquiry on plaintiff-favorable historical assumptions while barring selection among accounts, credibility decisions, or factual-sufficiency review. Section 1254 enlarges no circuit §1291 authority. Unlike Clanton's separately identified factual challenges, no factual-sufficiency component requiring dismissal is established here. The assumed account is accepted solely for this appeal.

Graham requires objectively reasonable arrest force from the officer's contemporaneous perspective, considering crime severity, immediate threat, and active resistance or flight. The earlier bat threat, refusal, flight, and feigned renewed flight justified caution and some restraint; submission neither established completed cuffing nor prohibited every further use of force. On the complete assumed account, however, Smith was wholly unresisting during the maneuver, complained of discomfort from an arm pull, and experienced a grunt and blow followed by multiple fractures requiring surgery. That considerable additional force was obviously outside the remaining latitude for reasonable error under pre-June 11, 1993 law. The whole account, rather than injury alone, supplies obvious unlawfulness; no factually identical handcuffing-injury case is needed. Continued resistance, gun-reaching, or an unassumed safety mistake cannot be inserted into it.

Harlow and Anderson require particularized objective notice, not merely an abstract bodily-security right. Graham supplies the preconduct rule; Elder and Lanier explain relevant-law review and obvious coverage without retrospective notice or transfer of criminal elements. No exhaustive absence-of-authority finding or exclusive factually-similar-case method is adopted. Subjective anger is neither assumed nor necessary; bad purpose, hindsight, or a fracture alone is not decisive. Later Kelly and Post formulations supply no earlier notice.

Harlow/Anderson remains governing immunity law. Mattox may prove a materially different supported account, including continued resistance or necessary force, and obtain lawful fact-dependent reconsideration. Elder imports no new claims or evidence. Crawford-El preserves ordinary competent proof and independent defenses without adding a motive element or retaliation's burden sequence to Graham. Unpleaded entry and probable-cause theories, disposed city and chief claims, unlawful-arrest damages, and a separate Fourteenth Amendment claim remain outside the decision. No final violation, causal finding, supervisory liability, damages, compulsory trial, hearing, discovery plan, special interrogatory, or scheduled return follows. Original pleadings and depositions are not reproduced; no anger, purpose to break the arm, completed cuffing, missing evidence, waiver, or litigant default is inferred.

### January 13, 1998 — Fidelity Financial Services, Inc. v. Fink

[Public Projection](../records/fidelity_financial_services_inc_v_fink_merits_1998-01-13.md#public-projection). Stone-Zsela's opinion is unanimous in full. Under the applicable §547(c)(3)(B), all state-required perfection acts must be completed within twenty days after the debtor receives possession. Section 547(e)(1)(B) looks to protection against a simple-contract creditor's superior judicial lien; state law supplies the interest, protection, and necessary acts, including a government employee's act when required, while federal law fixes the interval for this enabling-loan exception. A longer state relation-back period cannot convert later conditional completion into timely protection. Timely creditor effort or mailing is no universal safe harbor.

Missouri's ordinary creation and priority rules retain their own force, but its thirty-day retrospective-priority period cannot replace Congress's twenty days. Section 546(b)(1)(A)'s inclusion of §§544, 545, and 549 and omission of §547 corroborates this construction; it is neither an independently sufficient holding nor a universal prohibition on relation back. Barnhill's state-interest/federal-timing distinction supports the reasoning while its §547(c) reservation survives. BFP and Rash require fidelity to the particular federal provision without transplanting their foreclosure-value or retained-use valuation rules; Butner and McKenzie preserve state interests within applicable federal bankruptcy law.

Beasley acquired the car and executed the purchase-price note and security interest on August 17, 1994; Fidelity mailed its perfection application September 7, day 21. Even crediting mailing for the argument, it was late. No exact delivery date or earlier perfection act is established, and the unraised question whether mailing alone met Missouri's documents-and-delivery requirements is unnecessary. The governing interval is twenty days, not a later amended period.

The exception's remaining requirements stay distinct: specified new value secured by an interest given at or after a security agreement describing the property, by or for the secured party under that agreement, to enable acquisition and actually used for it. No separate dispute over those predicates is decided; §547(b)'s preference requirements and other exceptions retain their operation. Failure of this defense does not make every enabling loan avoidable, invalidate the underlying loan, reopen adjudicated predicates, or decide unrelated defenses or plan treatment. The existing avoidance judgment stands on the reviewed ground without a mailing remand, hearing, damages, title-transfer order, revaluation, or plan ruling. Original papers and filings are not reproduced; the unverified delivery date creates neither factual default nor a need to resolve an unnecessary issue.

### January 13, 1998 — Baker v. General Motors Corp.

[Public Projection](../records/baker_v_general_motors_corp_merits_1998-01-13.md#public-projection). Stevens's Parts I and III have all nine Justices, with Part III's common conclusions supported through the respective writings' stated reasons. Part II's independently sufficient federal recognition/execution ground has Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Kennedy, joined by O'Connor and Thomas, concurs in Parts I and III and judgment on a distinct Michigan-effect ground. Reversal of mandatory exclusion and remand are unanimous; the six-vote federal rule controls directly without a Marks combination.

Section 1738 applies to the state consent judgment in this diversity forum. Consent, mere modifiability, or disagreement with local evidence policy does not permit withholding lawful rendering-state adjudicative effect; no general public-policy exception or Michigan modification is recognized. Matsushita preserves that effect and constitutional authority to bind represented persons. Richards requires a sufficient relationship to bind strangers' rights: shared interests or opportunity to intervene alone is insufficient, while consent, control, genuine privity, adequate representation, or another sufficient relationship may qualify. Neither decision makes every nonparty consequence invalid preclusion.

Part II distinguishes lawful adjudicative recognition from the requested evidentiary execution. The GM–Elwell decree governed their relations, not the Bakers' wrongful-death claims or their entitlement to invoke ordinary evidence process; it does not itself compel this separate forum to exclude testimony. A personal command capable of affecting others does not supply the additional exclusion duty GM seeks. The decree remains operative within its lawful sphere. This bounded rule excludes neither injunctions from §1738 nor every cross-border personal duty, allows neither modification of Michigan's order nor policy override of valid preclusion, and creates no universal witness-access or nonparty exception. Land decrees, antisuit injunctions, other foreign evidence orders, and Elwell's lawful personal enforcement remain outside the holding.

The Bakers' evidence request is not a modification request on the asserted basis and requires no Michigan proceeding. Celotex retains actual jurisdictional connection, sufficient authority, covered restraint, and lawful relief/review requirements, including the United Mine Workers and Walker qualifications; it supplies no missing authority to bind outsiders or classify their evidence request as disobedience. The injunction's exception concerns the specified then-pending Georgia case. The separate settlement agreement protects court-ordered testimony in other proceedings against an action for breach of the agreement or injunction. These instruments and exceptions remain distinct, without unquoted contractual terms, permission generally to disregard orders, or adjudication of personal enforcement.

The Eighth Circuit must proceed without compulsory exclusion under §1738, preserving actual privilege, trade-secret, work-product, admissibility, protective-order, and supported preclusion questions. The undisputed reported representation that GM made no such objection to a particular question or answer establishes neither universal lack of protection nor waiver of every objection. Other appellate grounds remain; the entire $11.3 million verdict is not automatically restored, and no new trial, blanket admission, relief against Elwell, or injunction dissolution follows. Matsushita's collateral-review reservation remains open. Original instruments, the complete transcript, and all appellate issues are not reproduced; the Michigan authorities' full opinions, omitted qualifications, and complete historical Rule 2.613(B) text and amendments are unavailable within this source projection, supplying no exhaustive exception rule or factual default.

### January 14, 1998 — Rogers v. United States

[Public Projection](../records/rogers_v_united_states_dig_1998-01-14.md#public-projection). Thomas's Part I is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Ginsburg, and Breyer. The Court announces no separate numerical tally. Thomas's Part II affirmative charge construction is joined only by Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer and is not an opinion of the Court; neither competing charge reading becomes a controlling merits ruling.

The charge defined firearm to include a silencer and required knowing possession of the defined firearm, while later wording addressed knowledge that the item was a firearm requiring registration and separately described knowing possession and firearm status. Factual characteristic knowledge and knowledge of registration law are distinct. The requested instruction and later objection survive counsel's earlier acceptance of a knowledge term. Identification of the object is evidence, not automatic intentional waiver of jury determination or an admission of knowing possession of the bag. The supported mismatch supplies the procedural disposition, not a general policy of avoiding difficult constitutional questions.

No omitted-element harmless-error question is decided. The dismissal establishes neither universal instructional correctness, harmlessness of an actual omission, structural treatment of every omission, forfeiture, nor an extension of Staples to every silencer or §5861(i) issue. Staples retains its actual machinegun-scope distinction between weapon-characteristic knowledge and registration-law knowledge; Gaudin preserves jury allocation; Sullivan retains its vitiated-reasonable-doubt rule; Roy retains its qualified actual-verdict analysis. Olano and Johnson keep forfeiture, intentional waiver, and their distinct review predicates separate; Izumi retains question-presentation discipline. None of these merits rules is applied to sustain the convictions, and no admission exception or other substantive rule is adopted.

The Eleventh Circuit's two silencer convictions stand without Supreme Court merits approval; its separate machinegun reversal remains intact. No remand, retrial, acquittal, release, resentencing, or further review follows. Published lower-opinion and reproduced trial passages support the vehicle ruling. The full petition, grant wording, charge, appendix, and briefs are not independently reproduced; the granted question is reported in substance, and count three's charge is comparable rather than verified identical. Conflicting bag-location or color descriptions play no role. Missing originals establish no missing Court filing, waiver, or litigant default.

### January 15, 1998 — Inmates of Suffolk County Jail v. Rouse

[Public Projection](../records/inmates_of_suffolk_county_jail_v_rouse_merits_1998-01-15.md#public-projection). Kennedy's Parts I–III are unanimous. Part IV's assumed independent contract-impairment rationale has all eight Associate Justices; Stone-Zsela agrees only in rejection of that objection and adds no ground. Part V's ordinary affirmance has the eight Associate Justices; Stone-Zsela would affirm as modified and remand for implementation. All nine agree to end unsupported prospective judicial obligations, including the decree's future operation, reject the presented constitutional objections within their stated limits, and refuse retrospective annulment. The Court affirms the First Circuit's existing direction for implementation below, without a new merits remand.

Part I applies §3626(b)(2)'s independent termination route when the State establishes that covered prospective prison-conditions relief was approved without substantive need, narrowness, and least-intrusiveness findings. Actual findings can suffice without ritual statutory language; Rufo's separate significant-change threshold is no additional prerequisite. The November 26, 1997 amendment applies to this pending case: §3626(b)(3) preserves relief only on written, record-based findings that it remains necessary to correct a current **and** ongoing federal-right violation, extends no further than necessary to correct it, is narrowly drawn, and is the least intrusive necessary means. Lewis and Plyler addressed the former current **or** ongoing text; neither holding is retroactively rewritten. Consent decrees and pretrial detention fall within covered prospective relief and prison conditions. Negotiated protections are not automatically independent federal rights, but a necessary corrective remedy may exceed the minimum substantive conduct rule; exceeding that minimum alone does not justify termination.

The reported judicial assessment of existing findings and periodic monitoring reports since June 14, 1994, including assaults, rape, disease, and related conditions, supports termination. It identifies neither qualifying preservation findings nor a supported federal violation, including a violation meeting the broader former condition but excluded by the amended conjunction; the narrower text changes no outcome on this record. This is substantive findings review, not rejection for omitted statutory words. Compliance with the decree and absence of a violation remain distinct; no comprehensive legality or permanent safety finding follows. Speculative deterioration supplies neither a current violation nor an established refusal to consider qualifying evidence. Independent review and a proper opportunity to present relevant evidence remain necessary, but possible usefulness of more information or unreproduced originals alone requires no new hearing.

Part II permits Congress to change conditions for continuing prospective federal relief while courts determine actual violations, findings, and scope. Plyler directly supplies conditional prospective termination; Robertson and Wheeling Bridge distinguish changed law from adjudication under unchanged law, and Klein permits genuine legal change preserving the judicial task. The statute here commands neither false facts nor annulment of accrued damages or completed historical effects. Morgan Stanley and Plaut retain their defined securities-limitations-dismissal reinstatement holdings, supplying neither an absolute finality bar nor unlimited reopening power. Rufo keeps its separate equitable role. An application in which the amended conjunction excludes an actually supported violation protected by the original safeguard is expressly undecided.

Part III rejects perpetual entitlement to unchanged federal enforcement of mutable prospective decree terms under valid later remedial law. The reported motion, opposition, and review support rejection of the presented procedural objection without presuming an unreported hearing or approving denial of a demonstrated necessary opportunity. Plyler preserves accrued rights and completed effects. Relabeling continuing judicial duties as contractual cannot preserve coercive supervision contrary to governing conditions. The decree's historical existence and independent accrued claims are not erased.

Part IV assumes a contract and substantial impairment only for deciding the objection. Atchison's federal-nonparty rationality framework governs: a federal court's approval and legislation benefiting States do not make the United States a contracting party. Institutional responsibility and resource allocation rationally support the restriction on continuing supervision while federal-right protection remains available. No separate contract's existence, survival, breach, enforceability in a forum, or accrued contractual liability is adjudicated or cancelled.

Part III also rejects the presented equality objection under ordinary rational review connecting the actual supervisory disadvantage to legitimate institutional responsibility and resource concerns while preserving enforceable federal rights. Beach permits conceivable legitimate grounds; Heller and Romer retain the actual-relationship requirement and limits on irrational classifications. Nonsuspect status alone is insufficient, with no mandatory subjective-motive finding or universal economic-classification shortcut. Bell v. Wolfish retains pretrial protection from punishment, and Lewis preserves meaningful rather than nominal access. Proper relief against sufficiently supported threatened injury is not categorically barred; an actual later violation remains actionable under governing requirements.

Termination ends the decree's continuing judicial obligations themselves without declaring the original judgment erroneous or erasing completed determinations, payments, and lawful past effects. The separate Rule 60(b) vacatur theory was conditionally set aside upon obtaining termination; that satisfied condition leaves its merits unaddressed, rather than rejected or unconditionally abandoned. No new merits inquiry, hearing, release order, stay, statutory clock, deadline, or automatic return follows. Decree originals, full reports, and original appellate papers are not reproduced; their absence creates no default or future-compliance finding. Pub. L. 105-119, §123(a)(2) and (b), 111 Stat. 2470–2471, supplies the amendment and pending-case reach; its other release-order and stay changes create no unrecorded motion, clock, or order here.

### January 20, 1998 — Lee v. Harcleroad

[Public Projection](../records/lee_v_harcleroad_threshold_1998-01-20.md#public-projection). The following complete public blocks preserve this event's action, proposition-specific authority, published positions, procedure and source limits.

#### Event

Lee v. Harcleroad; Ninth Circuit Nos. 95-35804, 95-35805, 95-35854, 95-35948 and 95-35949. Reasoned threshold decision after plenary review, January 20, 1998. On review of Lee v. Oregon, 107 F.3d 1382, which vacated the District Court's August 3, 1995 equal-protection declaration and permanent injunction and directed dismissal for lack of Article III jurisdiction. Review concerns patient injury, continuing controversy and representation, provider participation, facility association, ripeness and the resulting jurisdictional remedy. No separate Supreme Court docket or exact argument date is supplied.

#### Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

#### Public Action

The judgment is affirmed in part and vacated in part. The compelled-participation claims return for further consideration of injury and ripeness; the other jurisdictional dismissals and vacatur of the District Court's declaration and injunction remain in effect. The Court does not decide assisted-death merits.

#### Judgment & Remedy

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Remedy or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Patient risk of unwanted assisted death | Dismissal affirmed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | — | Without prejudice; no merits ruling |
| Distinct reduction-of-care protection theory | Dismissal affirmed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | — | Statutory immunity construction remains undecided |
| Dutson, patient representation and proposed class | Dismissal remains operative, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | — | No revived personal controversy or class |
