| Compelled participation: injury and ripeness | Dismissal vacated and remanded, 6–3 | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Thomas | Ordinary claim-specific threshold analysis; no finding of standing or ripeness |
| Facility association and physician exclusion | Dismissal affirmed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | — | Remains outside the participation remand |
| Patient-based equal-protection declaration and permanent injunction | Vacatur preserved, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | — | No restoration or replacement injunction |

Stone-Zsela agrees only in the dispositions of the distinct reduction-of-care theory, Dutson/representation/class components, and preservation of vacatur of the declaration and injunction.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer in full | Supports all dispositions | Parts I–V |
| Same opinion, partial joins | Stevens | Scalia, Thomas | Support the affirmances; oppose participation remand | Parts I, II, III-A, IV and V; not III-B |
| Same opinion, partial joins | Stevens | Stone-Zsela | Supports the stated patient and association applications; opposes participation remand | Parts I-A and IV only |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm the entire appellate judgment | Concurs in Parts I, II, III-A, IV and V; dissents from III-B |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone-Zsela | — | Would affirm dismissal without prejudice in full | Joins Parts I-A and IV and the other affirmance dispositions; gives distinct grounds for rejecting participation remand |

#### Holdings

##### Patient exposure and the distinct reduction-of-care theory

**Holding and operative rule:** At summary judgment, a plaintiff seeking prospective relief must support a concrete, actual or imminent personal injury with specific facts by affidavit or other evidence, allowing favorable reasonable inferences. Elsner's progressive illness and prior depression, even with statutory eligibility and the general medical assertions assumed, do not establish sufficiently impending exposure to an unwanted assisted death; likewise, an assumed reduction of medical-care protection does not establish injury without supported personal exposure to changed care or threatened harm from that reduction.

**Authority:** Stevens's Part I-A has all nine Justices. Part I-B, addressing the separate care-protection theory, has all eight Associate Justices; Stone-Zsela agrees in that disposition only.

**Controlling explanation:** Lujan requires evidence connecting the particular plaintiff to the threatened harm at the litigation stage, and Lyons distinguishes an existing personal threat from speculation about future exposure. The seriousness of death does not relax that requirement. Elsner's illness and depression make her concern personal, but do not establish the requests, medical and witness decisions, continuing impairment and ingestion necessary for the injury alleged. This conclusion does not rest on certainty, a numerical probability, a particular document, or a finding that safeguards never fail. The Court assumes rather than finds her six-month qualification and the reported general medical propositions. The separate good-faith-immunity theory fares no better: even assuming a reduction from ordinary reasonable-care protection, no actual changed care or sufficiently impending personal injury from it is established. Lujan permits a procedural protection linked to a threatened concrete interest without certainty that procedure changes the ultimate result; the asserted legal alteration cannot itself supply that missing connection. Section 4.02's retained negligence and misconduct liability is not erased by the assumed construction of §4.01(1). The statutory construction, substantive adequacy of safeguards, and patient-claim traceability and redressability remain undecided. Dismissal is without prejudice.

##### Continuing controversy, representation and the proposed class

**Holding and operative rule:** Dutson's personal prospective controversy does not continue after his death on this record, which establishes no surviving personal claim, substitute or certified class. A provider's relationship to patients and a proposed class never certified cannot supply the represented patients' missing injury or an independently continuing controversy.

**Authority:** Stevens's Part II, joined by all seven other Associate Justices, controls with eight votes; Stone-Zsela agrees in these dispositions only.

**Controlling explanation:** Arizonans requires a live personal controversy throughout adjudication and keeps corrective jurisdiction distinct from constitutional merits power. Dutson died before the District Court's judgment, and the record establishes no surviving basis for his prospective claim. That conclusion does not dispose of another claimant's independently viable controversy. Powers keeps the representative's own stake, close relationship and hindrance to the third party distinct. The doctors' relationship to vulnerable patients cannot create an injury those patients have not shown; nor can an uncertified proposed class replace a missing injured claimant. The Court adopts no categorical prohibition on provider representation, universal class relation-back rule or damages-pleading requirement.

##### Compelled participation: injury and ripeness

**Holding and operative rule:** Absence of an express criminal or statutory penalty does not by itself defeat Article III injury: an actually applicable legal obligation or supported threatened civil enforcement may burden present conduct. Fitness and hardship must both be examined; predominantly legal questions do not alone establish hardship, but the possibility of a later civil-enforcement suit does not categorically require postponing every challenge to a presently burdensome legal duty. The participation claims must be reconsidered under these principles without a present finding of standing or ripeness.

**Authority:** Stevens's Part III-A states the legal boundary with all eight Associate Justices joining. Part III-B's application and remand command six direct votes: Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Stone-Zsela, Scalia and Thomas oppose the remand; no combination of separate rationales is needed for controlling authority.

**Controlling explanation:** Lujan requires concrete personal application, not punishment as a distinct element. Babbitt rejects forcing a properly situated claimant to undergo prosecution, but its applicable-prohibition setting does not establish injury under these conditional civil provisions. Abbott Laboratories requires both fitness and hardship, and Toilet Goods rests on its actual enforcement and review setting. The Court of Appeals assumed the claimed duties, made absence of penalties decisive, regarded imminence as probably sufficient if injury were cognizable, and carried the same premise into its ripeness ruling. Its acknowledgment of possible civil enforcement did not resolve a present compelled-conduct burden. The strongest basis for affirmance is the absence of a reported current request or enforcement threat. Yet the appellate court's assumed-duty analysis does not establish a sufficient independent determination of concrete application under the correct rule. Further consideration must distinguish §4.01(4)'s express freedom to refuse participation from its transfer-upon-request obligation when care transfers, §3.01(2)'s attending-physician context, and §2.02(4)'s long-term-care witness provision. The lawful record must establish any actual duty and burden, the proper defendants' relationship to it, traceability, redressability, fitness and hardship. The Court finds no duty to prescribe or recommend assistance and no present constitutional or statutory violation. Remand commands neither a new hearing nor additional evidence or filings.

##### Facility association

**Holding and operative rule:** The facilities' challenge to §4.01(2)'s alleged restriction on excluding participating physicians lacks actual or imminent injury where no intending participating physician at the facilities or present operational restriction is supported. That separate dismissal remains operative despite remand of the compelled-participation claims.

**Authority:** Stevens's Part IV has all nine Justices.

**Controlling explanation:** Lujan and Lyons require actual or sufficiently impending personal application. The allegation that a statute could restrict a facility's response to future participation does not establish that any physician there intends to participate or that the facility presently confronts the claimed conflict. An identified conflict or supported current burden would present a different case; general opposition to participation does not establish either. This application neither construes the anti-penalty provision conclusively nor adjudicates association or religious-freedom merits.

##### The District Court's declaration and injunction remain vacated

**Holding and operative rule:** Limited corrective jurisdiction permits clearing a constitutional merits judgment unsupported by a live injured claimant, and the equities here require leaving vacated the District Court's patient-based equal-protection declaration and permanent injunction. Reconsideration of a distinct, previously undecided provider claim does not restore that judgment or establish a new entitlement to relief.

**Authority:** Stevens's Part V has all eight Associate Justices; Stone-Zsela agrees in leaving the judgment and injunction vacated only.

**Controlling explanation:** Arizonans distinguishes jurisdictional correction from merits jurisdiction; Bender and Corrick supply that limited corrective authority. Bancorp makes vacatur equitable rather than automatic, with Munsingwear retaining its judgment-clearing role in the proper setting. Here no established live injured claimant supports the patient merits adjudication, and retaining its constitutional declaration and permanent injunction would give effect to a merits determination without the necessary controversy. Section 2106 does not supply substantive jurisdiction. The possibility that a different direct-provider claim may prove justiciable neither cures the patient defect nor resolves that claim's merits. The Court adopts no rule automatically vacating every judgment whenever jurisdiction is disputed and orders no restored or replacement injunction.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Lujan is applied for specific summary-judgment support, reasonable favorable inferences and the concrete-interest requirement. Its ordinary injury, traceability and redressability elements remain intact; its noncontrolling absent-agency application is not adopted.
- Lyons and Whitmore retain the requirement of supported actual or imminent personal exposure; severity of a possible harm does not replace that connection.
- Powers is applied without combining its own-stake, close-relationship and hindrance requirements or creating a universal bar to provider representation.
- Arizonans is applied for continuing controversy and limited jurisdictional correction. Bender and Corrick retain their corrective role; Bancorp retains equitable vacatur, and Munsingwear its role in clearing judgments in the proper circumstances.
- Abbott Laboratories is applied to both fitness and hardship; Toilet Goods is confined to its actual enforcement and review setting. Babbitt supplies protection against requiring completed prosecution, without establishing that these conditional duties actually apply.
- Vacco and Glucksberg remain decisions permitting their specified prohibitions. Neither requires prohibition, validates Oregon's safeguards or supplies opponents' standing.
- Tanford's actual continuing exposure, Jeffries's live-relief boundary, Chicago's distinction between jurisdiction and merits, and Rouse's supported-threat requirement remain intact; none decides this statute's duties.

#### Law After Decision

A civil obligation may create a concrete present burden without an express statutory penalty, but injury still requires supported personal application. Participation claims require their own injury and fitness/hardship analysis; the Court establishes no presumption of provider standing. The patient, care-protection, representation and association dismissals apply existing requirements to their separate deficiencies. The merits of Measure 16 under equal protection, due process, the First Amendment, the ADA, the Rehabilitation Act and RFRA remain unadjudicated. The statute's substantive duties and immunity scope remain open, as do the proper-defendant, traceability, redressability and ripeness questions on remand. The additional informed-consent-law theory under 42 U.S.C. §1395cc(f)(1)(A)(i) and O.R.S. §677.097 receives no new merits ruling or preserved claim.

#### Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, agrees that absence of an express penalty is not a categorical injury bar, but would affirm the participation dismissal. Assuming an obligation that arises upon a possible future request does not establish that these plaintiffs presently confront it. The reported absence of a concrete trigger, present burden or supported enforcement threat defeats injury, and absence of supported current hardship independently defeats ripeness despite predominantly legal fitness. The appellate court's conditional observation about imminence does not establish present application. Their position does not foreclose a later action supported by concrete jurisdictional facts.

Stone-Zsela would affirm dismissal without prejudice in full. The patient chain depends on future eligibility, requests, independent medical decisions, safeguard failures and ingestion contrary to true wishes. The providers and facilities identify no present request, concrete duty, compliance cost or credible enforcement threat. In his view that unformed chain prevents identification of injury, causation or a ripe dispute, so remand is unwarranted. He reaches no assisted-death merits and preserves a later appropriate challenge by an eligible patient facing imminent application, a provider confronting a concrete statutory duty and credible enforcement, or a facility with an identified participation conflict.

#### Procedure After Action

The case returns to the Court of Appeals for further proceedings confined to the compelled-participation claims. Both their standing and ripeness dispositions must be reconsidered under the correct rules, including separate statutory conditions, concrete burden, proper defendants, traceability and redressability. The other dismissals remain without prejudice, and the District Court's patient-based declaration and permanent injunction remain vacated. No merits judgment, new injunction, compulsory hearing, evidence-production order, additional filing requirement or automatic return to this Court follows. This Supreme Court review is complete.

#### Source Notes

The reported record and Measure 16 text appear in Lee v. Oregon, 107 F.3d 1382 (9th Cir. 1997), decided February 27 and amended March 21 and April 16, with the statutory appendix and notes 1–6. [OpenJurist judicial text](https://openjurist.org/107/f3d/1382/lee-v-state-of-oregon) and the [reporter reproduction](https://static.case.law/f3d/107/html/-202-01.html) support the stated lower-court proceedings. Elsner's six-month eligibility and the general medical assertions were assumed for analysis, not established medical findings. The appellate court did not decide immunity or participation-duty constructions. No new patient request, enforcement event, participating facility physician, substitution, surviving damages claim or certified class is established. January 20, 1998 is the date assigned to this plenary review; no exact argument or submission date is established.

### January 21, 1998 — Buchanan v. Angelone

[Public Projection](../records/buchanan_v_angelone_merits_1998-01-21.md#public-projection). The following complete public blocks preserve this event's action, proposition-specific authority, published positions, procedure and source limits.

#### Event

Buchanan v. Angelone, No. 96-8400. Plenary merits decision, January 21, 1998; argued November 3, 1997. On review of the Fourth Circuit's affirmance of federal habeas denial, 103 F.3d 344. The question concerns refusal of additional general and factor-specific capital mitigation instructions and the distinct statutory-factor due-process contention. Guilt and unrelated habeas claims are outside this review.

#### Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

#### Public Action

The judgment denying relief on the reviewed instruction claim is affirmed. The Court finds no constitutional restriction on effective consideration of mitigation under the complete charge and rejects the separate claimed entitlement arising from the identified statutory factors.

#### Judgment & Remedy

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Remedy |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Constitutional general and factor-specific mitigation instructions | Reviewed habeas denial affirmed, 6–3 | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | No instruction error; no writ, remand or new sentencing |
| Distinct statutory-factor due-process contention | Claimed entitlement rejected, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | — | Denial remains operative on this component; no different state-law entitlement adjudicated |

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O'Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas in full | Supports both dispositions | Parts I–II |
| Same opinion, partial join | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela | Supports constitutional affirmance; agrees only in disposition of statutory-factor claim | Part I only |
| Same opinion, partial joins | O'Connor | Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Support statutory-factor rejection; oppose constitutional affirmance | Part II only |
| Concurrence | Scalia | — | Retains full application join | Constitutional reservation does not displace governing law |
| Concurrence in Part II and dissent | Breyer | Stevens, Ginsburg | Would vacate the constitutional denial and remand | Agrees with limited statutory nonentitlement; rejects Part I's charge application |

#### Holdings

##### Effective consideration of mitigation under this complete charge

**Holding and operative rule:** The Eighth and Fourteenth Amendments require an effective sentencing route for the material independent culpability significance of supported mitigation; a reasonable likelihood that the charge, evidence and trial as a whole prevent that use requires correction. This charge supplies that route, so the Constitution did not require the requested additional general mitigation definition or four factor-specific instructions.

**Authority:** Part I of O'Connor's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas, controls with six direct votes. Scalia joins the application of governing law while separately reserving his disagreement with its constitutional foundation.

**Controlling explanation:** Lockett and Eddings protect meaningful mitigating effect, and Graham and Johnson require a lawful means of expressing independent reduced culpability even when aggravating facts are true. Boyde asks whether the complete charge and trial create a reasonable likelihood of prohibited operation, not whether a restrictive reading is merely possible. This jury was first told to choose death or life. The Commonwealth then had to prove statutory vileness beyond a reasonable doubt. Even after that finding, paragraph three permitted death rather than requiring it and directed life if all the evidence made death unjustified. Failure to prove vileness separately required life; death also required unanimous agreement. The latter directions did not convert the preceding discretionary choice into a mandatory death sentence. Background and emotional-disturbance evidence could therefore support life without a false answer about an aggravating fact.

The contrary reading treats the disjunctive wording and later failure-of-aggravation paragraph as confining life to absence of vileness. Read together with the initial choice and paragraph three's express post-aggravator alternative, they do not create the required reasonable likelihood. Two days of testimony, both sides' explanation of mitigation, the prosecutor's acknowledgment that vileness did not require death, and the verdict form's reference to mitigation confirm the judicially authorized route. Counsel and admitted evidence do not cure an otherwise restrictive instruction. Delo permits an evidentiary predicate for a particular-factor instruction; Tuilaepa permits individualized selection without fixed labels or weights. Neither makes admissibility alone sufficient. The Court finds no instruction error, no actual juror mental state, and no evidentiary predicate for every requested factor. It prescribes no universal wording or favorable weight and does not decide an unpresented mitigation-unanimity challenge.

##### The distinct statutory-factor due-process contention

**Holding and operative rule:** Virginia's identified mitigation factors and provision permitting relevant mitigation evidence do not by themselves create a federal due-process entitlement to each requested instructional formulation. No independent state-law entitlement to specified wording or separately preserved arbitrary refusal to honor such an entitlement is established here.

**Authority:** Part II of O'Connor's opinion, joined by Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer, controls with eight direct votes. Stone-Zsela agrees in rejection of this distinct claim only.

**Controlling explanation:** The statutory allowance of offense circumstances, history, background and other mitigation preserves a broad evidentiary field; its list is not exclusive. That permission does not require the judge to give the requested formulations for prior criminal history, emotional disturbance, impaired capacity and age. The constitutional effective-route inquiry remains separate and is answered in Part I. The Court neither determines every factor's factual support nor decides a different claim that Virginia arbitrarily disregarded an established instruction entitlement.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Lockett and Eddings are applied to preserve meaningful mitigating effect without guaranteed favorable weight.
- Graham and Johnson remain controlling: admission and partial future-danger relevance do not cure a mechanism that prevents independent culpability mitigation. Their restrictive factual special issues differ from this charge's lawful post-aggravator life choice; their holdings and remedies are not narrowed.
- Boyde supplies whole-charge and trial-context review for a reasonable likelihood of forbidden operation, not a mere possibility.
- Delo retains its evidentiary predicate for a particular-factor instruction. The Court does not determine whether each requested factor was proved or rely on an unjoined innocence-instruction rationale.
- Tuilaepa permits relevant individualized selection without fixed labels or weights while preserving effective mitigation and independent selection errors; its no-error rulings are not harmlessness decisions.
- Brecht, O'Neal and Roy keep their defined collateral-error categories. Lindh retains its provision-specific noncapital application holding. No broader error-correction or capital temporal rule is adopted.
- Trest supplies no mandatory default inquiry, and Rogers supplies no instruction-merits, waiver or harmlessness holding. Lee's separate threshold correction creates no general remand requirement.

#### Law After Decision

An additional mitigation definition or list is not constitutionally required where the complete charge already permits the jury to give supported mitigation independent effect in choosing life despite proved aggravation. This is a contextual application, not approval of every minimal charge or a rule that admitted evidence and argument always suffice. No favorable weight, prescribed words or universally required factor list follows. The Court decides neither a separate unanimity challenge nor the evidentiary support for every requested factor. The statutory-factor contention supplies no independent federal right to the requested formulations on this record.

Because no constitutional error is found, AEDPA's application to this capital petition, any new Teague bar, the appropriate correction standard for an actual mitigation error and ultimate writ conditions remain undecided. The federal filing date is not established. No waiver is inferred from agreement to the pattern instruction, and the unrelated second-degree-instruction exhaustion ruling does not bar the supplemental mitigation request.

#### Separate Writings

Breyer, joined by Stevens and Ginsburg, agrees with Part II's narrow statutory conclusion but finds a reasonable likelihood that the charge prevented independent mitigating use. Its disjunctive wording, followed by the direction to impose life if vileness was not proved, could lead ordinary jurors to understand life as dependent on failure of aggravation. The court supplied no explanation clarifying how background and emotional disturbance could independently justify life. Lockett, Penry, Graham and Johnson require that route; Boyde requires assessing the charge as jurors would understand it. Taylor and Carter reinforce that advocacy does not substitute for necessary judicial guidance. Two days of evidence and the verdict form's mitigation reference do not establish a cure. They would vacate only the reviewed instruction denial and remand under the error determination, preserving applicable collateral restrictions and any necessary correction or harmlessness inquiry. They require neither every requested factor instruction nor an unconditional writ, guilt vacatur or immediate resentencing.

Scalia joins the Court's application of governing law but repeats his constitutional objection to the mitigation doctrine. In his view, Lockett and Eddings' demand for discretionary mitigating consideration is difficult to reconcile with Furman's restraint on arbitrary sentencing discretion, and the eligibility/selection distinction does not answer that tension. His reservation changes neither his no-error join nor the Court's controlling rule.

#### Procedure After Action

The Fourth Circuit's denial of habeas relief remains operative on the reviewed instruction questions. This Supreme Court review is complete. No writ, new penalty hearing, remand, immediate release, alteration of the guilt judgments, execution order or execution date follows. Unrelated habeas claims are not newly adjudicated.

#### Source Notes

The reported trial charge, supplemental requests, sentencing context and verdict appear in 522 U.S. 271–274 and the complete charge reproduction at 280–281, from [the official report](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522269/usrep522269.pdf). The lower judgment and separate habeas claims appear in [103 F.3d 344](https://openjurist.org/103/f3d/344). The five-paragraph charge includes the final requirement of unanimity for death. The general mitigation direction and the four requested factors remain distinct. No exact federal habeas filing date or additional granted question is established; no omission in those particulars supplies a default or waiver.

### January 21, 1998 — LaChance v. Erickson / LaChance v. McManus

[Public Projection](../records/lachance_v_erickson_mcmanus_merits_1998-01-21.md#public-projection). The following complete public blocks preserve this event's action, proposition-specific authority, published positions, procedure and source limits.

#### Event

LaChance v. Erickson, No. 96-1395, with the Rule 12.4 McManus companion. Plenary merits decision, January 21, 1998; argued December 2, 1997. On review of King v. Erickson, 89 F.3d 1575, covering Walsh, Erickson, Kye, Barrett and Roberts, and the separate judgment reported at 92 F.3d 1208 concerning McManus. The questions concern constitutional and Civil Service Reform Act immunity for knowingly false factual denials, and the dependent charge, penalty, impeachment and reporting dispositions.

#### Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

#### Public Action

Both appellate judgments are reversed insofar as they adopt categorical denial immunity and its dependent charge, penalty and impeachment bars. The Barrett/Roberts reporting disposition is set aside only to the extent it depends on that erroneous categorical premise. The matters return for lawful case-specific proceedings; the Court selects no employee's final sanction.

#### Judgment & Remedy

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Remedy or limit |
|---|---|---|---|
| Constitutional denial immunity in both judgments | Reversed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Correct the rule; preserve meaningful response and actual proof |
| Independent CSRA immunity claim | Rejected, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Retain the statutory safeguards; no individual element or sanction decided |
| Dependent penalty bar | Reversed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Otherwise lawful consideration permitted, not compulsory enhancement |
| Distinct impeachment bar, including McManus | Reversed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supported relevant falsity may receive lawful consideration; no automatic inference |
| Barrett/Roberts reporting disposition | Dependent disposition vacated and remanded, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Actual duty and defenses remain; unreviewed false-time findings stay intact |

Stone-Zsela agrees only in the dispositions of the independent statutory claim, distinct impeachment bar and reporting branch, while joining the specified constitutional, proof, sanction and valid-privilege propositions below. Both matters return for case-specific review of charges and sanctions, without automatic restoration of any original adverse action.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer in full | Supports all dispositions | Parts I–IV |
| Same opinion, partial joins | Souter | Stone-Zsela | Supports every disposition and the common case-specific remand | Part I in full; Part III's penalty-consideration proposition and actual-proof/lawful-procedure qualifications; Part IV's preservation of separate compelled-incrimination review and prohibition on compulsory waiver of a valid privilege |

#### Holdings

##### Due process protects a meaningful answer, not proved knowing falsity

**Holding and operative rule:** The Fifth Amendment's opportunity to answer an agency misconduct charge does not immunize a knowingly false factual denial from an otherwise lawful employment falsification charge or penalty consideration. An unsuccessful defense is not itself a knowing lie: the agency must establish the actual false statement and requisite culpability under the governing employment rules, while preserving good-faith factual contest, mistakes, uncertain recollection, legal disagreement and procedural nonadmission.

**Authority:** Part I of Souter's opinion has all nine Justices.

**Controlling explanation:** Loudermill requires notice, an explanation of the employer's evidence and a meaningful opportunity to respond, with adequate later process; it does not presume that an inaccurate statement is knowingly false. Bryson rejects a general privilege to meet an official inquiry with deliberate falsehood, while its criminal statutory elements and proof requirements remain distinct. A denial's grammatical form does not make proved deception necessary to meaningful participation. The concern that an innocent employee will be punished twice merely for losing is answered by proof of the actual falsification charge and lawful process, not immunity after deliberate factual deception is established. A separate supported determination may use evidence also relevant to the underlying misconduct; no different-source or corroboration requirement is created. Insistence that the agency prove its case and disagreement about legal characterization are not automatically false assertions of fact. Nor does absence of an oath create the categorical immunity at issue. These are employment proceedings, with no criminal jury, criminal burden or universal materiality element added. Genuine self-incrimination questions remain separate.

##### The Civil Service Reform Act supplies safeguards without denial immunity

**Holding and operative rule:** Sections 7513 and 7701 do not confer categorical immunity for a proved knowingly false factual denial. Their cause, procedure, proof and review safeguards continue to govern every particular charge and sanction; rejection of immunity establishes no employee's falsity, service nexus, regulatory liability or final penalty.

**Authority:** Part II of Souter's opinion is joined by all seven other Associate Justices, providing eight direct votes. Stone-Zsela agrees only in rejection of the statutory immunity claim.

**Controlling explanation:** Section 7513(a) permits covered action only for cause promoting service efficiency. Section 7513(b) ordinarily requires at least thirty days' advance written notice of specific reasons; its exception requires reasonable cause to believe the employee committed a crime punishable by imprisonment, not a finding made here. The employee receives a reasonable period of at least seven days to answer orally and in writing and furnish affidavits and documents, representation, and a written decision with specific reasons at the earliest practicable date. The agency may provide a hearing by regulation under subsection (c); that permission is distinct from Board hearing and representation rights under §7701. Subsections (d) and (e) preserve Board appeal and maintenance of the notice, response, decision, action order and supporting materials, available to the employee or Board upon request.

Ordinary misconduct requires preponderance proof under §7701(c); specified performance and senior-executive matters retain their separate substantial-evidence rule. Otherwise supported action still cannot be sustained if the employee shows harmful procedural error, a prohibited personnel practice or legal invalidity. Section 7703(c)'s record-based review for unlawfulness, procedural deficiency, arbitrariness or abuse, and lack of substantial evidence is appellate review, not the agency's proof burden; designated discrimination facts retain their distinct de novo treatment. Section 7703(d)'s OPM substantial-policy-impact and erroneous-interpretation predicates, reconsideration prerequisite when the Director did not intervene, and discretionary appellate review remain intact. None of these protections requires immunity for deliberate falsehood. The employee retains a meaningful answer and review while the agency bears its actual burden. The Court finds no notice exception, review-prerequisite failure or new disciplinary ground in these cases.

##### Penalty consideration and impeachment remain conditional

**Holding and operative rule:** A proved knowing false denial may receive otherwise lawful consideration in selecting an employment penalty or assessing credibility; the categorical bars resting on denial immunity cannot stand. Such use is permitted, not compelled, and an adverse finding on the underlying misconduct alone establishes neither falsification nor an entitlement to enhance a sanction or impeach the employee.

**Authority:** Part III of Souter's opinion has all eight Associate Justices. Stone-Zsela additionally joins its penalty-consideration proposition and its actual-proof and lawful-procedure qualifications, but agrees only in the disposition removing the distinct impeachment bar.

**Controlling explanation:** The protected chance to contest an allegation does not require an adjudicator to disregard a properly established deliberate deception. Yet relevance, proof of the actual charge, procedural fairness and a lawful penalty basis remain necessary. The statutory decisionmaker must distinguish a knowing factual falsehood from an honestly held denial, ambiguity, memory failure or legal nonadmission. The same safeguards prevent automatic escalation based merely on an employee's unsuccessful defense. The Court therefore removes the categorical penalty and impeachment bars, including the identified McManus impeachment ruling, without selecting an adverse inference or sanction and without displacing unrelated evidence rules.

##### The reporting branch and self-incrimination require their own predicates

**Holding and operative rule:** The Barrett/Roberts reporting disposition is set aside only insofar as it rests on categorical immunity from providing information because it would implicate the employee; further proceedings must address the actual duty and every supported defense. Rejecting a right to lie neither compels waiver of a valid privilege nor decides a genuine compelled-incrimination claim, and it creates no general duty to confess.

**Authority:** Part IV of Souter's opinion has all eight Associate Justices. Stone-Zsela joins only the proposition preserving separate compelled-incrimination review and prohibiting compulsory waiver of a valid privilege; he agrees only in the reporting disposition itself.

**Controlling explanation:** Garrity bars criminal use of answers compelled by a threat of public-employment loss. Gardner forbids discharge solely for refusal to waive protection against criminal use of compelled testimony and its fruits, while distinguishing questions specifically, directly and narrowly concerning official duties without a required waiver. The reproduced 5 C.F.R. §5.4 concerns information required by the designated civil-service bodies or authorized representatives and retains its exception for disclosure prohibited by law or regulation. These actual conditions cannot be replaced with either an unrestricted duty to report or automatic immunity whenever information may be self-implicating. No respondent-specific compulsion, criminal-use threat, refusal, immunity or waiver is established here. The Court selects no silence penalty or adverse inference and establishes no universal prohibition on otherwise lawful civil inferences. Barrett and Roberts's unreviewed false-time determinations remain undisturbed; the dependent reporting and investigatory-falsification issues return for lawful consideration.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Loudermill is applied to preserve meaningful notice and response, adequate later process and the difference between inaccuracy and knowing deception. It supplies no immunity for a proved lie.
- Bryson supplies the bounded principle against deliberate falsehood as a response to an official inquiry; its criminal statutory elements and proof conditions are not transferred to employment discipline.
- Garrity and Gardner remain operative for compelled testimony, criminal-use protection and impermissible waiver demands, with Gardner's official-duty distinction intact. No respondent-specific privilege claim is adjudicated.
- Gaudin remains a criminal element-allocation holding, not a command for an administrative jury or criminal burden. Lanier preserves conduct-time warning in its stated setting; today's decision supplies no missing prior notice for an individual charge.
- Jeffries, Richenberg and Valot retain their actual-conduct, meaningful-rebuttal, lawful-process and bounded-remedy rules. None confers categorical denial immunity or creates a general investigation checklist.
- Hudson retains its distinct civil/criminal classification inquiry and supplies no complete due-process answer here. Lee's supported-duty and threshold-remand rulings establish neither employee falsity nor an individual reporting obligation.

#### Law After Decision

A meaningful employment answer procedure and the CSRA's safeguards do not categorically protect a knowingly false factual denial. The actual charge, its culpability, proper administrative burden, service nexus, lawful procedure and sanction must still be established. Negative phrasing and absence of an oath do not establish immunity; failure to prevail, mistake, uncertainty and procedural nonadmission do not automatically establish falsity. The Court creates neither a criminal false-statement rule nor a duty to waive a valid privilege or confess.

Lawful consideration of proved falsity for penalty or credibility is permitted, with no mandatory enhancement, adverse inference or final sanction. The reporting branch depends on its actual rule and supported defenses. McManus's missing individual factual particulars are not supplied from the other employees, and Barrett/Roberts's unreviewed false-time determinations remain outside the correction.

#### Separate Writings

#### Procedure After Action

Both matters return to the Federal Circuit for further proceedings under the corrected legal rules. Walsh's falsification and dependent penalty questions return without automatic removal or a Court-selected replacement for the reported 90-day suspension. Erickson's corresponding issues return without automatic removal in place of the 15-day suspension. Kye's actual statements, knowledge and penalty require lawful review without converting her suspicion about her son into a proved assertion or automatically replacing the 45-day suspension with removal. Barrett and Roberts's investigatory-falsification and dependent reporting issues return, preserving the unreviewed false-time findings and selecting no replacement for their reprimands or automatic restoration of demotions and 30-day suspensions. McManus's distinct falsification and impeachment rulings return without a newly found underlying event or final sanction.

The reported Board sanctions retain their procedural status while the authorized adjudicators determine the lawful consequences of the corrected rule. The Court orders no blanket reopening, compulsory hearing, additional evidence production, criminal referral, privilege waiver, final knowing-falsity finding or automatic penalty enhancement. This Supreme Court review is complete; no return date or retained jurisdiction is established.

#### Source Notes

The employee records and rule below appear in [King v. Erickson, 89 F.3d 1575](https://openjurist.org/89/f3d/1575). The companion and procedural recitations appear at 522 U.S. 264–265 in [the official report](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522262/usrep522262.pdf). The statutory provisions are the 1994 Code versions of [5 U.S.C. §7513](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-1994-title5/html/USCODE-1994-title5-partIII-subpartF-chap75-subchapII-sec7513.htm), [§7701](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-1994-title5/html/USCODE-1994-title5-partIII-subpartF-chap77-sec7701.htm) and [§7703](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-1994-title5/html/USCODE-1994-title5-partIII-subpartF-chap77-sec7703.htm). The lower opinion reproduces the relevant then-effective 5 C.F.R. §5.4 language. Allegations, AJ findings and Board dispositions remain distinct. No uniform oath status, individual compelled-incrimination predicate, waiver or final Supreme Court finding of knowing falsity is established, and McManus's underlying conduct and final sanction remain unadjudicated.

### January 21, 1998 — Lunding v. New York Tax Appeals Tribunal

[Public Projection](../records/lunding_v_new_york_tax_appeals_tribunal_merits_1998-01-21.md#public-projection). The following complete public blocks preserve this event's action, proposition-specific authority, published positions, procedure and source limits.

#### Event

Lunding v. New York Tax Appeals Tribunal, No. 96-1462. Plenary merits decision, January 21, 1998; argued November 5, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the New York Court of Appeals, 89 N.Y.2d 283, 675 N.E.2d 816 (1996), which sustained N.Y. Tax Law § 631(b)(6). The question is whether denying qualifying alimony deductions in calculating nonresidents' New York-source income, within the complete nonresident tax formula, violates Article IV's Privileges and Immunities Clause. Equal Protection and Commerce Clause claims are outside this review.

Christopher and Barbara Lunding were Connecticut residents. Christopher earned income practicing law in New York and paid alimony to a Connecticut former spouse under a Connecticut decree. They claimed a proportionate deduction using approximately 48% of his business income as attributable to New York. Disallowance produced an additional assessment of $3,724 plus interest.

#### Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

#### Public Action

The judgment of the New York Court of Appeals is reversed, and the case is remanded for nondiscriminatory treatment of the challenged assessment and appropriate relief under the governing state procedures and constitutional requirements.

#### Judgment & Remedy

**Reverse and remand, 7–2.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas and Breyer.
**Opposing:** Kennedy and Ginsburg.

State proceedings must provide lawful allocation and constitutionally adequate relief. If payment was compelled before a meaningful opportunity to contest the tax, adequate backward-looking correction is required. Independently, if the State clearly held out a postpayment remedy and payment occurred, it may not withdraw that remedy afterward, including for voluntary payments. Actual payment, preservation, amount and applicable procedural conditions remain to be established. No fixed refund, interest award or replacement formula is ordered.

#### Opinion Topology

Thomas delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter and Breyer. The same seven Justices adopt both holdings and all their qualifications. Ginsburg dissents, joined in full by Kennedy, on both the income-attribution and independent home-State personal-expense grounds.

#### Holdings

##### Article IV does not permit this inadequately justified categorical exclusion

**Holding and operative rule:** When a State taxes another State's citizens on income earned within its borders, a discriminatory deduction rule must rest on a substantial reason beyond noncitizenship, and the discrimination must bear a close relationship to that reason. Section 631(b)(6)'s categorical exclusion of qualifying alimony from deductions against New York-source income fails that inquiry on the source, personal-expense, initial-computation and family-income justifications offered here; genuine source allocation remains permissible, and identical resident and nonresident tax codes are not required.

**Authority:** The seven-Justice Opinion of the Court supplies direct majority authority for this rule, its application and the qualifications below.

**Controlling explanation:** Toomer protects substantial equality in earning a livelihood across state lines while allowing considerable state latitude. Shaffer permits taxation of local income and corresponding source-related deduction limits; Travis nevertheless rejects unjustified unequal personal allowances. Those decisions require examination of the particular deduction and tax, not ordinary rationality alone or mechanical equality.

New York taxes residents' worldwide income and nonresidents' local income. That real difference does not by itself justify excluding all proportionate recognition of alimony. The payment adjusts income available to the payer and lacks an exclusively Connecticut source merely because the former spouses and divorce decree are there. The lower court's Goodwin analogy involved geographically connected home-State property and medical expenses; it does not resolve this alimony provision. The Court adopts no rule requiring apportionment of every personal expense.

The whole calculation also matters. Qualifying alimony reduces hypothetical resident liability and the federal-adjusted-gross-income denominator, but § 631(b)(6) denies the deduction in the New York-source numerator. The initial benefit therefore does not establish substantial equality in the final burden. The ratio was uncapped; no particular actual ratio or total effective rate is found.

The asserted allocation of taxable family income likewise fails to match the enacted line. Payer residence determines the deduction without conditioning it on whether New York taxes the recipient. Examining that mismatch addresses the classification imposing the Lundings' own assessment, without finding how often different family arrangements occur. Neither a hypothetical recipient-based rule nor its constitutionality is decided.

Austin prevents another State's credit, deduction or fiscal choice from curing established discrimination. Connecticut's absence of earned-income taxation in this year supplies no cure. Fulton and Oregon Waste reinforce scrutiny of actual operation and unsupported offsets only by analogy; their Commerce Clause tests do not govern Article IV. States retain justified source limits and permissible cooperative tax arrangements.

##### Relief follows the established tax-remedy predicates

**Holding and operative rule:** The unconstitutional treatment of the challenged assessment requires reversal and remand for lawful state allocation, severability and constitutionally adequate relief. When the State compelled payment before meaningful review, it must provide adequate backward-looking correction; independently, a clearly held-out postpayment remedy cannot be withdrawn after payment, including voluntary payment, subject to ordinary proof and lawful previously announced conditions.

**Authority:** The same seven Justices join the Opinion of the Court's remedial holding in full.

**Controlling explanation:** McKesson requires meaningful correction when payment preceded an adequate opportunity to challenge; Harper distinguishes application of the governing federal rule from the lawful remedy. Fulton preserves state severability and remedial choices, including lawful refunds, constitutionally permissible nondiscriminatory retrospective equalization, or a lawful combination when backward-looking relief is required. Reich separately prevents a State from withdrawing a clear postpayment route after the taxpayer paid and substituting an unpursued prepayment route. Adequate prospective procedures remain permissible with fair notice.

The reported $3,724 additional assessment plus interest does not establish payment or an entitlement to that sum as a refund. The State must apply the constitutional rule through the procedures and payment facts actually established. The Court neither selects the taxpayers' proposed deduction formula as the exclusive remedy nor automatically restores every aspect of the Appellate Division's order. It orders no interest award, new assessment against others, or reopening of unrelated final tax years.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Toomer is applied for substantial equality, a substantial independent reason and the close relationship required between that reason and the discriminatory treatment.
- Shaffer retains nondiscriminatory source taxation and justified corresponding deduction limits; it supplies no categorical exception for every deduction labeled personal.
- Travis is applied to distinguish legitimate source limits from inadequately justified unequal personal allowances. Its rejection of presumed offsets remains operative.
- Austin is applied to reject a cure dependent on another State's tax choices; permissible cooperative arrangements remain available.
- Goodwin's reported home-State property and medical-expense setting is distinguished; that different deduction setting does not resolve this alimony provision.
- Friedsam's earlier statutory construction was superseded by § 631(b)(6); it is not revived as the constitutional rule or imposed as the exclusive replacement formula.
- Fulton and Oregon Waste supply analogical support concerning actual tax operation and unsupported offset explanations. Their Commerce Clause tests and Fulton's limited treatment of Darnell remain confined to their fields.
- McKesson, Harper, Fulton and Reich are applied to preserve conditional backward-looking correction, lawful state remedial choices and the independent protection of a clearly offered postpayment remedy.

#### Law After Decision

A State's narrower nonresident tax base and the personal character of a deduction do not automatically justify denying it. Courts must examine the challenged distinction in the complete tax scheme, require a substantial independent reason closely related to it, and preserve justified source allocation. Section 631(b)(6)'s categorical alimony exclusion cannot be sustained on the reasons offered here.

This decision adds that Article IV application without requiring identical deductions, strict numerical tax equality, the Commerce Clause's compensatory-tax elements or a particular replacement formula. It decides no Equal Protection or Commerce Clause claim, no dispute about federal alimony qualification, and no hypothetical recipient-based alternative. The existing conditional rules governing unconstitutional-tax relief remain in force.

#### Separate Writings

Ginsburg, joined by Kennedy, would affirm on two independent grounds.

First, the payer deduction and recipient inclusion allocate taxable income between former spouses. When both are New York residents, the State taxes the recipient and permits the payer's deduction; when both are Connecticut residents, denying the deduction preserves taxation of New York-source income that would otherwise pass outside the State's resident tax base. In their view, that source-tax and income-attribution relationship supplies a substantial justification in this case. Article IV requires substantial rather than mechanically precise equality. Imperfect treatment of other, mixed-residence couples does not establish these taxpayers' entitlement to remove the income from New York taxation. This position does not depend on a finding of aggregate interstate-payment balance or on Connecticut's imposing a tax.

Second, even treating alimony as an expense, the Connecticut payer, recipient and decree locate this particular marital obligation in the home State; it is not an expense of producing New York professional income. Shaffer and Travis permit the corresponding source limitation, and Goodwin's home-State expense analysis supports leaving that personal expense to Connecticut's law and policy. Travis's general personal exemption is different from a deduction for a particular nonlocal obligation. They also object that correlation with a payer's income does not distinguish alimony from many other personal expenditures; the Court's disclaimer of a universal deduction rule does not, in their view, resolve that distinction.

Their objection to relying on other former couples is a merits objection, not a vote to dismiss for lack of standing. They accept Austin's rule against curing established discrimination through another State's tax choices but find this allocation justified in the first place. They join neither the invalidity holding nor the correction mandate; their grounds are noncontrolling.

#### Procedure After Action

The case returns to the New York courts for proceedings consistent with the Article IV ruling, lawful allocation and state-law severability, and appropriate relief under applicable preserved procedures. Payment, amount, preservation and the actual remedial predicates must determine any backward-looking correction or monetary relief. No particular deduction formula, refund, interest award, compulsory hearing, retained Supreme Court jurisdiction or automatic further review is directed. This Supreme Court review is complete.

#### Source Notes

The challenged sentence and reported computation appear at 522 U.S. 291–292 & nn.1–2; taxpayer and state-proceeding facts appear at 293–296, with the Connecticut recipient and decree facts at 317 and 326. The state judgments are reported at 218 A.D.2d 268, 639 N.Y.S.2d 519, and 89 N.Y.2d 283, 675 N.E.2d 816. [Official report](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522287/usrep522287.pdf).

The reported record establishes an assessment but does not establish payment, complete return amounts, a particular actual apportionment percentage, or a refund or interest entitlement. The statutory ratio allowed percentages above 100%; that permission is not a finding that these taxpayers exceeded 100%. Connecticut's earned-income fact concerns these taxpayers and this tax year. No empirical finding about interstate household frequency or aggregate revenue parity supports the judgment.

### January 26, 1998 — Allentown Mack Sales & Service, Inc. v. NLRB

[Public Projection](../records/allentown_mack_sales_service_inc_v_nlrb_merits_1998-01-26.md#public-projection). The following complete public blocks preserve this event's action, proposition-specific authority, published positions, procedure and source limits.

#### Event

Allentown Mack Sales & Service, Inc. v. NLRB, No. 96-795. Plenary merits decision, January 26, 1998; argued October 15, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit, 83 F.3d 1483 (1996), which enforced the Board's unfair-labor-practice and bargaining order. The question concerns the evidence an employer must possess before polling employees about an incumbent union's majority support, including the asserted substitution of proof of lost support for the announced reasonable-doubt standard.

Allentown rehired 32 of 45 former unit employees after purchasing the branch. The successor finding and appropriate unit are unchallenged. The ALJ found the procedural polling safeguards satisfied but found insufficient objective doubt. The later secret ballot was 19–13 against representation; that result does not establish what management knew when it announced the poll.

#### Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

#### Public Action

The judgment is reversed, and the case is remanded to the D.C. Circuit with instructions to deny enforcement of the Board order under review. A different majority sustains the facial validity of the Board's good-faith reasonable-doubt threshold for incumbent-union polling.

#### Judgment & Remedy

| Question or judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Facial challenge to the reasonable-doubt polling threshold | Rejected, 5–4 | Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | The announced threshold remains permissible; this is not a partial affirmance of enforcement |
| Substantial-evidence application and enforcement judgment | Reversed, 5–4 | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Remand to the D.C. Circuit with instructions to deny enforcement of the order under review |

The independent, unpreserved attack on the bargaining order's explanation and decertification bar is left unreviewed. Denial of enforcement rests on the preserved application challenge. The mandate permits no second weighing of this unchanged record or retroactive substitution of a new, more demanding threshold against Allentown. It orders no new poll, election, hearing, permanent decertification or determination of present employee preferences.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | All eight other Justices | Common posture and record | Part I |
| Same opinion | Scalia | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Rejects facial challenge | Part II |
| Same opinion | Scalia | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports reversal and denial of enforcement | Parts III–IV: reach, application framework, evidence and mandate |
| Same opinion | Scalia | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Leaves the independent remedy objection unreviewed | Part V; Stone-Zsela agrees only in that nonreview disposition |
| Concurrence in Parts I and III–IV and the judgment, dissent from Part II | Stone-Zsela | O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports denial of enforcement and also would invalidate the threshold | Statutory ground and its preserved limits |
| Concurrence in Parts I–II and V, dissent from Parts III–IV and judgment | Breyer | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Would affirm enforcement | Question-scope objection and independent substantial-evidence defense |

#### Holdings

##### The announced reasonable-doubt polling threshold is permissible

**Holding and operative rule:** The Board may require an employer to possess a good-faith reasonable doubt, supported by objective considerations, of an incumbent union's majority support before conducting a safeguarded poll. Using the same prerequisite for polling, withdrawing recognition and seeking a Board election is not, by itself, irrational or inconsistent with the Act; objective doubt is uncertainty about majority support, not proof that the union has lost it.

**Authority:** Scalia's Part II, joined by Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, supplies five direct votes.

**Controlling explanation:** The Act protects employee choice and established bargaining relationships. Auciello permits explained stability policies within statutory boundaries, while Fall River separates their rationality from factual application. Neither decides this polling rule in advance. Even a safeguarded employer poll may unsettle employees and the bargaining relationship. The Board may account for that risk before permitting a poll.

The employer argues that evidence sufficient to withdraw recognition makes polling pointless. But an employer may reasonably wish to determine actual preferences before taking the more consequential step of withdrawing recognition; a lawful option to withdraw is not an obligation to do so. A poll therefore retains a confirmatory use. Differences between a poll and a Board election—including the statutory one-year bar after a valid election—do not compel the same consequences or a judicially selected lower polling threshold. An unsuccessful union in an employer poll may seek a Board election.

Sections 7 and 8(c) protect employee choice and noncoercive expression, but do not themselves establish an unconditional right to poll an incumbent unit. Gissel left undecided whether its recognized allowances for reasonable inquiry are mandatory. Compliance with Struksnes safeguards does not alone answer the separate incumbent-polling prerequisite. The rule is permissible, not compelled for every future Board, and does not authorize a concealed requirement of actual minority proof. Certification and contract protections during their lawful duration, statutory representation routes and protection against coercion remain distinct. No freestanding First Amendment question is decided.

##### The presented challenge reaches the standard actually applied

**Holding and operative rule:** A challenge expressly directed to the amount of evidence demanded before polling fairly includes whether the Board applied its announced reasonable-doubt standard to the order under review. That inquiry reaches the associated whole-record application here; it creates no general permission to add an independent factual question merely because merits briefs discuss it.

**Authority:** Scalia's Part III, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy and Thomas, supplies five direct votes.

**Controlling explanation:** The petition challenges a requirement of so much evidence of lost support that a poll becomes meaningless. Allentown's contention that the agency substituted individual repudiations for reasonable uncertainty concerns that asserted evidentiary demand, not a separate judgment. Hubbard preserves the difference between supporting arguments and enlarged questions. Examining the actual standard and the material reports is necessary to resolve this challenge. The Court does not reopen the successor finding, the unit or independently unpreserved objections.

##### An announced doubt standard cannot silently become minority proof

**Holding and operative rule:** Under NLRA §§10(e)–(f) and APA §706, the Board's finding must be supported by substantial evidence on the record as a whole under the substantive standard actually governing the case. When the announced predicate is objective reasonable doubt, the agency cannot sustain its order by effectively demanding proof approaching actual loss of majority support, or silently convert recurring evidentiary outcomes into a different substantive or proof rule.

**Authority:** Scalia's Parts III–IV have Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy and Thomas, providing five direct votes for this framework and its application.

**Controlling explanation:** Universal Camera requires relevant evidence a reasonable mind could accept as adequate, considered together with what fairly detracts from the finding. It preserves the Board's choice between fairly conflicting views; it does not permit the governing standard to change during application. Auciello likewise distinguishes circumstantial objective doubt from a numerical showing of actual minority. The agency's §10(c) preponderance burden and the reviewing court's substantial-evidence inquiry remain separate.

An employer's uncertainty may be reasonable without direct repudiations from a counted majority. A course of adverse findings cannot, without a lawful change in rule, redefine doubt as established opposition. Expertise and recurring evidentiary judgments matter, but do not replace the announced legal predicate. Lawful, expressly adopted presumptions or evidentiary rules remain possible, subject to their statutory basis and ordinary review; the Court abolishes no such authority and makes no finding of systematic manipulation. Neither subjective suspicion nor every unverified report necessarily establishes objective doubt. The question remains whether this whole record supports the particular finding under the rule the Board announced.

##### This record requires denial of enforcement

**Holding and operative rule:** The finding that Allentown lacked objective reasonable doubt when it announced the poll is unsupported by substantial evidence on the whole record. The enforcement judgment must therefore be reversed with instructions to deny enforcement of the challenged Board order; the record does not call for another agency weighing under the same or a newly demanding threshold.

**Authority:** Scalia's Part IV has Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy and Thomas, supplying five direct votes for the application and complete mandate.

**Controlling explanation:** The ALJ accepted six individual expressions and assumed a seventh supported doubt. Those seven are neither a numerical threshold nor the entire evidentiary picture. Marsh's dissatisfaction with representation, Bloch's report that the night shift did not want the union, and Mohr's assessment as shop steward and bargaining-committee member add material circumstantial force. Group reports are not mechanically counted as additional, nonoverlapping repudiations.

The ALJ identified weaknesses in the reports' sources, scope, interview setting and relation to the rehired workforce, and expressly found Mohr's statement insufficient even in combination with the other evidence. The Court does not erase that cumulative assessment. But the Board separately said the employer could not legitimately rely on Mohr because he described the old workforce. His union responsibilities and the substantial continuity of employees make that dismissal untenable. His failure to specify the size of a majority or personally renounce the union does not answer whether management reasonably doubted continued support.

Taken together, the material reports establish objective uncertainty even though they do not establish actual minority. The source and coverage limitations do not adequately sustain this contrary finding under the announced doubt standard. This is not substitution of a preferred inference between fairly conflicting views: the required quantum was effectively raised toward proof of lost support. The later 19–13 poll supplies no retroactive justification. The holding determines the legality of this enforcement request, not present preferences, an independent right to cease bargaining in every setting, or a permanent representation result.

##### The separate remedy objection remains outside review

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 10(e) bars an objection not urged before the Board unless extraordinary circumstances excuse the omission. The separate objection to the bargaining order's explanation and decertification bar was not preserved, and no such excuse is established; that objection is not reviewed.

**Authority:** Part V has all eight Associate Justices. Stone-Zsela agrees only in leaving the objection unreviewed.

**Controlling explanation:** Auciello preserves §10(e)'s distinction between the particular objection presented to the Board and another objection later asserted in court. The preserved threshold and application challenges do not themselves preserve this separate remedial attack. Correcting the predicate of the existing order resolves enforcement without deciding the barred objection or approving every possible bargaining remedy.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Auciello's accepted-offer cutoff remains intact. Its statutory limits, distinction between circumstantial doubt and actual minority, and separate §10(e) requirement are applied; it had not resolved incumbent polling or the weight of these reports.
- Fall River supplies separate review of rational Board policy and supported factual application. Its continuity and successor principles are not reopened, and its rebuttable presumption does not become proof of employee preference.
- Universal Camera supplies whole-record substantial-evidence review, including contrary evidence and the examiner's actual findings, while retaining deference between fairly conflicting views. The Court applies that standard to this unsupported finding rather than replacing it with de novo factfinding.
- Gissel retains its protection of noncoercive expression and its attention to the employment setting. Its reservation concerning mandatory permission for reasonable inquiry is preserved; the Court derives no unconditional polling entitlement from it.
- Hubbard's distinction between supporting arguments and new questions is applied to the wording and challenged order here. No exception based merely on merits briefing is adopted.
- Struksnes's procedural safeguards and the additional advance-notice requirement remain operative. Their satisfaction does not eliminate the separately permissible incumbent-polling prerequisite.

#### Law After Decision

The reasonable-doubt prerequisite remains a permissible policy for polling an incumbent unit with a rebuttable presumption of majority support. The decision adds a controlling application of that rule: objective uncertainty may rest on cumulative circumstantial evidence, and an agency must apply its announced predicate without silently raising it toward proof of actual minority. No headcount or replacement polling test is adopted. The Court's direct majorities govern their respective questions; the statutory invalidity proposal and the application dissent are not controlling law.

The existing safeguards remain: a purpose of determining the truth of the union's majority claim, communication of that purpose, assurances against reprisal, secret ballot, absence of unfair labor practices or a coercive atmosphere, and advance notice of time and place. Threats, reprisals, promises of benefit and coercive questioning remain regulable. Lawful certification and contract protections, representation procedures and the distinct withdrawal standard retain their own predicates. A future lawful policy may alter the polling rule within statutory bounds, but may not supply a retroactively higher prerequisite to revive this order. No independent First Amendment holding or determination of current employee support is made.

#### Separate Writings

**Stone-Zsela, joined by O'Connor, Kennedy and Thomas, concurring in Parts I and III–IV and the judgment and dissenting from Part II.** The Act's protection of employee choice does not adequately justify demanding essentially the same stringent showing before a safeguarded inquiry as before recognition withdrawal or a binding Board election. Those acts have different consequences: inquiry can resolve uncertainty, while withdrawal immediately changes bargaining and an election invokes distinct statutory rules. Stability is legitimate, but cannot make the rebuttable presumption practically untestable. Compliance with safeguards, independent secret-ballot supervision and the absence of a finding that this poll itself coerced employees support the statutory objection. The writing would invalidate the existing unitary polling threshold while preserving neutral purpose, notice, anti-reprisal assurances, secrecy, advance notice and protection against coercive unfair labor practices. It leaves lawful conclusive periods, the withdrawal standard and a prospectively adopted, source-grounded polling policy intact, and decides no independent First Amendment question. The authors also fully join the Court's independent substantial-evidence ground and denial-of-enforcement mandate even though the facial standard survives.

**Breyer, joined by Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg, concurring in Parts I–II and V and dissenting from Parts III–IV and the judgment.** The granted rule challenge does not require an independent factual-sufficiency decision. If that issue is reached, Universal Camera requires respecting a supported agency choice between competing inferences. Objective doubt operates in the labor setting, where interviews, ambiguity about dissatisfaction versus opposition, incomplete sources for group reports, departmental coverage and the retained workforce affect evidentiary force. The ALJ gave actual reasons and expressly assessed Mohr together with the other reports; the adopted assessment need not be recast as a hidden majority-count requirement. Mohr's union role and continuity give his account weight, but do not require the conclusion management urges. These Justices accept that the agency must obey its governing legal and proof standards and reject a categorical bar to circumstantial evidence; they disagree that the actual application crossed those limits. They would affirm the enforcement judgment and order no further agency proceeding.

#### Procedure After Action

The case returns to the D.C. Circuit with instructions to deny enforcement of the Board order under review. This Supreme Court review is complete. No renewed agency application, evidentiary hearing, new poll, election, permanent decertification, present-preference finding or retained Supreme Court jurisdiction is ordered. Ordinary future representation proceedings remain subject to their own lawful predicates and safeguards.

#### Source Notes

The D.C. Circuit majority and dissent, 83 F.3d 1483, support the posture, preservation ruling, Board reasons and employee reports. The Board proceeding is reported at 316 N.L.R.B. 1199. The governing text is NLRA §§7, 8(a)(1), 8(a)(5), 8(c), 9 and 10(c), (e)–(f), and APA §706. The employee reports do not establish distinct nonoverlapping groups or a numerical majority against representation. The general account's references to §§8(a)(1) and 8(a)(5) do not establish that each independently prohibited the poll itself; the polling issue and refusal-to-bargain consequences remain distinct.

### January 26, 1998 — Brogan v. United States

[Public Projection](../records/brogan_v_united_states_merits_1998-01-26.md#public-projection). The following complete public blocks preserve this event's action, proposition-specific authority, published positions, procedure and source limits.

#### Event

Brogan v. United States, No. 96-1579. Plenary merits decision, January 26, 1998; argued December 2, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Second Circuit, United States v. Wiener, 96 F.3d 35 (1996). The question is whether the version of 18 U.S.C. §1001 governing Brogan's October 4, 1993 statement excludes a bare false denial of wrongdoing during federal investigative questioning.

Department of Labor and Internal Revenue Service agents made an unannounced visit to Brogan's home. He agreed to answer and denied receiving cash or gifts from JRD Management. The agents already had payment records; only afterward did they reveal those records and warn that lying to investigators was criminal. He did not change his answer, and no further questions were asked. A jury convicted him of the false statement and unlawful employer payments. The latter conviction is outside this review.

#### Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

#### Public Action

The judgment on the reviewed §1001 conviction is affirmed. The statute supplies no categorical exculpatory-no exception for an otherwise covered false statement merely because it is a simple denial of wrongdoing.

#### Judgment & Remedy

**Affirm the reviewed §1001 conviction, 7–2.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg.
**Opposing:** Stevens and Breyer.

The conviction remains in force. No special remand, new trial, hearing, release, resentencing or collateral relief is ordered. The separate employer-payment conviction and other issues resolved in the appellate summary order remain outside this decision.
