Leon does not protect knowing or reckless deception, abandonment of judicial neutrality, reliance on an affidavit wholly lacking reasonable probable-cause indicia, or a facially deficient warrant; its inquiry includes obtaining and supplying officers and assumes execution within scope. Evans's qualifying court-clerical error supplies no general exception for deliberate police investigation. Inevitable discovery requires its own actual predicates. Intentional waiver and forfeiture remain distinct under Olano; if Rule 52(b) is properly invoked, error, plainness, substantial rights and the separate fairness, integrity or public-reputation inquiry retain their roles. No such omission or predicate is found merely because original papers are not reproduced.

The Court orders no new hearing, affidavit-excision proceeding or final admission/exclusion. Rule 11(a)(2) permits withdrawal after prevailing on the reserved issue; this limited correction itself neither awards Crawley withdrawal nor vacates Fields's jury verdict.

##### The judgment does not establish a unified search or entry application

**Judgment and authority:** Five Justices vacate the reviewed suppression determination and dependent appellate affirmances and remand. O'Connor, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg would require a renewed actual-vantage application while reserving search and entry legality. Stone-Zsela finds the observation a search and the planned exigency insufficient, leaving remedies for remand. The four affirming Justices find the actual shared view and specific pre-force exceptions sufficient.

The directly supported propositions above control their subjects. The two judgment-supporting application rationales are not treated as a single Marks holding: the plurality leaves open a lawful observation and entry that the concurrence rejects on this record, while the concurrence adds a police-created-exigency ground the plurality does not adopt. Neither full rationale is a logical subset supplying a common answer. The binding judgment therefore requires lawful further proceedings under the actual majority rules, without imposing either noncontrolling application as the Court's final answer. No automatic exclusion, plea withdrawal, verdict vacatur or unrelated appellate reopening follows.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Rakas and Jones supply the personal-interest inquiry; Olson's protection of shared residential privacy is extended to Crawley's specific recurring, permitted closed-room use without an all-visitors rule.
- Katz, Pinson and Myers supply protection of private domestic information and the distinction between a protected relationship, exposure, search justification and remedy. Their thermal-search holdings are not converted into a rule about every naked-eye view or every device.
- Ciraolo and Riley retain their actual public-vantage settings. Dunn supplies contextual proximity, enclosure, use and screening considerations; no first-fence rule or classification of every shared yard follows.
- Ornelas is extended to ultimate home-entry exigency, preserving factual protection and genuine due weight. Its limits on hearings, remedies and other mixed questions remain intact; Gates's warrant-issuance review is unchanged.
- Payton and Hayden retain the need for an actual home-entry exception. Wilson and Richards retain separate, particularized announcement rules and Richards's already governing independent ultimate review. No general police-created-exigency doctrine or announcement-exclusion rule is adopted.
- Murray and Karo retain genuine independence and supported untainted-warrant possibilities. Leon, Evans and Olano retain their stated institutional, factual and procedural limits; no later warrant or consent is treated as an automatic cure.

#### Law After Decision

Each defendant has a personal privacy interest on his own supported facts, with the narrower nonovernight-guest extension adopted for Crawley. Limited visibility to cotenants is not itself general publication, and incomplete screening cannot negate subjective privacy without regard to the actual vantage. The Court has not determined whether this shared yard was curtilage or the particular observation was a search.

Independent appellate review now governs ultimate warrantless-home-entry exigency, with historical facts and genuine factual inferences protected and grounded experience given due weight. The already governing Richards announcement allocation remains separate. No majority adopts either a final entry determination or a general police-created-exigency test. Conditional independent-source, consent, attenuation, reliance and preservation rules retain their actual requirements. The judgment binds the parties, while neither the plurality's reserved application nor the concurrence's broader illegality grounds becomes controlling by aggregation.

#### Separate Writings

**O'Connor, joined by Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg, in Parts II-B and V.** These four find the panel's actual-vantage analysis insufficient, even though it expressly considered trespass and shared tenant access. The fenced approach beyond street or sidewalk view, domestic room, respected closed door and lowered blinds require more than equating cotenant visibility with public exposure. The substantial gap, shared use and absence of enhancement are strong contrary facts and could support a lawful observation under the proper inquiry. They therefore make no final curtilage or search finding. O'Connor's Riley qualification leaves the defendant's privacy burden intact; the remand shifts no new public-access burden and guarantees no survey or second evidentiary opportunity. Stevens's different earlier position on the empirical aerial-use burden is not resolved anew. If the observation's legal status changes, the lower courts must consider actual untainted information, contemporaneous urgency and supported remedial alternatives. The warning is a serious potential exigency; the tactical plan does not by itself establish Stone-Zsela's asserted illegality. These Justices would remand for lawful application without automatic suppression, jury-verdict erasure or immediate conditional-plea withdrawal.

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in Parts I and II-A and in the judgment.** The officers conducted a search by deliberately crossing a fenced, nonpublic side yard to obtain a view into the substantially screened bedroom unavailable from the public way. Fields's repeated paid access and key, together with Crawley's permitted sharing, establish their bedroom privacy; limited cotenant visibility is not general publication, and plain view requires lawful presence and access. He would decide the search question now, without protecting every apartment common area or restricting observation from a public or lawfully accessible position.

He also finds the warrantless entry unjustified. Once officers had probable cause, they could not create their own entry justification by choosing a tactic designed to expose or flush the occupants instead of pursuing a warrant. The warning and movement foreseeably produced by that plan cannot furnish the emergency the plan was designed to create. His rule preserves independent safety emergencies, hot pursuit and destruction risks not created by police. He would require the lower courts to resolve suppression, consent, independent source, attenuation and the later warrant under those illegality determinations, while entering no final suppression result. Neither his actual-search application nor his police-created-exigency ground commands a majority.

**Scalia, joined by Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer in Part I, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** The actual readily visible gap exposed the well-lit activity to ordinary unaided observation from a yard shared with other tenants. Defendants retained a protected relationship to the room, but not an expectation against this particular shared view. Pinson and Myers protect concealed information; they do not require officers to disregard what this opening exposed. Possible trespass does not alone answer the personal privacy inquiry. These Justices assume Crawley's protected interest rather than exclude commercial guests categorically, and they classify neither every shared yard as public nor this yard as an open field. Their disagreement concerns the sufficiency of the established view, not a denial that the occupants desired privacy.

On that lawful-observation premise, the warning before force, the identified suspect's known violence and the observed readily disposable cocaine establish actual entry urgency and particular danger/destruction grounds for omitting advance announcement. The eventual gun and postentry flight add nothing retrospectively. Earlier opportunity to seek a warrant does not alone eliminate the later danger; the planned operation supplies no established secret evasion purpose. The four adopt no categorical drug exception and no automatic later-warrant or consent cure. They would affirm the reviewed suppression consequences, making independent-source and consent alternatives unnecessary, while joining the conditional rules that would govern an antecedent violation.

**Scalia alone, Part II.** He rejects the new extension of independent ultimate review to entry exigency, maintaining his Ornelas objection that the particular urgency assessment calls for greater appellate deference. That disagreement does not license disregard of Richards's already governing announcement review. Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer join the Court's new exigency allocation and do not join this methodological objection.

#### Procedure After Action

The case returns to the Second Circuit for lawful disposition of the reviewed suppression claims and the dependent appellate affirmances under the controlling personal-interest, limited-sharing, review and conditional-remedy rules. Questions without a majority resolution remain for the lower courts within the preserved claims and ordinary lawful procedures. Existing lawful findings may be used; no new hearing or evidence production is commanded.

Fields receives no automatic new trial, verdict vacatur, release or sentence change. Crawley receives no automatic withdrawal of his conditional plea. Informant disclosure, cocaine sentencing, Fields's fine and jury selection are not reopened. This Supreme Court review is complete; no retained jurisdiction, return date or automatic further review is ordered.

#### Source Notes

The reported proceedings, physical approach, personal-use facts, warning, entry sequence, later warrant and consent appear in [United States v. Fields, 113 F.3d 313, 318–323](https://static.case.law/f3d/113/html/0313-01.html), corroborated by the [judicial text at OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/113/f3d/313). The sole panel note explains the lower court's two-judge disposition after Judge Mahoney's death. The opinion separately records Fields's jury conviction and Crawley's conditional plea.

The published account does not conclusively establish every yard boundary, access permission or domestic-use fact, the complete warrant affidavit, independent-source findings, or the voluntariness and attenuation consequences of consent. Those limits establish neither default nor a right to a new hearing. The February 9 review date supplies the event's chronology; no historical Supreme Court docket, argument date or additional filing is represented.

### February 12, 1998 — Danahy v. Buscaglia

[Public Projection](../records/danahy_v_buscaglia_merits_1998-02-12.md#public-projection). The following complete public blocks preserve this event's action, proposition-specific authority, published positions, procedure and source limits.

#### Event

**Danahy v. Buscaglia**, Second Circuit No. 97-7264, 134 F.3d 1185. Plenary review decided February 12, 1998; October Term 1997. The Court reviews the January 27, 1998 judgment vacating denial of qualified-immunity summary judgment and remanding on seven employees' First Amendment individual-damages claims. The question is whether the individual defendants receive qualified immunity on the facts proper for this interlocutory appeal. No separate oral-argument date is established.

#### Participation

All nine Justices participated at submission and decision.

#### Public Action

The Court affirms the Court of Appeals' judgment on the reviewed First Amendment personal-damages claims, without deciding whether each dismissal was constitutionally permissible.

#### Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The judgment affirms personal damages immunity for Vacco, Buscaglia and Page on the appealed claims of Danahy, Burke, Bissram, Mungo, Matthews, Angiletta and Henesey, preserving the Court of Appeals' vacatur of the summary-judgment denial and its corresponding remand. The same unanimous coalition supports each employee's disposition.

The judgment does not dismiss the whole action, decide Weiss, Stark or Mahl's claims, or adjudicate other claims outside the appeal. It creates no prospective or official-capacity claim and decides none of their separate requirements. No final constitutional classification, personal-causation or same-decision finding, damages, reinstatement, backpay, new trial, hearing, discovery or description-production order follows.

#### Opinion Topology

Stevens delivers the opinion of the Court, Parts I–II, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Both the legal-review boundary and the particular notice ground have nine direct joins. There are no separate writings.

#### Holdings

##### A legal immunity appeal accepts the proper factual assumptions

**Holding and operative rule:** An interlocutory qualified-immunity appeal may resolve whether the properly assumed conduct violated clearly established law, but may not choose among disputed accounts of duties, knowledge, participation or motive. Section 1254 permits review of the circuit judgment without enlarging the circuit's §1291 jurisdiction; the particular notice question here is separable, and no additional factual-appeal component requiring dismissal has been identified.

**Authority:** Parts I–II of Stevens's opinion are joined in full by all eight other participating Justices. Each holding and its qualifications have nine direct votes.

**Controlling explanation:** Johnson separates legal immunity from factual sufficiency, and Behrens permits the legal question only on the proper assumed facts. Clanton and Smith preserve that distinction when reviewing a circuit judgment. The present defense accepts alleged patronage motive and nonpolicymaker status and uses the reported direct prosecutorial work and defendants' knowledge on their assumed-fact footing. It does not depend on finding a different motive or deciding that each employee personally selected targets or set policy. Calling ultimate classification a legal issue would not authorize resolving a genuine historical dispute. Conversely, the existence of unresolved constitutional or factual issues elsewhere in the action does not eliminate this separable legal question. The Court neither resolves those issues nor brings them within the appeal.

##### The particular November 1995 uncertainty supports personal damages immunity

**Holding and operative rule:** On the proper assumptions about these seven employees' actual direct and confidential prosecutorial work, the law in November 1995 left reasonable uncertainty whether political affiliation could be appropriate to their functions. Vacco, Buscaglia and Page therefore receive personal damages immunity on the reviewed First Amendment claims, without a final constitutional classification of any position or an office-wide confidentiality, at-will or civil-service exception.

**Authority:** The unanimous Parts I–II authority stated above applies to this holding, each employee's application and the claim-limited mandate.

**Controlling explanation:** Branti asks whether affiliation is appropriate for effective performance of the particular work; ordinary trust and professional secrecy are insufficient. Its reservation concerned a prosecutor's deputy, not every support employee. Harlow and Anderson nevertheless protect reasonable conduct-time legal mistakes independently of ultimate constitutional permission. Elder requires all relevant pre-existing law; obvious coverage needs no factual twin, but an abstract affiliation right does not resolve every uncertain application.

The affirmative connection here is direct work with prosecutors developing confidential investigations, audits and cases. Danahy supervised investigators working with confidential informants; Burke performed undercover work; Bissram undertook surveillance and undercover work. Mungo's audit-investigator work had the reported direct prosecutor relationship, without invented statutory powers. Matthews and Angiletta shared that relationship without borrowing others' individual admissions. Henesey combined billing analysis, extensive recipient/provider interviews and evidence custody in it; his clerk title does not reduce that account to mere filing.

Those functions, evaluated under Branti and the preconduct functional authorities described in the appellate opinion, permitted reasonable uncertainty about their relationship to prosecutorial discretion. The Court does not find that each employee exercised charging authority or that partisan agreement actually improved performance. The strongest alternative is a partial remand for Henesey, Matthews and Angiletta. Their thinner individual descriptions do not erase the affirmative common relationship, while missing descriptions permit no invented power. The immunity question is reasonable legal error on the supported functions, not final proof of partisan necessity.

Civil-service exemption, general institutional importance and confidentiality alone would not suffice. Patronage motive remains assumed; subjective innocence and a hypothetical same-decision reason supply no alternative defense. Later description refinements and later decisions cannot establish November 1995 notice or require a trial solely to obtain later-demanded descriptions. The result preserves constitutional elements, ordinary proof and other claims while resolving only these personal damages defenses.

#### Precedent Treatment

- **Elrod, Branti and Rutan:** Apply protection against impermissible political-affiliation conditions and the actual-function inquiry. Nonreappointment and ordinary confidentiality supply no categorical exception; Branti's reservation concerns a prosecutor's deputy, not all prosecutor-office staff.
- **Harlow and Anderson v. Creighton:** Apply the retained objective, particularized conduct-time defense, without a replacement burden or mandatory merits-first sequence.
- **Elder v. Holloway:** Apply all relevant preconduct law on the preserved issue; no new claim, evidence or retrospective notice follows.
- **Johnson, Behrens, Clanton and Smith:** Preserve legal immunity review on proper assumptions and exclude factual selection or unrelated claims. Smith's obvious-force application does not decide this employment boundary.
- **United States v. Lanier:** Preserve obvious coverage without a factual twin; the clarity analogy transfers no criminal element and supplies no retrospective notice.
- **Crawford-El and Mt. Healthy:** Preserve ordinary competent proof, actual personal responsibility and causation, and the independent actual same-decision defense. No such defense is found here, and a dispositive notice ground remains available.
- **Umbehr, O'Hare, Jeffries and Valot:** Preserve their distinct affiliation, speech, causation and remedy requirements; later holdings do not supply November 1995 notice.
- **Fletcher v. Kalina:** Preserve immunity by actual function, without absolute advocacy immunity for personnel decisions by prosecutorial title.
- **McCardle v. Haddad:** Preserve claim-specific procedural requirements; its different Rule 50 omission establishes no omission in this interlocutory appeal.

#### Law After Decision

The decision applies existing doctrine to the particular November 1995 legal uncertainty associated with these seven employees' reported direct prosecutorial functions. It creates no general patronage exception for confidential, technical, at-will or exempt employees and does not definitively classify any position. The substantive affiliation rule, ordinary proof requirements, legal/factual appellate boundary and separate personal defenses remain in force. The personal-damages disposition does not establish the legality of the dismissals or decide other claims and remedies.

#### Separate Writings

None.

#### Procedure After Action

The Court of Appeals' claim-limited vacatur and remand remain operative. Further lower-court proceedings must respect personal immunity on the seven reviewed First Amendment damages claims and the existing posture of other matters. The Court orders no additional hearing, evidence production, trial or return to this Court.

#### Source Notes

The published record is *Danahy v. Buscaglia*, 134 F.3d 1185, 1187–1193 (2d Cir. 1998), including its six numbered footnotes. The relevant functions and knowledge are used on the reported assumed-fact footing, not as newly resolved historical disputes. Branti, 445 U.S. 507, 517–520 and footnotes 6 and 12–14, supplies the functional distinction and the precise deputy reservation. No complete description, unquoted delegation or independent prosecution authority is inferred for an individual employee. The reported description gaps establish neither litigant default nor a duty to obtain every description, and later authorities supply no retrospective notice.

### February 13, 1998 — J. Roderick MacArthur Foundation and Lance E. Lindblom v. Federal Bureau of Investigation

[Public Projection](../records/j_roderick_macarthur_foundation_v_fbi_merits_1998-02-13.md#public-projection). The following complete public blocks preserve this event's action, proposition-specific authority, published positions, procedure and source limits.

#### Event

J. Roderick MacArthur Foundation and Lance E. Lindblom v. Federal Bureau of Investigation; D.C. Circuit No. 95-5386, 102 F.3d 600. Plenary merits decision, February 13, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 3. On review under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) of the D.C. Circuit's December 24, 1996 affirmance of summary judgment. No separate Supreme Court docket or exact argument date is stated.

The questions concern continued retention of Lindblom's First Amendment records under the Privacy Act and the plaintiffs' separate constitutional standing. The Foundation has no statutory claim. The FBI retained identified records about Lindblom's associations; the district court found they arose from legitimate investigations of others with whom plaintiffs had incidental, apparently innocent contacts. Initial collection is unchallenged. The September 6, 1995 district order dismissed the complaint with prejudice. Original monetary and equitable relief were sought; neither a final monetary entitlement nor abandonment of Lindblom's statutory money request is established. FOIA and Fifth/Ninth Amendment claims are outside review.

#### Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate. All nine were seated at submission and decision.

#### Public Action

Affirmed in part, vacated in part, and remanded. The Court corrects the statutory retention rule and adopts the complete Continuing Pertinence Test. The separate constitutional claims remain dismissed for lack of standing, without prejudice. No final damages or expungement award issues.

#### Judgment & Remedy

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Remedy or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Lindblom's Privacy Act retention claim | Vacate statutory summary judgment and remand, 7–2 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | Apply the complete test and resolve remaining lawful liability and relief questions; no finding that the actual records lack pertinence and no final award. |
| Foundation's constitutional standing | Sustain rejection, 9–0 | All nine participating Justices | — | No constitutional merits decision or restored relief. |
| Lindblom's separate constitutional standing | Sustain rejection, 9–0 | All nine participating Justices | — | No constitutional merits decision; the distinct statutory claim proceeds. |
| Preclusive form of both constitutional dismissals | Vacate the with-prejudice effect and direct dismissal without prejudice, 9–0 | All nine participating Justices | — | Conform the dismissal to its jurisdictional basis only; Stone-Zsela agrees in this disposition without joining its rationale. |

Original collection and abandoned or unreviewed components remain undisturbed without adoption of their reasoning. The earlier discovery refusal is not independently reversed. Further procedure must follow the corrected issues and ordinary standards, with confidentiality protected; no hearing, discovery, amended pleading, agency audit or destruction is automatically ordered.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg in full | Supports every component | I: statutory Article III/action/consent; II: adverse effect; III: retention and complete test; IV-A: constitutional standing; IV-B: without-prejudice form; V-A: statutory remand; V-B: separate remedy limits. |
| Same opinion, partial joins | Breyer | Stone-Zsela | Supports every disposition; agrees in reaching statutory merits and in the constitutional form correction without additional rationale joins | III, IV-A and V-A. Does not join I, II, IV-B or V-B's additional grounds; agrees that no final monetary or expungement relief is awarded. |
| Same opinion, partial joins | Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | Support statutory merits reach and constitutional dispositions; would affirm statutory summary judgment | I, IV-A, IV-B and V-B. Reserve II; reject III and V-A. |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas in full | Would affirm the statutory judgment; supports constitutional dismissals without prejudice | Lawful historical-activity construction; additional record-specific constitutional injury/imminence ground; independent remedy limits. |

Parts I, IV-B and V-B have all eight Associates. Part II has Breyer, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg. Part III's complete test and V-A's statutory mandate have those six plus Stone-Zsela. IV-A has all nine. Scalia and Thomas's agreement with particular protection, lawful-authority and remedy propositions is not a join in the complete test. Every controlling rule rests on direct majority agreement; no combined fractured-decision rationale is asserted.

#### Holdings

##### I. A live personal statutory controversy and an express statutory action permit merits review

**Holding and operative rule:** Actual continued custody of identified records describing this claimant's protected associations supplies a concrete personal information interest directly traceable to the retaining agency; an actually requested award within §552a(g)(1)(D)/(g)(4)'s legally available monetary category can meaningfully redress the claimed invasion if its requirements are established. Jurisdiction does not depend on first proving the disputed retention violation or final damages entitlement, and the substantial statutory claim falls within the Act's express agency action and conditional sovereign consent without creating a new constitutional damages cause.

**Authority:** Part I, joined by all eight Associate Justices; Stone-Zsela agrees in reaching the merits without joining this rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** Steel Co. requires Article III power before these disputed merits and, with Bell v. Hood, separates a substantial federal claim from ultimate success. The acknowledged personal records establish the occurrence of the claimed intrusion. Warth and Havens recognize the difference between invasion of one's own protected interest and a demand for lawful government; neither supplies a Privacy Act remedy or excuses summary-judgment proof under Lujan and Lee. Subjective chill, presumed emotional loss and speculation about employers do not carry this statutory conclusion.

The FBI itself retains the information. Lindblom actually sought damages, the statutory claim continued on appeal, and no abandonment or legal bar to every monetary remedy is established. Arizonans rejects legally unavailable relief; it does not require a renewed formulaic prayer. Alexander supports meaningful partial redress despite uncertainty about recovery, without transferring its pleading-stage standard here. Culpability, adverse effect and monetary entitlement still require their own legal treatment.

Section 1331 and the review statute provide jurisdictional channels, not consent. Section 552a(g)(1)(D) authorizes an individual action against the agency and jurisdiction over it; (g)(4) supplies conditional United States monetary liability. Meyer requires that separate source of cause and obligation; the text supplies it here. Bell is not a waiver, §702 supplies no money, and no agency Bivens action is created. The Court neither classifies every federal waiver condition as nonjurisdictional nor presumes every remaining statutory predicate satisfied.

##### II. The protected personal retention invasion can itself be an adverse effect

**Holding and operative rule:** Actual unwanted maintenance of identified records describing this individual's protected associations, if outside §552a(e)(7)'s lawful alternatives, can itself supply the adverse personal effect required by §552a(g)(1)(D). A merely abstract or technical violation with no adverse personal consequence is insufficient; an adverse agency determination, public disclosure, lost employment or pecuniary loss is not independently required as such.

**Authority:** Part II, joined by Breyer, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg. Scalia and Thomas reserve this construction; Stone-Zsela does not join it.

**Controlling explanation:** Section (D) requires personal adverse effect, not the adverse determination separately required by (C). Its additional words exclude abstract procedural noncompliance without personal impact. Where the substantive protection concerns keeping the claimant's own associational information, the actual invasion and its adverse personal consequence can coincide. Requiring a different kind of downstream injury would leave that particular interest unprotected merely because the information remained undisclosed.

Havens illustrates a direct personal statutory invasion but does not decide this additional Privacy Act language. Albright distinguishes adverse effect, causation, intentional or willful conduct and monetary recovery; its recognition of emotional trauma does not make such trauma exclusive or settle the damages minimum. Doe v. FBI enforced a no-adverse-effect finding unchallenged in that case, where money was not pursued on appeal. Neither finding nor abandonment is established here. The Court does not find distress, declare these records unlawful, equate every violation with adverse effect or treat adverse effect as actual damages under (g)(4).

##### III. Continued retention must satisfy the complete Continuing Pertinence Test

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 552a(e)(7) regulates continued maintenance as well as original collection. Unless retention is expressly authorized by statute or by the individual, the information must remain pertinent to and within the scope of an authorized law-enforcement activity; lawful original collection is not conclusive permission to retain it after every qualifying connection has ceased. The Court adopts the following complete rule:

1. **Does the challenged record fall within the statutory protection?**

   The plaintiff identifies records describing their First Amendment activities and establishes that the government continues to maintain them. The court first checks whether retention is independently authorized by statute or by the individual.

2. **Can the government identify an authorized law-enforcement activity?**

   The agency must establish the legal authority for that activity. It need not prove the plaintiff committed a crime. Intelligence investigations, criminal investigations, and authorized civil enforcement may qualify.

3. **Does the information remain pertinent to that activity?**

   The court examines the actual relationship, not a hypothetical future possibility. Relevant considerations include:

   - Why the information was collected.
   - What investigative purpose it still serves.
   - Whether it preserves necessary context or evidence.
   - Whether subsequent events have changed its relevance.
   - Whether continued use or indexing exceeds the justified investigative scope.

   No single consideration automatically decides the case.

4. **What disposition and remedy does the law authorize?**

   If a qualifying connection remains, the agency may retain the information under the statutory exception. If no qualifying connection remains, the court determines the violation and available relief under the Privacy Act.

   The court separately accounts for lawful evidence-preservation and federal records-management duties.

**Authority:** Part III, joined by Breyer, Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg. Each joins the entire four-step rule, including every listed consideration and the separate preservation/relief requirements. Scalia and Thomas reject the complete rule.

**Controlling explanation:** The Act defines maintenance to include keeping, collection, use and dissemination, while (e)(7) attaches its prohibition and alternatives to that continuing conduct. Congress's different acquisition-based terms elsewhere do not make initial lawfulness permanently dispositive. The three alternatives remain distinct: express statutory authorization, individual authorization and authorized-law-enforcement pertinence.

Authorized activity is broader than an active investigation or a current investigation of this plaintiff. Genuine historical intelligence value, connected or later inquiries, necessary evidentiary context and legitimate preservation of a law-enforcement record may supply an actual continuing relationship. Age, closure, innocence and name retrieval alone decide nothing. Conversely, the agency's general mission or the possibility that any datum might someday prove useful cannot conclusively establish every record's pertinence indefinitely. The Government's identified continuing-interest contention remains available for record-specific assessment.

Step 2 requires the agency to establish the activity's legal authority, without proof that the plaintiff committed a crime. Step 3's considerations illuminate actual pertinence and scope; they are nonexclusive, none is automatically decisive, and they create no current-necessity or least-restrictive-recordkeeping standard. The rule imposes no periodic audit, automatic request-response duty, deadline, universal consultation, prescribed explanation format or public disclosure of classified reasons, and no general shift of the independent burdens concerning injury, fault or damages. Ordinary judicial procedure and appropriate confidential review remain available.

The contrary historical-activity construction properly recognizes preservation value but improperly makes that value conclusive solely from acquisition. Department of Defense v. FLRA keeps disclosure protections and their exceptions distinct; confidentiality does not replace Congress's separate retention restriction. No dissemination claim, automatic purge on closure or present finding of nonpertinence is decided.

##### IV-A. The separate constitutional claims lack the required supported connection

**Holding and operative rule:** The Foundation's affidavits do not establish the causal mechanism connecting FBI retention to prospective grantees' or employers' knowledge and reactions; Lindblom's separately presented constitutional claim supplies no independent supported basis beyond that failed derivative theory. Both constitutional-standing rejections therefore stand, without a ruling on the First Amendment merits or a categorical exclusion of reputational or associational injury.

**Authority:** Part IV-A has all nine Justices. Scalia and Thomas's additional injury/imminence ground is separate and noncontrolling.

**Controlling explanation:** Lujan and Lee require specific support at summary judgment, with favorable reasonable inferences. Predictions of stigma or employment effects do not themselves establish how these audiences learn of the retained references. Meese involved an actual public governmental label and detailed evidence connecting it to threatened harm. It shows why completed loss or identification of every deterred person is unnecessary; it does not supply the missing exposure link here. Nor does a claimant's own publicity categorically make all resulting injury self-inflicted.

Lindblom supplied no separate constitutional-standing argument or evidentiary showing. His pleaded subjective chill is not summary-judgment proof under Laird, and his employment elsewhere supplies no independent demonstrated constitutional injury. The actual personal statutory information interest has a different direct connection to the FBI's custody; its recognition does not manufacture the missing presentation of the distinct constitutional claim. The Court decides neither that FBI recordkeeping can never violate the First Amendment nor that these statutory records have no adverse personal effect.

##### IV-B. The constitutional dismissals must be without prejudice

**Holding and operative rule:** A dismissal of these constitutional claims for want of Article III standing cannot carry a merits-preclusive effect. The standing rejections are sustained, but the district judgment's with-prejudice effect is vacated and the case is remanded to conform those dismissals to their jurisdictional ground.

**Authority:** Part IV-B has all eight Associates; Stone-Zsela agrees in the disposition only. The correction is unanimous.

**Controlling explanation:** Arizonans and Lee distinguish limited authority to correct a lower court's jurisdictional disposition from authority to decide constitutional merits. The September 6 order dismissed the entire complaint with prejudice. Leaving that effect on claims dismissed for lack of standing would imply a merits adjudication the courts lack power to enter. Section 2106 permits the bounded correction but creates no substantive jurisdiction. No declaration or injunction is restored, and no evidentiary proceeding on the constitutional merits is directed.

##### V-A. The statutory judgment requires record-specific further proceedings

**Holding and operative rule:** The conclusive original-collection rule cannot sustain summary judgment on Lindblom's retention claim. That component is vacated and remanded for the complete test's application to the actual records, with any independent statutory or individual authorization and lawful preservation obligation considered separately; the Court awards neither expungement nor damages.

**Authority:** Part V-A has Breyer, Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg. Scalia and Thomas oppose statutory remand.

**Controlling explanation:** The finding of legitimate original compilation does not answer whether each record retains the relationship now required. The Government contends that some other persons and organizations remain of investigative interest; that contention may matter, but the Court does not make the missing record-specific pertinence determination in either party's favor. Confidential and in camera assessment remains available where appropriate. Any concrete evidence-preservation or federal records-management duty must be accounted for without treating a general reference to preservation as conclusive permission or as an automatic destruction command. The remand corrects the governing legal rule; it does not itself reverse the discovery refusal, require additional evidence or pleadings, order an agency review program, or find intentional wrongdoing. Unreviewed claims remain undisturbed.

##### V-B. Each remedial route retains its independent requirements

**Holding and operative rule:** The retention restriction does not create unlimited relief: monetary liability under §552a(g)(1)(D)/(g)(4), amendment relief under (d)/(g)(1)(A)/(g)(2), access relief, and any independently permissible prospective equity retain their distinct conditions. Neither an automatic monetary award nor a general expungement entitlement follows from the Court's construction.

**Authority:** Part V-B has all eight Associates. Stone-Zsela agrees that no final award issues and joins Part V-A's separate lawful-relief and preservation directions, without adopting V-B's additional grounds.

**Controlling explanation:** Intentional or willful noncompliance, causally resulting compensable actual damages and the statutory minimum remain separate questions; the Court decides no amount or nonpecuniary-damages issue. Costs and fees do not independently supply redress for an otherwise absent controversy. No constitutional damages cause against the FBI is created.

An amendment route must satisfy its applicable request, refusal, review and exemption predicates. Exhausted access requests do not alone establish amendment exhaustion. Nagel recognizes the distinct (e)(7) claim and monetary route; Clarkson links possible expungement to the amendment provisions and their limits; Doe rejects bypassing the Act's remedies. Their qualified accounts supply no unconditional purge warrant. Sections (j) and (k) do not permit exemption from (e)(7), and a regulation cannot defeat that nonexemptible duty merely by exempting civil liability. A valid (d) exemption may nonetheless defeat a derivative amendment claim. A FOIA exemption or general records-system designation does not alone settle those distinct predicates; no waiver is presumed.

Reich and §702 preserve possible nonmonetary restraint subject to an existing lawful action, statutory preclusion and ordinary equitable limits. The Court need not decide that entire route because the monetary action permits this statutory review. No separate APA claim is created. If a mandatory decree is authorized, §702 requires identification of the responsible officer or successor by name or title; absence of a personal name from the caption alone does not answer every equitable question. These reservations govern any further relief, not the independently resolved Article III controversy.

#### Precedent Treatment

- **Steel Co. (June 2, 1997):** Applied for constitutional power before disputed statutory merits and the distinction between substantial claims and recovery; its fully corrected reporting default does not decide this ongoing personal-custody controversy. Its confined already-controlled-merits exceptions are not used.
- **Bell v. Hood:** Applied to the substantial federal claim; preserved as neither a sovereign waiver nor permission to assume personal injury.
- **Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife:** Applied for concrete personal injury, direct traceability, likely redress and summary-judgment evidence; no particular-document or additional downstream-loss requirement is added.
- **Warth v. Seldin; Havens Realty Corp. v. Coleman:** Applied for actual personal statutory interests and the distinction from abstract illegality; neither is treated as deciding Privacy Act remedies or its adverse-effect text.
- **Arizonans for Official English:** Applied to exclude legally impossible remedies and to permit limited jurisdictional correction; no magic-word damages rule is inferred.
- **Lee v. Harcleroad:** Applied to claim-specific proof and jurisdictional dismissal; its unsupported future patient injury does not erase an established personal statutory occurrence.
- **Alexander v. Whitman:** Applied for meaningful partial redress despite uncertain ultimate recovery, preserving procedural-stage and defendant-specific limits; its controlled-merits setting is not extended.
- **FDIC v. Meyer:** Applied to distinguish sovereign consent, an authorized action and relief; its ban on direct agency Bivens damages remains intact.
- **Department of Defense v. FLRA:** Applied to the distinct Privacy Act disclosure protection and enumerated exceptions; neither its disclosure balance nor §(b)(7) is substituted for retention's three alternatives.
- **Laird v. Tatum; Meese v. Keene:** Applied respectively to subjective constitutional chill and a supported governmental mechanism for reputational injury; neither categorically excludes the personal statutory interest decided here.
- **Chamber of Commerce v. Reich:** Preserved for qualified prospective equitable restraint; no automatic bypass of the Privacy Act's remedial scheme is adopted.

The circuit decisions Albright, Doe v. FBI, Nagel and Clarkson inform the distinct statutory elements and remedial routes at their actual scope. Albright's damages questions and Doe's case-specific adverse-effect finding are not imported as findings here. No Supreme Court precedent is overruled.

#### Law After Decision

Courts reviewing maintained First Amendment records must apply the complete Continuing Pertinence Test. Original lawful collection remains relevant but cannot conclusively excuse all later retention. Genuine historical law-enforcement value remains permissible; closure, age, innocence and name indexing do not automatically require deletion. The distinct statutory, individual-authorization, remedy and preservation requirements remain operative.

The six-Justice adverse-effect holding recognizes the actual unlawful personal information invasion as potentially sufficient under (g)(1)(D), without establishing actual damages. The eight-Associate threshold/action rulings permit merits review of this existing personal monetary controversy without assuming ultimate recovery. The constitutional dismissals rest on the actual causal and claim-specific defects, with no new categorical restriction on reputational injury. No periodic review program, unrestricted disclosure, automatic damages or unconditional expungement rule is created.

#### Separate Writings

**Scalia, joined by Thomas, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** They agree that the actual personal statutory controversy permits review, that the constitutional claims fail on the Court's stated grounds, and that their dismissals must be without prejudice. They reserve whether the direct retention invasion alone satisfies statutory adverse effect because statutory nonviolation suffices on their view.

For them, a record pertinent to a lawful investigation remains pertinent to that authorized activity even after it is completed. The continuing maintenance prohibition still forbids keeping records never within a lawful statutory category; it need not require a renewed present-use justification for the conceded lawful historical record. Legitimate intelligence preservation reinforces that reading. They would affirm statutory summary judgment and reject the complete test's continuing-connection requirement, while accepting its severable protected-record, legal-authority and independent-remedy principles. They do not find the information unlawfully collected, endorse unrestricted disclosure or regard closure and innocent contact as automatic violations.

They also conclude that the reported constitutional predictions lack sufficiently concrete or imminent personal consequences on this record, independently of traceability. They impose no named-lost-grantee rule, particular affidavit form or numerical probability requirement. That additional ground has two votes and is not the Court's holding. No final monetary or expungement entitlement follows under their position.

#### Procedure After Action

The statutory summary judgment is vacated and the case returns through the Court of Appeals for lawful further proceedings under the complete test. Present pertinence, independent authorization, remaining monetary liability, lawful amendment or equitable relief, confidentiality and any concrete preservation obligation remain for their proper determination. The constitutional-standing rejections remain in place; the lower judgment must be conformed to dismissal of those claims without prejudice. No constitutional declaration or injunction revives. Original collection, abandoned claims and other unreviewed components remain undisturbed.

#### Source Notes

The reported record is 102 F.3d 600–609 (D.C. Cir. 1996), including its separate opinion and footnote; the district order is reproduced at petition appendix 20a. The Government's opposition at 3 attributes the original damages request to Lindblom, and 9 n.6 states its alternative continuing-investigative-interest position. The reply at 2 and 4–5 disclaims an active-investigation-only condition and continual review. These party accounts are not findings of current pertinence, distress, monetary entitlement or exhaustion of amendment procedures.

The operative provisions are the 1997 text of 5 U.S.C. §§552a(a), (b), (d), (e)(7), (g), (j), (k), and 702, with 28 C.F.R. §16.96 (1997). The complete four-step rule above states the Court's adopted Continuing Pertinence Test verbatim. Source limits concerning original complaint details, administrative correspondence and incorporated district arguments establish no litigant default, abandonment or exemption waiver. The Court makes no record-specific final pertinence, fault, damages or purge finding.


### 1998-02-14 — United States v. Kirk

[Public Projection](../records/united_states_v_kirk_merits_1998-02-14.md#public-projection).

#### Event

United States v. Kirk (William Joseph Kirk v. United States); Fifth Circuit No. 94-50472, judgment reported at 105 F.3d 997. Plenary merits decision, February 14, 1998, on review of the Fifth Circuit's en banc affirmance by an equally divided court. No separate Supreme Court docket or oral-argument date is established. The question is whether 18 U.S.C. §922(o)'s possession prohibition is within the commerce power as applied to Kirk's preserved Count One conviction without transaction-specific interstate proof. Kirk pleaded guilty conditionally after denial of his constitutional motion; dismissed transfer counts and other appellate issues are outside the grant. Rybar, decided November 24, 1997, is earlier precedent, not a consolidated companion.

#### Participation

All nine Justices participate: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Public Action

The Court affirms the judgment on the preserved Commerce Clause challenge.

#### Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed, 9–0.** Supporting: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Count One remains effective against the Commerce Clause challenge. No Commerce-based vacatur, evidentiary remand, new trial, or transaction-specific interstate allegation is required. The Court does not reinstate dismissed transfer counts or reach the ungranted plea-agreement, double-jeopardy or sentencing issues.

#### Opinion Topology

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. No separate writing is filed.

#### Holdings

##### Possession of prohibited post-1986 machinegun stock

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 922(o) may reach possession of a completed machinegun within the prohibited post-May 19, 1986 civilian stock without proof of a defendant-specific interstate or commercial transaction, because the possession prohibition is reasonably necessary to prevent evasion or material impairment of Congress's valid national restriction on additions to that stock. The Commerce Clause together with the Necessary and Proper Clause supplies that class-and-regime connection; no additional transaction-specific interstate element is required.

**Authority:** The unanimous opinion of the Court; all nine Justices join this rule, its application and its qualifications.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Hale's possession rule does not disappear when a particular transfer cannot be proved. Local conversion creates completed prohibited stock that can be retained, concealed, acquired and kept available for circulation. Excluding that possession would materially impair the stock restriction itself. Rybar applied the same rule where gun-show sales made the market connection concrete; its facts did not convert a proved sale into a universal constitutional prerequisite. Kirk's possession conviction therefore stands without treating the reported sales as admitted elements or reviving dismissed transfer charges.

Lopez requires an identified interstate mechanism before aggregation can measure its magnitude. Here the mechanism is the specific transfer-and-stock regime, its grandfathered lawful stock, and the fitted possession prohibition. It is not dangerousness, national marketability, remote interstate movement, generalized gun crime or downstream productivity. Nor does administrative convenience alone establish necessity. A particular interstate element may supply a connection in another statute, but courts need not insert one where this valid regime supplies the connection. Formal findings are not universally required and cannot create power by recital; the identified enacted structure supplies the necessary relationship here.

**Operative exceptions and limits:** Section 922(o)(2) preserves transfers to or by, and possession by or under the authority of, the United States or its departments or agencies, or a State or its departments, agencies or political subdivisions. It separately preserves lawful transfer or possession of a machinegun lawfully possessed before May 19, 1986; earlier manufacture alone is insufficient. Neither exception is established for Count One. The holding establishes no general federal firearms police power, no rule for every locally manufactured object, and no automatic justification for every product-market possession prohibition. It decides neither Second Amendment protection nor every conversion-parts, readily-restorable or wear-induced application of the machinegun definition.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Lopez supplies the connection-before-magnitude architecture and the necessary-inclusion route; its limit on generalized downstream effects and judicial insertion of elements remains intact.
- Hale is applied to completed prohibited stock produced by local conversion; its anti-evasion and material-impairment rule and statutory exceptions are preserved.
- Rybar is applied without treating its gun-show sales as a new element or a constitutional condition for every possession prosecution; its separate arms holding is not before the Court.
- Wickard and Perez retain their economic-class aggregation scope; neither supplies an independent disposition here.
- Parker's cross-border payment obligation is a distinct commerce mechanism and supplies no substitute firearms rule.

#### Law After Decision

Hale's existing possession authority includes this locally converted completed machinegun even without transaction-specific interstate proof. The decision clarifies that application rather than adding a new Commerce category. It leaves ordinary firearm possession, other statutory applications and independent constitutional challenges subject to their own rules.

#### Separate Writings

No separate writing is filed.

#### Procedure After Action

The Fifth Circuit judgment is affirmed in the reviewed respect. Count One is not vacated or remanded on the Commerce Clause ground; no new trial, resentencing, transfer prosecution or factual inquiry is ordered.

#### Source Notes

The indictment, conditional plea, reported conversion and test firing, scope of conviction, and divided appellate posture appear in [70 F.3d 791](https://static.case.law/f3d/70/html/0791-01.html), [105 F.3d 997](https://static.case.law/f3d/105/html/0997-01.html), and the [rehearing order, 78 F.3d 160](https://static.case.law/f3d/78/html/0160-01.html). The reported sales are background recitations, not additional admitted elements of Kirk's plea. No interstate movement of the weapon, parts or charged possession is established or required by the holding.

### 1998-02-17 — Johnson v. Rodriguez

[Public Projection](../records/johnson_v_rodriguez_merits_1998-02-17.md#public-projection).

#### Event

Johnson v. Rodriguez; Fifth Circuit Nos. 95-50879 and 96-50382, 110 F.3d 299. Plenary merits decision, February 17, 1998, on review under 28 U.S.C. §1254. No separate Supreme Court docket or oral-argument date is established. After a consent bench trial under §636(c), the trial court entered relief concerning confidential parole protest letters and writ-writers. The Fifth Circuit reversed the protest-letter decree and directed dismissal with prejudice, reversed and remanded the writ-writer claim, including retaliation and equality, and vacated attorney fees. The questions concern initial-parole liberty, protest-letter equality, protected litigation, causation, injury and the scope of class relief. This is an official-capacity prospective-relief action, not a personal-damages immunity appeal.

#### Participation

All nine Justices participate: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Public Action

The Court affirms the protest-letter dismissal and fee vacatur, vacates the incompatible writ-writer directions, and remands the retaliation and writ-writer equality claims for further proceedings.

#### Judgment & Remedy

The supporting coalition on every disposition below is Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer: 9–0 on each component. Stone-Zsela agrees only in the disposition of the separate writ-writer equality and fee components, adding no ground.

| Component | Disposition | Remedy and scope |
|---|---|---|
| Initial-parole process and protest-letter equality | Affirm rejection of process claim and dismissal with prejudice of protest-letter claim | No disclosure, accuracy-review or adversarial-hearing entitlement arises from the claimed parole interest |
| Writ-writer retaliation | Vacate incompatible categorical protection and burden directions; preserve remand otherwise | Identify protected conduct, actual adverse injury, motivating role and any actual same-decision defense |
| Distinct writ-writer equal protection | Preserve remand under corrected protection and causation premises | Determine similarly situated treatment and purposeful adverse effect; no liability finding |
| Classwide prospective decree | Do not restore the overbroad injunction; remand for supported injury, attribution and scope findings | Named-plaintiff standing, ongoing attributable practice and corresponding class injury must support fitted relief |
| Attorney-fee award | Preserve vacatur | Any later entitlement depends on the lawful judgment eventually entered |

No parole, release, damages, new trial, compulsory discovery, fee award or statewide injunction is ordered.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer in full | Supports every component | Parts I–V |
| Same opinion, partial joins | Kennedy | Stone-Zsela | Supports every disposition | I-A: no initial-release entitlement; I-B: protest-letter rejection; II-A: retaliation and Mt. Healthy; IV-A: standing, attributable practice and fitted relief; V's corresponding mandate. Agrees in correcting and remanding the writ-writer standard without joining II-B's additional assistance/petition grounds. Disposition only, with no additional ground, on III's separate writ-writer equality and IV-B's fee vacatur. |

No separate writing is filed. The eight Associates supply a direct majority for every part; Stone-Zsela's identified joins add a ninth vote only at that scope.

#### Holdings

##### I-A. Initial parole and confidential-file process

**Holding and operative rule:** Texas's parole scheme creates no entitlement to initial release because its substantive eligibility conditions and required procedures do not meaningfully restrict the Board's authority to deny release. Absent that entitlement, claimed unreliability in confidential protest material does not establish the asserted procedural due-process right to file correction, disclosure or an adversarial parole hearing.

**Authority:** Kennedy's Part I-A, joined by all nine Justices.

**Controlling explanation:** Sandin requires a genuine substantive constraint, not mandatory words alone. The Court examines the whole scheme: eligibility restrictions and guidelines limit when the Board may grant release but do not require release when particular facts exist. Young protects community freedom already conferred; Johnson remains in ordinary sentence-authorized custody. Article 42.18 §8(f)(2) requires victim-related notice, permits written and oral participation without a numerical ceiling on written speakers, and requires consideration of the victim-impact statement. Those duties do not compel parole. Section 8(e)'s file-inclusion commands do not require reliance on every allegation; §18(a)'s confidentiality likewise creates no release entitlement. This holding concerns the asserted procedural-liberty theory and does not excuse a separate unconstitutional penalty for protected conduct.

##### I-B. Protest-letter equal protection

**Holding and operative rule:** The protest-letter system establishes no purposeful unconstitutional differential treatment or irrational parole criterion on this record. The distinct class claim is therefore dismissed with prejudice; inaccurate or vindictive statements do not alone justify a prohibition on receiving or considering every protest statement.

**Authority:** Kennedy's Part I-B, joined by all nine Justices.

**Controlling explanation:** Even assuming prisoners receiving protest letters supply a comparison, receiving relevant information, allowing victim participation, protecting sources from reprisal and avoiding a full adversarial parole proceeding are rational purposes. The trial court's concerns about some letters do not invalidate that entire process or establish the required purposeful discrimination. The Court does not adopt a universal rule that an equal-protection class must exist before government acts. Nor does it decide claims of knowing official falsification, racial discrimination, retaliation for protected litigation, use of an invalidated conviction contrary to a binding order, or every possible individual false-statement claim.

##### II-A. Retaliation for protected litigation

**Holding and operative rule:** A parole board may not deny discretionary parole because a prisoner engaged in protected, nonfrivolous litigation. The claimant must prove the protected conduct, adverse action and its substantial or motivating causal role; officials may then defeat the claim by proving by a preponderance that they would have taken the same action without that conduct.

**Authority:** Kennedy's Part II-A, joined by all nine Justices.

**Controlling explanation:** Crawford-El applies Mt. Healthy's separate burdens. A merely conceivable lawful parole reason is not proof that the same decision would actually have occurred. Competent direct and circumstantial evidence remain available under the ordinary preponderance standard; no heightened quantum or categorical direct-evidence rule is added. Lack of a parole entitlement does not authorize a protected-right penalty. A retaliatory parole denial itself supplies adverse injury when causally established: a claimant complaining of punishment for completed protected litigation need not additionally prove that the lawsuit failed. Nonfrivolous direct appeals, habeas claims and conditions litigation are protected, while frivolous filings and unrestricted jailhouse lawyering are not protected merely by their labels. Further findings must identify Johnson's own protected conduct, its causal role and the Board actors responsible. The Court finds neither ultimate retaliation nor entitlement to parole.

##### II-B. Necessary assistance and independent petition activity

**Holding and operative rule:** The remand may not categorically exclude all secondary legal assistance or independently protected communication and petition activity. Necessary assistance must be assessed under the recipient's meaningful-access right, effective alternative arrangements and valid penological restrictions; no free-standing right to act as another prisoner's chosen lawyer follows.

**Authority:** Kennedy's Part II-B, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer: eight direct joins. Stone-Zsela joins the corrective disposition without this additional rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** Lewis requires actual or evidence-supported imminent hindrance of a qualifying nonfrivolous claim for a claim of inadequate access assistance. It neither terminates all assistance protection once a case is filed nor makes independent retaliation depend on a failed lawsuit. Johnson v. Avery and Wolff preserve assistance needed for meaningful access while allowing effective alternatives and legitimate restrictions. The lower court must distinguish necessary help, Johnson's own filings, frivolous conduct and any separately grounded communication or petition theory actually within the case. Mere reference to litigation does not make all conduct protected, but a categorical exclusion would discard potentially protected activity without examining its predicate. Supported findings and common evidence remain available; the remand adds no new evidence code.

##### III. The separate writ-writer equality claim

**Holding and operative rule:** The remanded writ-writer equality claim requires relevant similarly situated treatment and purposeful discriminatory adverse effect under the actual right asserted. A penalty for independently protected litigation is not justified merely by describing all litigiousness as unreformed behavior; generalized litigation references or historical bias by another agency do not establish the claim.

**Authority:** Kennedy's Part III, joined by all seven other Associates: eight direct joins. Stone-Zsela agrees in preserving this component on remand and adds no ground.

**Controlling explanation:** The protest-letter dismissal does not resolve this different claim. Courts must identify the comparison, protected-right predicate when invoked, purposeful differential treatment and causal injury. A conceivable rational basis for an ordinary classification is not a same-decision defense to proved retaliation. Comparative evidence need not always be statistical; missing statistics alone do not resolve every claim. The Court adopts no new strict-scrutiny rule and makes no liability finding.

##### IV-A. Standing, Board attribution and class relief

**Holding and operative rule:** Johnson must establish his own redressable injury and basis for prospective relief. Systemwide relief for the challenged parole practice requires a proved ongoing official policy or widespread authorized practice, corresponding class injury and relief fitted to that violation; class certification and isolated employee references to litigation establish none of those predicates by themselves.

**Authority:** Kennedy's Part IV-A, joined by all nine Justices.

**Controlling explanation:** Lewis links common relief to a demonstrated common barrier and identified affected group; ordinary standing requires the named plaintiff's own injury and prospective exposure. The record does not yet connect identified protected conduct and attributable Board retaliation to Johnson's parole decision or justify the decree's statewide reach. Earlier Ruiz findings about other agencies and subjective perceptions do not prove the challenged Board practice. Common evidence may establish a common wrong, but its functional and geographic reach must be supported. Any relief must respect valid security interests and applicable prospective-relief law. The Court imposes no universal Monell prerequisite on every state-officer injunction, does not purge all references to litigation, and does not order a particular discovery method or trial.

##### IV-B. Dependent fees

**Holding and operative rule:** The attorney-fee award cannot rest on the reversed trial decree and remains vacated. Any later prevailing-party entitlement must follow the lawful judgment ultimately entered.

**Authority:** Kennedy's Part IV-B, joined by all seven other Associates: eight direct joins. Stone-Zsela agrees in this disposition without an added ground.

**Controlling explanation:** The present correction preserves the need for new findings and supplies no merits victory establishing fees. It determines neither a later amount nor a permanent inability to recover a fee.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Sandin is applied to genuine substantive constraints; mandatory procedures alone do not require initial parole.
- Young and Morrissey are distinguished because community liberty already conferred is different from initial discretionary release.
- Crawford-El and Mt. Healthy supply ordinary motive proof and the officials' distinct preponderance same-decision defense; neither is altered.
- Lewis supplies meaningful assistance, qualifying access injury and fitted common relief; its noncontrolling categorical secondary-assistance and initial-filing restrictions are not adopted.
- Johnson v. Avery and Wolff preserve necessary assistance and effective alternatives; they establish no unrestricted entitlement to serve as jailhouse counsel.
- Lujan and Lyons preserve personal injury, causation and the prospective basis for relief.
- Turner preserves restrictions reasonably related to legitimate penological interests; an asserted security label alone is insufficient.
- Umbehr and Valot preserve the distinction between absence of benefit entitlement and a protected-right penalty; their separate employment or contractor rules are not imported into this prison case.
- Monell's governmental-attribution principle is not converted into a universal condition for every state-officer injunction. No precedent is overruled.

#### Law After Decision

Discretionary initial parole creates no federal accuracy code for the confidential file. That absence of entitlement remains separate from protected-litigation retaliation. Existing proof and access rules govern the remand, and the distinct equality claim survives for its own required showing. Neither a statewide violation nor a right to parole follows. The Court preserves the unappealed ex post facto, tentative-parole-month/progress, nonresident-furlough and corporate-entity-immunity dispositions.

#### Separate Writings

No separate writing is filed; Stone-Zsela’s partial joins and disposition-only agreements are stated in the opinion topology.

#### Procedure After Action

The protest-letter dismissal and fee vacatur stand. The appellate writ-writer directions are vacated only insofar as they conflict with the protection and burden rules above; further proceedings otherwise remain required on retaliation and writ-writer equality. The statewide decree is not restored. Lower courts must determine protected activity, causation, Johnson's injury, attributable practice and the lawful scope of relief without reopening the unappealed claims.

#### Source Notes

The lower judgment, bench-trial posture, Texas provisions, unresolved findings and limits on the appeal appear in [Johnson v. Rodriguez, 110 F.3d 299](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/110/110.F3d.299.95-50879.96-50382.html). The descriptions of unreliable letters and litigation references are record findings or submissions at their stated scope, not a finding that every letter is false or all of Johnson's litigation is frivolous. The appellate account identifies the findings still needed on remand; it supports no present personal-damages immunity ruling.

### 1998-02-23 — Arteaga v. United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit / Arteaga v. Wilson

[Public Projection](../records/arteaga_v_united_states_court_of_appeals_filing_order_1998-02-23.md#public-projection).

#### Event

Arteaga v. United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, No. 97-6749, and the separate financial-leave motion in Arteaga v. Wilson. Filing-administration order, February 23, 1998. No separate printed Supreme Court docket for Wilson is supplied. The two current Rule 39 motions concern permission to file without prepaying fees; a distinct proposed restriction concerns unidentified future noncriminal certiorari petitions. The underlying Ninth Circuit memorandum affirmed the failure-to-amend dismissals in Nos. 96-16222 and 96-16223. No plenary argument occurs and no underlying petition is decided by this order.

#### Participation

All nine Justices participate: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Public Action

The Court denies each present motion for leave to proceed in forma pauperis, meaning without prepaying fees. It declines to impose the proposed new fee-and-form condition on future noncriminal certiorari petitions. Justice Stevens, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, dissents from both current fee denials and joins the refusal of the future condition. These are financial-leave determinations, not grants or denials of certiorari.

#### Judgment & Remedy

Each current financial-leave motion is denied separately under Rule 39.8, 7–2. Supporting each denial: O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Opposing each denial: Stevens and Stone-Zsela. The Court declines the proposed future noncriminal filing restriction, 9–0. Supporting: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Arteaga may pay the applicable Rule 38 docketing fee and submit each petition in Rule 33.1 form within twenty-one days after issuance of this order. No new prospective noncriminal filing restriction issues. The order neither denies the underlying petitions nor directs automatic denial if that period later passes; any later disposition requires its own procedural basis.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to action | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion and order of the Court, Part I | Per curiam | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Denies each present fee motion separately | Particular-paper Rule 39.8 applications and paid-compliance opportunity |
| Opinion and order of the Court, Part II | Per curiam | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Declines the proposed prospective condition | Applicant-specific fit and inadequacy-of-individual-treatment ground |
| Dissent from Part I, joining Part II | Stevens | Stone-Zsela | Would grant both fee motions | Discretionary financial-access ground; no merits, default-excuse or certiorari ruling |

These are the published opinion joins; they disclose no vote on either underlying certiorari petition.

#### Holdings

##### I. Separate assessment of each present motion

**Holding and operative rule:** Rule 39.8 permits denial when the particular submission is frivolous or malicious, even assuming inability to prepay; earlier unsuccessful filings cannot substitute for that determination. On separate assessment of these papers, neither the lead submission nor the Wilson submission presents an arguable challenge connecting the alleged interference to the independent failure to comply with the 1996 amendment instruction; the Court exercises its discretion to deny each motion while preserving a paid-filing opportunity.

**Authority:** Part I of the per curiam opinion, expressly joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer: seven Justices. Stevens and Stone-Zsela would exercise the discretion differently.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court considers Arteaga's actual explanations. He sought an extension, alleges withheld papers and obstructed access, names both appeals, and later sought reopening. He was not silent about noncompliance. But the detailed nonwillful-default account concerns No. 92-15341, rather than an identified withheld item or event preventing compliance with the March–April 1996 amendment directions. Those directions required separate properly captioned complaints and extended the deadline after his competing-work explanation. The lower orders establish the nonamendment; absence of an archival item does not.

Wilson receives its own assessment. The July 21 memorandum identifies No. 96-16222 and C 94-3046, amendment opportunities, warnings and the Rule 41(b) ground. The Wilson cover, appeal reference, extension request, interference account and reopening materials do not supply the missing particular connection. The December reopening order shows what explanation was presented but does not automatically become an additional judgment under review.

Demos and Anderson preserve a low filing threshold where a specific interference claim connects to the reviewed obstacle. They do not make a generalized access allegation conclusive merely because the paper names the affected appeal. Nor does a lower-court loss itself establish frivolity. The Court assumes financial sufficiency: the sworn unemployment, income and asset statements are affirmative evidence, and uncertain caption linkage is not proof of wealth or filing default. The decision does not find the interference allegations false, make two daily library hours universally adequate, or decide certiorari or the underlying claims.

##### II. No future noncriminal fee-and-form condition

**Holding and operative rule:** A prospective restriction requires demonstrated applicant-specific abuse and a supported relation between that abuse, the future category restricted and inadequacy of individual treatment. The present record does not establish those predicates for the proposed fee-and-form condition on unidentified future noncriminal certiorari petitions, so the Court imposes none.

**Authority:** Part II of the per curiam opinion, joined by all nine Justices.

**Controlling explanation:** Martin, Day, Sassower, Anderson and Brown require a separate justification for future restraints.
