Thomas delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. All nine adopt the functional immunity rule and its application to both officials. No separate writing issues.

#### Holdings

##### Local legislative work receives absolute personal damages immunity

**Holding and operative rule:** Local officials performing genuine legislative functions have absolute immunity from personal damages under §1983 for those acts. The protection follows the function, so an executive official participating in that legislative process receives it for legislative work without immunity for all executive conduct.

**Authority:** The unanimous Opinion of the Court directly adopts the rule and the distinction between function and title.

**Controlling explanation:** Tenney protects legislative independence against damages litigation over legitimate legislative activity. Lake Country Estates establishes that the institutional justification does not disappear below the state legislature. Local scale likewise does not remove the need to deliberate and enact policy without personal damages exposure. Antoine requires historical and functional support rather than immunity based on importance or title; that support exists for the lawmaking process. Consumers Union recognizes that executive participation in legislative work can carry the corresponding immunity. The historical municipal-liability examples do not establish an exception for these particular lawmaking functions merely because municipal officials were defendants. The Court decides personal damages immunity for the acts before it, not municipal liability or every form of prospective relief.

##### This budget ordinance is legislative in substance and form

**Holding and operative rule:** Roderick’s committee work and vote, and Bogan’s introduction of the budget and signature on the abolition ordinance, are legislative acts here because they enact prospective policy concerning the city’s departments, offices and resources. The retaliatory-motive verdict does not transform those otherwise legislative functions into administrative acts or defeat absolute immunity.

**Authority:** All nine join the complete classification, motive rule and bounded reversal.

**Controlling explanation:** The budget anticipated reduced state aid, froze salaries and proposed eliminating 135 positions. The ordinance eliminated an entire department and its office prospectively, even though Scott-Harris was its sole employee. It therefore determined future structure and resource allocation as well as affecting the incumbent. Forrester’s individualized administrative personnel decision is materially different. The Court examines substance as well as formal enactment and does not decide whether formal legislative character alone would suffice in another case. Once this function is correctly classified, Tenney’s protection cannot depend on a jury’s assessment of motive. Fletcher likewise separates protected function from allegedly improper purpose; its personal-attestation exception illustrates that different acts remain independently classified. The jury’s rejection of racial discrimination and finding of retaliation are not reweighed. Immunity does not declare the acts constitutional. Separate administrative execution, personal discharge decisions outside this lawmaking function, ministerial enforcement and unclassified threats receive no automatic protection.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Tenney supplies protection of the legislative process and the rule that improper motive does not defeat immunity for legislative acts.
- Lake Country Estates supports function-based legislative immunity below the state-legislature level; the principle extends here to local lawmaking.
- Consumers Union supports equivalent protection for executive participation in the legislative function.
- Antoine preserves the obligation of the official claiming immunity to establish historical and functional justification; importance or office alone remains insufficient.
- Forrester’s administrative personnel action is distinguished from prospective abolition of a department and office.
- Fletcher preserves separate classification of actual functions and the motive/function distinction. Richardson’s different private-prison setting does not negate historically supported public legislative immunity. Clinton’s function-based analysis is consistent, but its distinct presidential constitutional rationale is unnecessary here.

#### Law After Decision

Absolute legislative immunity defeats the reviewed personal damages claims for these local legislative acts. The functional inquiry remains necessary, and the Court leaves formal legislative character alone undecided. The decision creates no general personnel immunity, municipal absolute immunity or defense for all administrative implementation.

#### Separate Writings



#### Procedure After Action

This Supreme Court review is complete. The lower courts must give effect to the officials’ absolute immunity on the reviewed legislative damages claims. No liability or proximate-cause ruling is needed, no additional evidentiary hearing is directed and no claim against a nonpetitioning defendant is restored.

#### Source Notes

The budget, ordinance, trial verdicts, post-verdict immunity ruling and appellate posture are reported in [523 U.S. 44](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-523/pdf/USREPORTS-523-44.pdf). The city-council vote was a municipal act, distinct from this Court’s vote. The jury’s retaliation finding is accepted for the immunity issue; it is not a new merits finding by this Court. The full trial record and original pleadings are not separately reproduced.

### 1998-03-03 — Lexecon Inc. v. Milberg Weiss Bershad Hynes & Lerach

[Public Projection](../records/lexecon_inc_v_milberg_weiss_bershad_hynes_lerach_merits_1998-03-03.md#public-projection).

#### Event

Lexecon Inc. v. Milberg Weiss Bershad Hynes & Lerach, No. 96-1482. Plenary merits decision, March 3, 1998; argued November 10, 1997. On writ of certiorari under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) to the Ninth Circuit. The question is whether an MDL transferee court may use 28 U.S.C. §1404(a) to assign a transferred action to itself for trial despite §1407(a)’s remand command. Lexecon’s Illinois diversity tort action was transferred to Arizona for coordinated pretrial proceedings. The Arizona court entered pretrial dispositions, denied requests to suggest remand and retained the defamation claim for a defense trial judgment; the Ninth Circuit sustained the self-transfer. Lexecon preserved the objection through remand requests, mandamus and direct appeal.

#### Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate. No Justice is recused.

#### Public Action

The Ninth Circuit’s judgment is reversed and the case remanded for enforcement of the statutory return obligation and treatment of properly remaining appellate issues.

#### Judgment & Remedy

**Reverse and remand, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The defamation trial judgment resulting from the preserved unlawful self-transfer must be set aside and the continuing action returned to the originating district through the proper §1407 process. The authorized pretrial summary judgments, including the distinct Cotchett Rule 54(b) judgment, are not automatically void; their properly remaining appellate issues retain their own posture. Stone-Zsela agrees with the ancillary dispositions described in Part III without adding a ground.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Reverses and remands | All eight Associate Justices join Parts I–III; Stone-Zsela joins Parts I–II on statutory return and the trial-forum remedy, and agrees only with Part III’s ancillary dispositions |

The opinion rests on text, structure and the preserved objection. It adds no legislative-history rationale. No separate writing issues.

#### Holdings

##### I. The MDL transferee cannot defeat mandatory return through self-transfer

**Holding and operative rule:** Unless the transferred action has previously terminated, §1407(a) requires the Panel to remand it to the original district at or before completion of coordinated pretrial proceedings. Section 1404(a) does not authorize the transferee court to retain that continuing action for trial by transferring it from itself to itself.

**Authority:** Part I is joined by all nine Justices and supplies direct statutory authority.

**Controlling explanation:** Section 1407 authorizes coordination for pretrial proceedings and commands return of each continuing action. The Panel may separate and remand particular claims earlier; an action that has actually terminated needs no return. Changing the asserted venue basis does not terminate the action. Addressing the command to the Panel does not authorize the transferee court to prevent its performance. Section 1404(a)’s general permission to transfer to another eligible district or division coexists with, rather than defeats, this specific temporary allocation. Section 1407(f) permits only rules consistent with Acts of Congress, so the cited self-assignment practice cannot alter the return obligation. Subsection (h)’s specific Clayton Act trial-transfer authorization does not confer that power in this tort action. The investment in coordinated pretrial work is substantial, but efficiency cannot delete the enacted sequence. A later lawful transfer by the proper court and consent or waiver questions not presented remain open.

##### II. The preserved trial-forum error requires correction of this judgment

**Holding and operative rule:** A party that preserved its §1407 return objection need not prove that a different jury would have reached a different verdict to obtain correction of the resulting unauthorized trial forum. This uncured violation requires setting aside the defamation trial judgment and enforcing statutory remand.

**Authority:** Part II is joined by all nine Justices; its remedy follows the mandatory return right asserted here.

**Controlling explanation:** Lexecon repeatedly requested remand, sought mandamus and then pursued the promised direct appeal. The possibility that Arizona could originally have been a proper venue does not supply the procedure Congress required after an MDL transfer. Caterpillar preserves a completed judgment when the jurisdictional defect actually disappeared before trial; the prohibited retention for this trial never received an equivalent cure. Good Real Property rejects inventing dismissal of a substantive action as a sanction for administrative delay. This case instead directly enforces a particular allocation of the trial forum without extinguishing the cause of action. No constitutional or subject-matter-jurisdiction label is necessary, and the Court adopts no universal rule that every procedural error defeats a completed judgment. The statutory return must occur before any independently lawful later transfer; no permanent right against such a transfer is adjudicated.

##### III. Authorized pretrial work and unreviewed tort issues remain separate

**Holding and operative rule:** The improper self-transfer does not automatically invalidate decisions made within authorized pretrial proceedings or decide the merits of the underlying tort claims. Properly remaining appellate challenges must be addressed in their own posture; the unpreserved request for prospective application does not alter this disposition.

**Authority:** Part III is joined by all eight Associate Justices. Stone-Zsela agrees with these dispositions without joining an additional rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** The malicious-prosecution and abuse-of-process rulings and the Cotchett Rule 54(b) judgment are pretrial dispositions, not the defamation trial verdict. Lexecon disputed the Rule 54(b) exercise of discretion without denying power to enter an authorized pretrial judgment. Those distinctions prevent wholesale reinstatement of every claim merely because trial retention was unlawful. The argument that the defamation claim should independently have been dismissed before trial remains for proper consideration, not a new factual or tort-law ruling here. The prospectivity request was not presented below or in opposition to review and supplies no basis for a different result in this case. No comprehensive retroactivity doctrine, new waiver finding or adjudication of a later transfer is adopted.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Caterpillar’s completed-judgment protection requires an actual pretrial cure; the preserved trial-forum defect here is distinguished as uncured. Its statutory remand duty remains mandatory.
- Good Real Property preserves mandatory administrative duties while rejecting an unstated forfeiture-dismissal sanction; direct enforcement of the specified trial allocation is different.
- City of Chicago retains the distinction between statutory power and convenience or discretionary retention; it supplies no authority to enlarge §1407’s temporary pretrial grant.

#### Law After Decision

Section 1407’s return command cannot be displaced by a transferee’s §1404 self-transfer. Authorized pretrial adjudication remains distinct from unauthorized trial retention, and a preserved objection secures the statutory forum remedy without independent proof that the verdict would differ. No general subject-matter-jurisdiction invalidity, bar on consensual arrangements or decision about a later lawful transfer follows.

#### Separate Writings



#### Procedure After Action

The case returns for proceedings consistent with mandatory remand to the Northern District of Illinois and correction of the defamation trial judgment. The proper courts must keep the distinct pretrial and remaining appellate issues separate. The Court decides no tort liability, automatic new verdict or permanent prohibition on lawful later transfer. This Supreme Court review is complete.

#### Source Notes

The transfer, preservation history, trial and distinct pretrial judgments are reported in [523 U.S. 26](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-523/pdf/USREPORTS-523-26.pdf). Sections 1404 and 1407 appear in the official 1997 United States Code. The decision uses the enacted statutory text and the reported contemporary rule argument; later Panel rule revisions supply no premise. The original motions, trial record and complete briefs are not separately reproduced, and no additional waiver or trial error is inferred.

### 1998-03-03 — Spencer v. Kemna

[Public Projection](../records/spencer_v_kemna_mootness_1998-03-03.md#public-projection).

#### Event

Spencer v. Kemna, No. 96-7171. Decision after plenary review, March 3, 1998; argued November 12, 1997. On writ of certiorari under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) to the Eighth Circuit, which affirmed dismissal of the §2254 petition. Spencer challenges the September 1992 parole revocation, not the original burglary and stealing convictions. He filed on April 1, 1993 while imprisoned under the revocation; the challenged sentence expired October 16, 1993. Review concerns statutory custody, continuing Article III injury, mootness exceptions and the asserted effect of possible civil-remedy limits.

#### Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate. No Justice is recused.

#### Public Action

The Eighth Circuit’s judgment affirming dismissal is affirmed on mootness alone.

#### Judgment & Remedy

**Affirm dismissal as moot, 8–1.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.
**Opposing:** Stevens.

The dismissal rests on absence of a continuing Article III controversy, not failure of custody when the petition was filed or a decision on the revocation’s merits. Stevens would reverse and remand the live petition for its remaining lawful adjudication. Stone-Zsela agrees with the ancillary custody-at-filing, capable-of-repetition exception, litigation-delay and civil-remedy dispositions without adding a ground.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg | Affirms mootness dismissal | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer join all parts; Stone-Zsela joins Part II’s actual-continuing-injury ground and mootness result, but agrees only with Part II’s capable-of-repetition and litigation-delay dispositions and Parts I and III’s ancillary dispositions, without those additional rationales |
| Dissent | Stevens | — | Would reverse and remand | Recognizes the particular official adverse adjudication’s continuing reputational consequence; does not decide the revocation merits or an affirmative section 1983 exception |

#### Holdings

##### I. Custody at filing and a continuing controversy are separate requirements

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 2254’s custody requirement is satisfied when the petitioner is held under the challenged revocation at filing; later expiration does not retroactively defeat that threshold. Article III nonetheless requires a concrete continuing injury after the challenged sentence expires.

**Authority:** Part I is joined by O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer, seven direct votes. Stone-Zsela agrees with the custody disposition without an additional ground; Stevens also accepts custody at filing.

**Controlling explanation:** Spencer was imprisoned under this revocation on April 1, 1993. The District Court’s alternative suggestion that expiration removed statutory custody is incorrect. The later seven-year imprisonment arises from an unrelated conviction, rather than the unexpired consecutive aggregate addressed in Garlotte. A timely filing therefore satisfies the statutory entry condition without itself supplying an enduring constitutional controversy. Lujan and Arizonans require an actual personal interest throughout adjudication, not simply an interest that existed when suit began.

##### II. The asserted collateral consequences and exceptions do not keep this petition live

**Holding and operative rule:** After this parole-revocation sentence expires, collateral injury is not presumed merely from the revocation; the petitioner must show an actual continuing consequence redressable in this action. Speculative future parole, sentencing or evidentiary uses, the possibility of recurrence and litigation delay do not supply that showing on this record.

**Authority:** Part II’s continuing-injury rule and mootness result have eight direct joins: Stone-Zsela and the seven Associates joining the Court opinion. The seven Associates also directly adopt the specific exception and delay applications.

**Controlling explanation:** An attack on a conviction is distinct from this challenge to a revocation of an otherwise valid sentence. Lane supports examining the actual surviving interest, although Spencer, unlike Lane’s respondents, challenges the adverse revocation finding itself. Future parole use depends on discretionary treatment, sentencing use on later legal developments, impeachment on future testimony and permissible use, and other-crimes evidence on additional contingencies. No present mandatory disability or demonstrated imminent adverse use is established. MacArthur’s actual statutory information interest and existing damages action do not make every retained adverse record a live habeas controversy; the Court adopts no categorical exclusion of reputational injury. The capable-of-repetition exception requires both a duration too short for full review and a reasonable expectation that this claimant will again face the action. Neither the asserted delay nor possible injury to others establishes those conditions. State extensions and delayed judicial action cannot create Article III power through fault or estoppel. Expiration occurred by the sentence’s terms, not a newly established strategic cessation. The Court decides no revocation claim, criminal guilt or exoneration.

##### III. An unresolved future civil remedy does not create this habeas controversy

**Holding and operative rule:** Heck and Edwards do not establish an unavoidable favorable-invalidation bar for every former prisoner: their controlling rules expressly reserve that category and persons without a realistic lawful custody-review opportunity. The unresolved scope of a possible later civil action does not create a damages claim in this habeas case or dispense with continuing Article III injury.

**Authority:** Part III is joined by O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer, seven direct votes. Stone-Zsela agrees with the disposition of this ancillary contention without adding a ground.

**Controlling explanation:** Heck’s decided prerequisite concerns the specified in-custody, conviction-negating damages claim; Edwards preserves that limit while requiring genuinely independent injuries to be classified by their consequences. Neither decision supplies the universal former-prisoner bar assumed by Spencer’s argument. A claim whose success does not necessarily invalidate the revocation would also require its own analysis. The Court need not choose an affirmative former-prisoner exception to reject the asserted inevitable bar. No damages claim is pleaded here, and possible difficulty obtaining another remedy does not empower the Court to decide an otherwise moot habeas petition. The existing reservations remain open rather than becoming either a categorical bar or a newly adjudicated exception.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Lane is applied for the continuing-interest inquiry while its unchallenged-revocation posture is distinguished; no automatic consequence is inferred for this revocation.
- Lujan and Arizonans preserve an actual personal controversy throughout review. Arizonans’ vacatur remedy does not require clearing a nonexistent intervening merits judgment here.
- Garlotte’s continuous consecutive-sentence aggregate is distinguished from Spencer’s unrelated later custody; custody at filing remains sufficient for the statutory threshold.
- MacArthur preserves supported actual personal information interests without creating a universal reputation-based habeas rule.
- Heck and Edwards retain their bounded invalidation prerequisite and express former-prisoner/realistic-opportunity reservations; no affirmative section 1983 exception or universal bar is added.

#### Law After Decision

A timely custody-based habeas filing does not survive expiration of the challenged sentence without continuing Article III injury or a valid exception. This record establishes neither. The revocation’s merits remain unadjudicated, and the separate civil-remedy questions expressly reserved by Heck and Edwards remain open.

#### Separate Writings

**Stevens, dissenting.** Stevens distinguishes Lane because Spencer challenges the official adverse revocation finding itself, not merely completed custody while leaving that finding untouched. He considers the particular government adjudication and the State’s continuing reliance on the incorporated serious-misconduct report a present personal reputational consequence that invalidation could meaningfully remove. He does not presume every revocation carries statutory disabilities or depend on a future crime, audience reaction or invented damages claim. The written order found condition violations and incorporated a report; it was not an express rape conviction, and the State’s characterization remains distinct. MacArthur’s requirement of a supported personal interest does not, in his view, erase this already imposed official determination. He would remand for unresolved lawful adjudication, without granting a writ, finding procedural wrongdoing or deciding the civil-remedy question.

#### Procedure After Action

The habeas action remains dismissed as moot, without constitutional-merits preclusion. No writ, new revocation hearing, expungement, damages or exoneration is ordered. This Supreme Court review is complete, and no continuing return condition is created.

#### Source Notes

The revocation order, incorporated report, custody dates, claimed procedural defects and later separate sentence are reported in [523 U.S. 1](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-523/pdf/USREPORTS-523-1.pdf). The woman’s allegations, Spencer’s admitted cocaine use and denial of force, the written condition-violation order and the State’s description remain distinct. The underlying reports and complete record are available here through published recitations; they establish no criminal conviction for the alleged conduct. No reputation-only submission or damages demand by Spencer is attributed to him. [Lane, 455 U.S. 624](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-455/pdf/USREPORTS-455-624.pdf), supplies the earlier posture and continuing-interest analysis.

### Chunk-5 public law and actions

Each copied decision is effective only on its own date. Independent same-date events share their entering-law baseline. The following text is copied from each Record's bounded Public Projection; dated earlier excerpts above retain their original temporal perspective.

### Kawaauhau v. Geiger, No. 97-115. — 1998-03-03

#### Event

Kawaauhau v. Geiger, No. 97-115. Plenary merits decision, March 3, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 5. Argued January 21, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit, sitting en banc. The question is whether a malpractice debt arising from negligent or reckless medical treatment is for willful and malicious injury under 11 U.S.C. §523(a)(6). The Bankruptcy Court held the debt nondischargeable, the District Court affirmed, and the Eighth Circuit reversed. The creditors seek reversal of that appellate judgment.

#### Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate. No Justice is recused.

#### Public Action

The judgment of the Eighth Circuit is affirmed.

#### Judgment & Remedy

Affirmed, 9–0. Supporting: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. The malpractice debt remains dischargeable under §523(a)(6). The Court does not revisit the jury’s malpractice finding or damages calculation; it decides dischargeability under §523(a)(6). No new intent finding, remand for relitigation of malpractice or decision under another exception is ordered.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer | Affirms | The complete injury-intent holding, application, precedent treatment and limits |

No separate writing is filed.

#### Holdings

### Deliberate injury is required; a deliberate act causing negligent or reckless harm is insufficient

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 523(a)(6) requires a deliberate or intentional injury, in addition to the separately stated malicious character; intentionally doing an act that negligently or recklessly causes injury does not satisfy willfulness. This malpractice debt, established on negligence or recklessness rather than injury-directed intent, is therefore dischargeable under that paragraph.

**Authority:** Ginsburg’s opinion of the Court, joined in full by all eight other Justices identified in Opinion Topology, directly controls the rule, application and stated limits.

**Controlling explanation:** The statutory modifier describes injury. Moving it to the treatment decision would make many voluntary acts producing negligent harm sufficient and deprive the injury requirement of independent work. The medical choices here were deliberate, but that does not establish deliberate injury. The exceptionally deficient standard of care found below establishes malpractice; calling it willful and malicious does not answer the federal question.

Tinker involved an intentional invasion of a legally protected right, and McIntyre involved deliberate unauthorized appropriation of property. Neither requires personal hostility, but neither makes a voluntary act alone enough. Tinker itself distinguished negligent driving from intentionally running over a victim. To the extent its broader language is read to include merely reckless injury under the present statute, that reading is rejected. Davis confirms that even conversion liability does not automatically establish willful and malicious injury. Field likewise keeps a federal discharge predicate distinct from a state tort label.

The creditors’ cumulative account of inferior treatment, canceled transfer and discontinued antibiotics establishes the gravity of the alleged wrong, but supplies no additional finding of intended injury. Nor does uninsured practice authorize a judicially created exception. Congress fixes the exceptions. The Court reserves whether subjective knowledge of substantial certainty suffices, the independent meaning of malicious, any other independently pleaded discharge exception and the treatment of proved injury-directed harm. No categorical exemption for medical debts or new criminal-law proof requirement follows.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Tinker v. Colwell, 193 U.S. 473, retains its intentional-invasion holding and rejection of a personal-hostility requirement; a reading that makes merely reckless injury sufficient under §523(a)(6) is rejected.
- McIntyre v. Kavanaugh, 242 U.S. 138, is distinguished as deliberate appropriation; it does not make all conversions or negligent professional acts nondischargeable, and its old-Act imputation question is not decided.
- Davis v. Aetna Acceptance Co., 293 U.S. 328, is applied to preserve the distinction between tort liability and willful and malicious injury.
- Field v. Mans is applied for the independent federal discharge predicates; its actual-fraud reliance rule is unchanged. In re Cohen’s debt-extent rule remains distinct because a qualifying injury predicate must first exist here.

#### Law After Decision

From March 3, 1998, §523(a)(6) excludes debts for negligently or recklessly inflicted injury from its willfulness category even when the underlying act was intentional. Actual deliberate injury presents a different question. The independent malicious requirement, substantial certainty, other discharge exceptions and state-law liability retain their separate roles.

#### Separate Writings

No separate writing is filed.

#### Procedure After Action

The Eighth Circuit’s discharge determination under §523(a)(6) stands. This review is complete; no additional hearing, intent finding or reconsideration of malpractice is directed.

#### Source Notes

The [reported proceeding, 523 U.S. 57](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-523/pdf/USREPORTS-523-57.pdf), supplies the treatment history, malpractice and bankruptcy sequence, argument date and appellate posture. The [1994 Code text of §523](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-1994-title11/pdf/USCODE-1994-title11-chap5-subchapII-sec523.pdf) preserves the pertinent paragraph. The published recitations establish no subjective-substantial-certainty finding or state requirement that Geiger carry insurance.

### United States v. Ramirez, No. 96-1469. — 1998-03-04

#### Event

United States v. Ramirez, No. 96-1469. Plenary merits decision, March 4, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 5. Argued January 13, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit. The United States challenges the affirmance of an order suppressing firearms evidence under the Fourth Amendment and 18 U.S.C. §3109 because officers broke a garage window while executing a no-knock warrant. The questions concern the announcement standard when property is damaged, the reasonableness of that damage and the separate statutory ground.

#### Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate. No Justice is recused.

#### Public Action

The judgment of the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for further proceedings consistent with the Court’s opinion.

#### Judgment & Remedy

Reversed and remanded, 9–0. Supporting: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. The suppression judgment cannot stand on a heightened announcement standard triggered by damage, or on the challenged entry and single-window damage under the standards applied here. This decision orders no acquittal, conviction or sentence and establishes no automatic exclusionary exception for later statements, a later warrant or recovered evidence.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg | Reverses and remands | All constitutional and statutory holdings, the bounded application and remedy |

No separate writing is filed.

#### Holdings

**Common authority:** Breyer’s opinion of the Court is joined in full by all eight other Justices identified in Opinion Topology. That direct unanimous coalition controls each holding and its limits; no combination of separate rationales is necessary.

### I. Property damage does not raise the announcement threshold

**Holding and operative rule:** Damage during warrant execution does not categorically require a greater exigency than particularized reasonable suspicion that ordinary announcement would be dangerous or futile, or would inhibit effective investigation, including through destruction of evidence. The facts supporting that exception must be known when the challenged force begins, and the means and extent of any destruction remain independently subject to Fourth Amendment reasonableness.

**Controlling explanation:** Wilson makes the common-law announcement protection part of constitutional reasonableness while preserving supported exceptions. Richards requires particular facts and rational inferences rather than an offense category, unsupported hunch or a no-knock label on a warrant. Breaking property does not change that announcement inquiry into a separate higher tier. The contrary rule correctly recognizes that destruction aggravates an intrusion, but addresses that concern in the wrong place. Whether the particular destructive act is reasonable remains a separate question; justified surprise does not authorize needless or disproportionate damage. The Court adopts no universal waiting period and revisits no disputed standard-of-review rule.

### II. The contemporaneous danger and limited damage justified this execution

**Holding and operative rule:** The reliable report of a dangerous escapee, corroborating observation and location-specific information about garage weapons supplied reasonable suspicion that ordinary announcement would create danger. Breaking one garage window to control access to those weapons, with a firearm directed through the opening for that purpose, was reasonable on this record; the justification permits neither gratuitous destruction nor any amount of damage merely because announcement may be excused.

**Controlling explanation:** Richards requires circumstances known before force. The informant’s report was partly corroborated by the agent’s observation, and the reported weapons gave the officers a concrete reason to prevent a rush into the garage. The limited window break addressed that identified danger. The number of officers outside did not eliminate a possible armed person’s access to weapons inside. Their security purpose is supported by those facts; purpose alone would not suffice. Ramirez’s later shot supplies no retrospective justification, and the later failure to find Shelby does not erase the information reasonably available beforehand. The Court decides the challenged entry and window damage, not every subsequent act. Gratuitous, punitive, disproportionate or objective-unrelated destruction receives no approval, and the remedy for materially different damage remains open.

### III. Section 3109 preserves a supported common-law danger exception

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 3109 ordinarily permits breaking to execute a search warrant after notice of authority and purpose and refused admission, and separately permits breaking when necessary to liberate the officer or someone assisting execution. Its codification of the common-law announcement rule also preserves a danger exception satisfied by particularized reasonable suspicion that following the ordinary process would expose officers or occupants to danger; the pre-entry escapee and garage-weapons information satisfies that exception here.

**Controlling explanation:** Simultaneous announcement and breaking do not establish prior refused admission. Nor does this record establish an officer or assistant needing liberation. The entry instead rests on the incorporated common-law danger qualification. Miller identifies the common-law source of the statutory protection but reserved a general exigency question. Sabbath treats opening an unlocked door as breaking and, in its discussion of common-law exceptions, supplies direct interpretive support without having held that every asserted danger suffices. Indeed, its unsupported danger claim failed. The Court now adopts the danger construction for the concrete supported circumstances here; it does not attribute that holding to those earlier reservations.

The statutory protection carries its traditional safety qualification, rather than automatically acquiring every constitutional exception. Wilson’s common-law account and Richards’s particularized standard guide this bounded construction. The express liberation alternative concerns freeing an impeded officer or helper and remains distinct. Because breaking already includes entry without physical damage, the statute creates no greater announcement threshold merely because property is damaged. No statutory rule for every possible futility, escape or evidence-destruction situation is necessary. Both asserted grounds for suppression fail, so the Court need not decide attenuation, independent source, inevitable discovery or the exclusionary consequence of an established violation.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Wilson v. Arkansas is applied for the common-law announcement protection and supported exceptions; it does not constitutionalize every federal statutory detail.
- Richards v. Wisconsin is applied for particularized reasonable suspicion and contemporaneous justification; its announcement quantum is not increased because damage occurs, and its separate review-method holding is unchanged.
- Miller v. United States, 357 U.S. 301, supplies the statutory common-law background. Its reservation of a general exigency exception is respected rather than described as an earlier holding.
- Sabbath v. United States, 391 U.S. 585, retains its rule that opening a closed unlocked door is breaking and its rejection of unsupported danger. Its common-law discussion supports the danger qualification adopted here; physical damage creates no new announcement tier.
- Aponte Matos v. Toledo Dávila retains its separate conduct-date qualified-immunity scope and supplies no direct suppression immunity here.

#### Law After Decision

The constitutional announcement inquiry retains Richards’s particularized reasonable-suspicion standard when execution damages property, while the destructive means receive independent reasonableness review. Section 3109 separately incorporates the supported common-law danger exception applied here. Neither the validity of a warrant nor the availability of an announcement exception authorizes unreasonable damage. No suppression-causation doctrine or rule for materially different destruction is resolved.

#### Separate Writings

No separate writing is filed.

#### Procedure After Action

The case returns for further proceedings under the determination that the challenged entry and limited window damage violated neither the Fourth Amendment nor §3109. Those asserted grounds cannot sustain the suppression judgment. Any genuinely separate and properly preserved question retains its own requirements; no such claim is invented and no particular prosecution result is ordered.

#### Source Notes

The [reported proceeding, 523 U.S. 65](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-523/pdf/USREPORTS-523-65.pdf), supplies the argument date, entry facts, lower-court suppression posture and later statements and warrant. The [official text of §3109](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-1994-title18/pdf/USCODE-1994-title18-partII-chap205-sec3109.pdf) supplies its notice, refusal and liberation terms. The factual account rests on published recitations; the Court draws no additional causal finding from unproduced testimony. Miller and Sabbath remain relevant at the limits stated in the opinion.

### Glendora v. Porzio, No. 97-7300. — 1998-03-09

#### Event

Glendora v. Porzio, No. 97-7300. Filing-administration order, March 9, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 5. The full Court considers the present Rule 39 financial-leave motion and a separate proposed fee-and-form restriction on future noncriminal certiorari petitions. The petition seeks review after the Second Circuit declined financial leave in the appeal from dismissal of Glendora's federal action. No oral argument occurs and the underlying certiorari petition is not decided.

#### Participation

All nine Justices participate: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. No recusal is stated.

#### Public Action

The present motion for leave to proceed in forma pauperis, meaning without prepaying the docketing fee, is denied. The proposed prospective fee-and-form restriction on future noncriminal certiorari petitions is declined. Stevens, joined by Stone-Zsela, dissents from the current denial and joins the refusal of prospective control. No grant or denial of certiorari follows.

#### Judgment & Remedy

| Component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Remedy |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Present financial-leave motion | Denied, 7–2 | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Stevens and Stone-Zsela | Glendora may pay the Rule 38 fee and submit the petition in Rule 33.1 form by March 30, 1998 |
| Proposed future noncriminal fee-and-form condition | Declined, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | — | No new prospective restriction |

This order neither decides the underlying petition nor directs automatic denial if the compliance date passes. It does not reverse or reopen any lower judgment, award damages, remand the civil action, release anyone, or appoint counsel.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to action | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion and order of the Court, Part I | Per curiam | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Denies present financial leave | Particular-paper assessment and paid-compliance opportunity |
| Opinion and order of the Court, Part II | Per curiam | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Declines future restriction | Separate fit and inadequacy-of-individual-treatment ground |
| Dissent from Part I, joining Part II | Stevens | Stone-Zsela | Would grant present financial leave | Discretionary access for ordinary petition screening |

These published joins disclose no vote on the underlying certiorari petition.

#### Holdings

### I. The present fee motion

**Holding and operative rule:** Rule 39.8 permits, but does not require, denial of financial leave when the particular submission is frivolous or malicious, even if the applicant cannot prepay. The Court exercises that discretion here because the present petition supplies no arguable route through the independent state-action, conspiracy and judicial-immunity obstacles to the federal action; it allows payment of the Rule 38 fee and submission in Rule 33.1 form by March 30, 1998, without deciding certiorari.

**Authority:** Part I of the per curiam opinion, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; seven Justices adopt this rule, application and limited filing opportunity. Stevens and Stone-Zsela would grant the motion as a permissible discretionary choice.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court examines the grounds Glendora actually presents. She alleges false service, lack of personal jurisdiction and denial of a hearing to challenge service, and contests the appellate financial warning and clerk-signed order. Those allegations are not rejected as false. They nevertheless supply no developed arguable challenge connecting the service dispute to the independent immunity, state-action and conspiracy grounds defeating this federal action. A clerk's signature and the challenged panel composition do not themselves establish a federal defect. The Court does not confuse its own appellate jurisdiction with the District Court's inability to review a state judgment.

Martin requires particular-paper assessment and preserves a discretionary denial where an independent obstacle receives no arguable answer. Demos and Anderson permit an arguable access allegation to clear the fee threshold without proving entitlement to relief, but do not make the allegation's label conclusive. Arteaga applies the same distinction between considering an actual procedural explanation and finding an arguable route through the operative obstacle. The lower court's characterization, financial warning and unsuccessful filing history do not substitute for this examination. Financial denial is not a finding of wealth, dishonesty or default, and it decides neither the alleged misconduct nor any claim's ultimate merits. Payment and proper form permit ordinary processing, not a promised grant of review.

### II. The proposed restriction on future noncriminal petitions

**Holding and operative rule:** A prospective fee-and-form restriction requires demonstrated applicant-specific abuse, a supported fit to the future category restricted, and support for the inadequacy of individual-paper treatment. The present record does not establish those predicates for Glendora's unidentified future noncriminal certiorari petitions, so the Court imposes no new restriction.

**Authority:** Part II of the per curiam opinion, joined by all nine Justices: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

**Controlling explanation:** Martin, Day, Sassower, Anderson, Brown and Arteaga require an independent justification for future restraints. The present petition's deficiencies and the lower-court warning do not demonstrate repeated applicant-specific abuse fitted to all future noncriminal petitions, or why individual treatment is inadequate. A count of unsuccessful filings cannot establish the content of unidentified future papers. Keeping paid and criminal routes available would narrow the proposed condition but would not supply the missing justification. This refusal creates no numerical threshold, universal prior-warning requirement, duty to exhaust every conceivable lesser measure, or categorical prohibition on adequately supported restrictions. Targeted controls remain available on their own demonstrated predicates.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Zatko preserves discretionary denial on a supported current-paper record and a paid-compliance opportunity; its limited remedy does not decide certiorari or justify every future restraint.
- Martin is applied to separate current financial leave from prospective control; it permits particular-paper denial without commanding denial whenever relief is doubtful.
- Demos and Anderson are distinguished on whether the concrete access allegation supplies an arguable route through the independent obstacle; their low financial-access threshold remains intact.
- Day and Sassower preserve the need to justify the particular future category on its own record.
- Brown and Arteaga are applied without change: current allegations receive individual examination and future restrictions require independent fit and inadequacy support.
- McDonald and Sindram retain their targeted extraordinary-writ authority; they do not automatically authorize this new noncriminal-certiorari condition.

#### Law After Decision

Existing Rule 39 doctrine is applied. Supported present denials remain discretionary; financial permission, certiorari and underlying relief remain separate. No new future filing category is restricted, and supported targeted controls remain available under their own requirements.

#### Separate Writings

**Stevens, joined by Stone-Zsela, dissenting from Part I and joining Part II.** The dissent would accept the present papers under the financial-leave rules for ordinary petition screening. The concrete service and hearing allegations warrant that discretionary course despite the serious independent obstacles. Alleged false service and judicial acceptance do not themselves establish state action by private counsel, conspiracy, or an available collateral challenge to the state judgment. Martin permits supported present denials but does not compel denial in every difficult case; the dissent's preferred exercise of discretion does not displace the Court's rule. Granting financial leave would neither excuse a jurisdictional defect nor revive the Second Circuit appeal, establish the allegations, grant certiorari, appoint counsel, or award relief. The dissent joins the separate refusal of prospective controls because present deficiencies and filing volume do not establish inadequacy of individual treatment.

#### Procedure After Action

The fee motion is disposed of and the underlying certiorari petition remains at the post-fee stage. Payment and Rule 33.1 compliance by March 30, 1998 permit ordinary processing under the Court's jurisdictional and review requirements. Any later disposition needs its own procedural basis; the date alone supplies no automatic denial.

#### Source Notes

The [current petition, financial papers and appended lower orders](https://archive.org/details/micro_IA40385014_0200) supply the service, hearing, warning and order-form content. False service and judicial misconduct remain allegations. The lower dismissal rests on independent immunity, state-action, conspiracy and jurisdictional grounds; its characterization does not establish this Court's finding without the individual examination stated above. Rules 39.8, 38 and 33.1 govern the present order. The [reported proceeding, 523 U.S. 206](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-523/pdf/USREPORTS-523-206.pdf), supplies the docket and procedural setting.

### Gray v. Maryland, No. 96-8653. — 1998-03-09

#### Event

Gray v. Maryland, No. 96-8653. Merits decision, March 9, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 5. Argued December 8, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Maryland Court of Appeals, which reinstated Gray's conviction after the intermediate court set it aside. The question is whether a nontestifying codefendant's confession remains within Bruton when Gray's name is replaced by conspicuous blanks or spoken deletions. Gray seeks relief from the conviction; harmless error remains a distinct question.

#### Participation

All nine Justices participate at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. No recusal is stated.

#### Public Action

The Maryland Court of Appeals' judgment is vacated and the case remanded. The Court holds the challenged redaction unconstitutional without deciding harmlessness.

#### Judgment & Remedy

**Vacated and remanded, 6–3.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.
**Opposing:** Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas.

The state court receives the case for proceedings consistent with the confrontation holding, with any properly available Chapman harmless-error issue preserved. The Court makes no harmlessness finding and orders neither automatic retrial nor acquittal. Stone-Zsela agrees with vacatur and remand but would expressly direct the state court to determine harmless error.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Direct-accusation redaction rule and application |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II | O'Connor | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Preservation-qualified Chapman review and absence of an automatic retrial or acquittal |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stone-Zsela | — | Vacate and remand; would direct determination of harmless error | Joins Part I; states immediate-context application and a distinct affirmative remand instruction |
| Dissent | Scalia | Kennedy and Thomas | Would affirm on the preserved redaction question | Richardson facial-versus-inferential line, limiting instruction and separate preservation of prosecutorial-use questions |

#### Holdings

### I. Obvious deletions that retain a direct accusation

**Holding and operative rule:** When a nontestifying codefendant's confession replaces a defendant's name with a conspicuous blank, spoken deletion, symbol or equivalent substitution that directly identifies the defendant from the statement and its immediate trial context, admitting that confession at their joint trial with only an instruction to consider it against the declarant violates the other defendant's right of confrontation under Bruton. Richardson continues to govern a confession neutral on its face that becomes incriminating only through later evidentiary linkage; this decision does not condemn every neutral pronoun, redaction or joint trial.

**Authority:** Part I of O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; six Justices adopt the rule and this application. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas dissent. This is direct majority authority, not a combination of unlike rationales under Marks.

**Controlling explanation:** Bruton recognizes that a powerful direct accusation by an unavailable codefendant cannot reliably be confined to the declarant by an instruction. Richardson upheld a different kind of statement: the redaction removed every reference to the other defendant's existence, and only other evidence later supplied the incriminating link. Its reservation concerning symbols and substitute words leaves this question open.

Here the spoken deletions and written blanks leave the accusation's grammar and obvious missing identities in place. At this joint trial they invite the jury immediately to supply the other accused's name. Two omissions and testimony describing additional participants do not convert that transparent accusation into Richardson's otherwise neutral narrative. The distinction concerns the kind of inference, not whether any inference is involved. The limiting instruction therefore cannot cure the preserved confrontation violation.

Zafiro preserves a specific trial right even when instructions ordinarily provide adequate joint-trial protection; the benefits of joinder do not answer whether this particular accusation can be admitted. The holding rests on the challenged redaction itself. The prosecutor's follow-up question connecting Bell's information with Gray's arrest is not adjudicated as a separate constitutional wrong, and no separate preservation requirement is bypassed. The Court does not prescribe substantive rewriting of confessions or decide the validity of every neutral substitute, an independently admissible statement, or testimony subjected to cross-examination.

### II. Constitutional error and the remand remain separate

**Holding and operative rule:** The Maryland judgment reinstating Gray's conviction is vacated and the case remanded for proceedings consistent with the confrontation holding. Any properly available harmless-error contention requires the State to establish beyond a reasonable doubt under Chapman that the constitutional error did not contribute to the verdict; this Court makes no harmlessness finding and orders neither an unconditional new trial nor acquittal.

**Authority:** Part II of O'Connor's opinion, joined by Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; five Justices adopt the preservation-qualified remand. Stone-Zsela supplies the sixth vote to vacate and remand, but would affirmatively direct the state court to determine harmless error and does not join Part II's narrower instruction.

**Controlling explanation:** Establishing an admission error does not itself establish the remedy required on the whole trial record. Chapman supplies the State's demanding harmlessness burden for constitutional trial error. Williamson preserves the distinction between determining an evidentiary or constitutional wrong and deciding its consequences, without substituting the standard for nonconstitutional error. The present materials do not permit this Court to find the confrontation error harmless. Further proceedings must respect the holding and any properly available error review; a materially different redaction or lawful trial-management alternative presents its own question. No conviction is reinstated by the possibility of harmlessness, and the State receives no favorable finding on an unexamined record.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Bruton v. United States, 391 U.S. 123, is applied to obvious name deletions retaining a direct accusation; its limiting-instruction exception remains bounded to that confrontation risk.
- Richardson v. Marsh, 481 U.S. 200, is distinguished because its redaction removed reference to the defendant's existence; its rule for neutral statements requiring later evidentiary linkage remains effective.
- Zafiro v. United States, 506 U.S. 534, preserves specific confrontation protection alongside discretionary joint-trial management; it supplies no universal instruction cure.
- Chapman v. California, 386 U.S. 18, supplies the State's beyond-a-reasonable-doubt harmlessness burden for constitutional trial error, without an application finding here.
- Williamson v. United States, 512 U.S. 594, preserves separate analysis of error and remedy; its assertion-specific evidentiary holding and different nonconstitutional harmlessness standard are not displaced.
- White v. Illinois, 502 U.S. 346, retains its exception-specific confrontation rules; no new categorical testimonial definition or exception for an accusatory police confession is adopted.

#### Law After Decision

An obvious name deletion that retains a direct accusation falls within Bruton. Richardson's neutral-statement rule remains available, and no general prohibition on pronouns, lawful redaction, joint trials or cross-examined testimony follows. Preservation, constitutional harmlessness and lawful trial management remain separate inquiries.

#### Separate Writings

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and in the judgment.** Stone-Zsela joins Part I. The blanks, grammar, limited named participants and prosecutor's immediate connection between Bell's information and Gray's arrest directly identify whose name was removed. The jury need not wait for later independent evidence to identify the accused, so an instruction cannot cure the uncross-examined accusation. His reliance on immediate context explains the preserved admission objection; it creates no separate ruling that the follow-up question itself was a preserved independent constitutional wrong. He would direct the state court to determine harmless error, without deciding it here, and permit consideration of materially different redactions or lawful trial-management alternatives. He does not join Part II's limitation of the remand instruction to a properly available harmless-error contention. Joint trials, genuinely neutral wording, independently admissible confessions and cross-examined testimony remain outside his prohibition.

**Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas, dissenting.** The dissent would apply Richardson's distinction between a confession that identifies the accused on its face and one that becomes incriminating through inference from other evidence. Bell's statement contains two deletions, while other testimony describes six participants and only one other defendant is at this trial. A blank does not uniquely identify Gray without a further inference. Conspicuous editing alone is not the powerfully incriminating express accusation for which Bruton displaces the ordinary presumption that jurors follow limiting instructions. The dissent objects to a rule encouraging concealed edits or substantive rewriting, and would affirm on the preserved confession-admission issue. It does not treat instructions as a cure for every confrontation problem or eliminate Rule 14 severance and evidentiary protection beyond the constitutional minimum. The prosecutor's follow-up question presents a separate issue: the State says an arrest warrant preceded the confession and characterizes the question as a transition, and no contemporaneous objection to that question is identified. The dissent would not use it to circumvent preservation or to transform the preserved redaction question into a different adjudication.

#### Procedure After Action

The Maryland judgment reinstating Gray's conviction is vacated. Further state proceedings must apply the redaction holding and, for any properly available harmless-error contention, Chapman. Materially different wording and lawful trial-management alternatives may be addressed in those proceedings on their own records. No present new-trial, acquittal or harmlessness result is directed.

#### Source Notes

The [reported record and confession appendix, 523 U.S. 185](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-523/pdf/USREPORTS-523-185.pdf), supply the joint-trial setting, two deleted names, additional-participant testimony, limiting instruction and appellate sequence. The [Bruton opinion, 391 U.S. 123](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-391/pdf/USREPORTS-391-123.pdf), and [Richardson opinion, 481 U.S. 200](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-481/pdf/USREPORTS-481-200.pdf), supply their distinct rules. The State's account of the prior warrant and reason for its follow-up question remains a submission, not a new factual finding. The record identifies objections to severance and admission, but no contemporaneous objection to that particular question; no new waiver or harmlessness finding is inferred from that limit.

### Quality King Distributors, Inc. v. L'anza Research International, Inc., No. 96-1470. — 1998-03-09

#### Event

Quality King Distributors, Inc. v. L'anza Research International, Inc., No. 96-1470. Merits decision, March 9, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 5. Argued December 8, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Ninth Circuit, which affirmed summary judgment for L'anza after holding the first-sale defense unavailable against §602(a). The question concerns copyrighted labels on copies made in the United States, sold abroad and reimported without renewed copyright permission. Quality King seeks application of the first-sale rule and reversal.

#### Participation

All nine Justices participate at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. No recusal is stated.

#### Public Action

The Ninth Circuit's judgment is reversed and the case returned for proceedings consistent with the Court's statutory holding. Section 602(a) does not exempt the distribution right from §109(a) for the domestic-manufacture round trip presented here.

#### Judgment & Remedy

| Component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Remedy |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Categorical denial of the first-sale defense | Reversed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | — | Apply the statutory rule for qualifying domestically made copies |
| Further proceedings rather than directed judgment | Return for proceedings consistent with the opinion, 8–1 | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Stone-Zsela | No new transaction findings or final independent-claim judgment; Stone-Zsela would direct judgment for Quality King on the copyright-importation theory |

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | Scalia | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Rejects the categorical importation rule | Complete owner-or-authorized-person rule, statutory linkage and domestic-manufacture limit |
| Same opinion, partial join | Scalia | Stone-Zsela | Agrees with reversal | Lawful-owner reimportation proposition; no join in the authorized-person extension |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II | Scalia | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Reverses and returns for consistent further proceedings | No directed final judgment or new transaction findings |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone-Zsela | — | Agrees with reversal; would direct judgment for Quality King on the copyright-importation claim | Joins Part I only as to lawful-owner reimportation; rejects Part II's open remand in favor of the stated final relief |

#### Holdings

### I. The first-sale limitation governs these domestically made copies

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 602(a) defines unauthorized importation as infringement of the distribution right in §106(3), and that right remains subject to §109(a). For a particular copy lawfully made in the United States and sold abroad with the copyright owner's authorization, its lawful owner, or a person authorized by that owner, may sell or otherwise dispose of that copy without renewed copyright permission; reimportation does not revive the exhausted distribution right.

**Authority:** Part I of Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; eight Justices adopt the complete owner-or-authorized-person rule and its domestic-manufacture boundary. Stone-Zsela joins the rule only as it concerns reimportation by the lawful owner, giving that narrower proposition nine votes; he does not join the extension to a person authorized by that owner.

**Controlling explanation:** The enacted provisions operate together. Section 602(a) does not declare a freestanding right immune from the limits on the distribution right it expressly invokes. Section 106 subjects its rights to §§107–120, including §109(a), which permits the owner of a qualifying copy to dispose of it without the copyright owner's authority. A sale abroad does not, by itself, remove that limit for these domestically made copies. L'anza's interest in maintaining different domestic and foreign prices cannot supply an exception Congress did not enact.

The contrary argument that this reading makes §602(a) meaningless overlooks its remaining operation when the copy is not lawfully made, or the importer possesses it without ownership or the owner's authorization. The Court need not determine every remaining application to reject an independent importation right exempt from §109.

Bobbs-Merrill supplies the first-sale background: copyright's distribution authority does not itself enforce a continuing resale restriction after the owner's authorized sale. That principle does not decide contract liability. Campbell and Fogerty likewise respect Congress's actual rights and limitations; general market-policy preferences cannot enlarge the enacted entitlement. Here the claim concerns distribution and importation of copyrighted labels on domestically manufactured goods, not unauthorized reproduction. The Court leaves foreign-manufactured copies unresolved and does not decide counterfeiting, reproduction rights, independent contractual duties, trademark confusion, labeling or consumer-protection law.

### II. The categorical importation judgment cannot stand

**Holding and operative rule:** The Ninth Circuit's judgment, which treated §602(a) as barring the first-sale defense to this domestic-manufacture round trip, is reversed and the case returned for proceedings consistent with Part I. This disposition supplies no new finding about the first purchasing intermediary, exact place of title transfer or actual importer, and directs no damages, contract judgment or final judgment on an independently preserved theory.

**Authority:** Part II of Scalia's opinion, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; all eight Associates adopt that limited remedial direction. Stone-Zsela joins reversal of the legal rule but would direct judgment for Quality King on the copyright-importation theory.

**Controlling explanation:** The District Court rejected the first-sale defense and entered summary judgment for L'anza; the Court of Appeals affirmed because it regarded the importation right as independent of §109. That categorical legal premise is incorrect. Reversal restores the statutory limitation without making new findings about the transaction's precise title sequence or resolving claims governed by different law. The published account supports the domestic-origin export-and-return setting but does not settle every intermediary detail. The Court therefore states the governing rule and returns the case for its consistent application, without turning the rejection of L'anza's legal theory into an unexpressed adjudication of every remaining issue.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Bobbs-Merrill Co. v. Straus, 210 U.S. 339, supplies the first-sale principle limiting copyright control over a particular copy after an authorized sale; its resale-notice rule is not enlarged into a general contract holding.
- Campbell v. Acuff-Rose Music, Inc., 510 U.S. 569, preserves enforcement of enacted copyright limits rather than dispositive market-policy presumptions; its four-factor fair-use inquiry is unchanged and is not substituted for §109.
- Fogerty v. Fantasy, Inc., 510 U.S. 517, preserves copyright's actual statutory boundaries; its discretionary fee rules do not determine first sale or any fee award here.

#### Law After Decision

The first-sale limit applies to the §106(3) distribution right invoked by §602(a) for the domestically made copies at issue. Foreign sale alone does not recreate that right on reimportation. Ownership or authorization by the copy's owner and lawful manufacture remain necessary; foreign manufacture and the distinct legal regimes identified above remain unresolved. No infringement, damages, fee or independent contract rule is added beyond the stated holding.

#### Separate Writings

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** Stone-Zsela joins Part I only insofar as it addresses copies made in the United States, sold abroad with the copyright owner's authorization and later reimported by their lawful owner. He does not join its broader rule for a person authorized by that owner. Section 602(a) invokes the §106(3) right, which §109(a) limits; crossing the border cannot recreate an exhausted distribution right, and territorial-pricing policy cannot do so either. He would direct entry of judgment for Quality King on the claim that this reimportation infringed that distribution right through §602(a), rather than leave that theory for further proceedings. Any preserved independent contract, trademark, labeling or consumer-protection claim remains governed by its own law. His disposition would not decide foreign manufacture, unauthorized reproductions, counterfeits, reproduction rights, trademark confusion or contractual duties enforceable against a contracting party.

#### Procedure After Action

The appellate judgment upholding L'anza's copyright-importation claim under the categorical no-first-sale rule is reversed. Further proceedings must apply the Court's statutory construction. No particular damages amount, contract result or final judgment on an independent claim is directed. Stone-Zsela's preferred direction of judgment on the importation theory is not the Court's mandate.

#### Source Notes

The [reported proceeding, 523 U.S. 135](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-523/pdf/USREPORTS-523-135.pdf), supplies the domestic manufacture, distribution arrangements, export-and-return account and summary-judgment posture. The exact foreign intermediary first purchasing, place of title transfer and importer identity are not newly found. No unauthorized reproduction of the labels is alleged. Sections 106(3), 109(a) and 602(a) govern the statutory question; [Bobbs-Merrill, 210 U.S. 339](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-210/pdf/USREPORTS-210-339.pdf), supplies the first-sale background.

### Aktepe v. United States; Eleventh Circuit No. 96-2167. — 1998-03-10

#### Event

Aktepe v. United States; Eleventh Circuit No. 96-2167, judgment below 105 F.3d 1400. Plenary decision on justiciability, March 10, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 5. The Court reviews under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) the February 20, 1997 affirmance of summary judgment for the United States. The question is whether the pleaded maritime negligence controversy is nonjusticiable, including whether a separable operational claim can be adjudicated without choosing military policy. No separate Supreme Court docket or oral-argument date is established. The sailors seek adjudication of two wrongful-death and 299 personal-injury claims arising when the Saratoga fired live missiles at the Muavenet during an intended mock NATO attack.

#### Participation

All nine Justices participate at submission and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

#### Public Action

The Court affirms the Eleventh Circuit's political-question judgment.

#### Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed, 5–4. Supporting:** O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer. **Opposing:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg. The political-question summary judgment remains effective. No negligence, damages, statutory-coverage, sovereign-immunity or Feres ruling, discovery order or new trial follows.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas and Breyer | Affirms | Complete claim-specific nonjusticiability ground and limits |
| Dissent | Stevens | Stone-Zsela, Souter and Ginsburg | Would vacate and remand | Issue-specific separation of execution allegations from policy choices; no guaranteed surviving claim |

#### Holdings

### The pleaded duties cannot be adjudicated without choosing military training policy

**Holding and operative rule:** A military setting alone does not make a damages action nonjusticiable. These claims nevertheless cannot proceed because determining the alleged duties concerning drill notification, command terminology, live arming and questioning orders would require choosing among military training, obedience and safety policies without an independently manageable legal standard on the supplied record.

**Authority:** Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas and Breyer; five of nine participating Justices directly adopt the rule, application and limits.

**Controlling explanation:** Baker requires examination of the particular claim, not rejection because its subject is military. Nixon preserves that issue-specific inquiry. Here ordinary negligence terminology does not identify a duty independent of the disputed training choices. Deciding which personnel should know that an attack is simulated, what live-weapons commands should mean, and when operators should question an apparent firing instruction would select the balance between realistic preparation, command obedience and safety that the pleaded allegations challenge.

The contrary argument has force: this was an unintended live discharge during an intended simulation, and compensation after an accident differs from continuing supervision. Gilligan expressly left specific-injury controversies distinct from the ongoing supervision it rejected. But that reservation neither makes every damages action justiciable nor supplies a manageable standard for these proposed duties. Retrospective relief can still depend on judicial selection of training policy. No independently fixed operational command is established in the supplied record that separates the alleged breach from that selection; this is not a finding that no such command exists or that plaintiffs withheld evidence.

Weiss and Loving preserve enforcement of actual constitutional and enacted limits. Dalton concerns a particular statutory review scheme and supplies no general executive immunity. Richenberg likewise combines substantial military weight with an enforceable legal standard. None supplies the independent duty missing from this claim-specific inquiry. NATO participation, possible embarrassment and military status alone would be insufficient. The Court affirms the political-question judgment without finding that the firing was lawful, deciding negligence or damages, or resolving statutory coverage, sovereign immunity or Feres. A materially different claim enforcing an independent legal duty remains outside this holding.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Baker v. Carr supplies the claim-specific political-question inquiry; military subject matter alone remains insufficient.
- Nixon v. United States preserves review of independently fixed legal limits while recognizing the confined nonjusticiability of an actually committed procedural choice; its impeachment holding is not military immunity.
- Gilligan v. Morgan, 413 U.S. 1, is applied to the policy-selection concern, while its reservation of specific-injury controversies remains intact and supplies no categorical damages exception.
- Weiss v. United States and Loving v. United States preserve constitutional and statutory review in military matters; their appointment and delegation holdings do not establish the maritime duty proposed here.
- Dalton v. Specter retains scheme-specific statutory preclusion and is not extended to these maritime statutes.
