- Richenberg v. Cohen preserves substantial military weight within actual legal constraints, without creating a military-tort immunity. No precedent is overruled.

#### Law After Decision

The issue-specific political-question rule applies to these pleaded training and communication duties. Military status does not alone bar judicial review, and the decision establishes no categorical military-tort immunity. Independent statutory defenses and any materially different claim resting on an independently manageable duty retain their own requirements.

#### Separate Writings

**Stevens, joined by Stone-Zsela, Souter and Ginsburg, dissenting.** The dissent would vacate and remand for separate examination of the claims. The intended simulation and accidental live firing supply a concrete reason to distinguish execution from design; listing training and command purposes does not establish that every allegation requires choosing policy. Gilligan's reservation of specific-injury cases and Nixon's claim-specific limitation oppose the blanket effect of the judgment. Weiss and Loving preserve enforceable legal constraints despite military subject matter, and Dalton does not supply general executive insulation.

The dissent accepts that a court cannot invent a safer training rule, call it operational, and thereby evade the political-question doctrine. The remand would require the Eleventh Circuit to identify which allegations actually require selection of constitutionally committed military policy or lack manageable standards, without guaranteeing a surviving negligence claim, discovery, liability or compensation. No particular written protocol is found or presumed violated. Statutory coverage, sovereign immunity, Feres, causation, damages and the justiciability of any specifically identified high-level policy challenge remain unresolved. The dissent's proposed examination is not the Court's mandate.

#### Procedure After Action

This Supreme Court review is complete. Summary judgment on the political-question ground remains in force; the Court directs no further merits proceedings.

#### Source Notes

The [official Eleventh Circuit opinion, 105 F.3d 1400](https://media.ca11.uscourts.gov/opinions/pub/files/19962167.OPA.pdf), supplies the summary-judgment posture, uncontested drill and communication facts and the unadjudicated Feres alternative. [Gilligan, 413 U.S. 1](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-413/pdf/USREPORTS-413-1.pdf), distinguishes continuing training supervision from reserved specific-injury controversies. The original maritime pleadings and evidence are not separately reproduced; no particular written operational protocol is established.

### W.P. v. Verniero; Third Circuit No. 96-5416. — 1998-03-16

#### Event

W.P. v. Verniero; Third Circuit No. 96-5416, reported with E.B. in 119 F.3d 1077. Plenary merits decision, March 16, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 5. On review under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) of the W.P. class judgment decided August 20 and amended September 4, 1997. The questions concern punishment and ordinary pre-notification process for persons whose offenses preceded October 31, 1994 and who are or will be assigned Tier 2 or 3. The Court of Appeals rejected punishment relief but reversed officials' summary judgment and directed an injunction requiring advance opportunity to contest tier and plan with clear and convincing State persuasion. E.B., No. 96-5132, is a distinct jurisdictional proceeding outside this review. No separate Supreme Court docket or argument date is established.

#### Participation

All nine Justices participate at submission and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

#### Public Action

The W.P. judgment is affirmed: punishment relief is denied and the ordinary-process injunction is preserved, including its clear-and-convincing requirements for tier and notification plan.

#### Judgment & Remedy

| Component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing or distinct position | Remedy |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Denial of punishment relief | Affirmed, 8–1 | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Stevens would grant Ex Post Facto relief and does not reach independent Double Jeopardy identity | No classwide punishment injunction |
| Advance review and State persuasion for both tier and plan | Affirmed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | — | Preserve this ordinary-process injunction |
| Clear and convincing proof for risk classification | Affirmed, 5–4 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas would permit State preponderance | Preserve heightened classification proof |
| Clear and convincing proof for notification plan | Affirmed, 5–4 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas would permit State preponderance | Preserve the plan requirement; Stone-Zsela agrees with this disposition only |

The W.P. appellate judgment and its instruction to deny further relief are affirmed within this scope. The unripe emergency question remains reserved, with all nine supporting that disposition; Stone-Zsela adds no emergency ground. E.B.'s separate dismissal mandate is undisturbed, and no individual tier, plan, registration-only claim, Tier 1 restriction, post-enactment application or damages award is adjudicated.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | Souter | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer | Rejects punishment relief | Common civil classification under both presented clauses |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II | Souter | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Preserves advance review and State persuasion for tier and plan | Substantive state constraint, hearing and burden allocation |
| Opinion of the Court, Part III-A | Souter | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer | Affirms heightened proof for risk classification | Clear and convincing factual basis and tier determination |
| Opinion announcing the judgment, Part III-B | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer | Affirms heightened proof for notification plan | Four-Justice plan-specific explanation; Stone-Zsela agrees only with this judgment component |
| Opinion of the Court, Part IV | Souter | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Leaves emergency issue unripe | No approval or categorical prohibition of exceptional notice procedure; Stone-Zsela agrees in disposition only |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stone-Zsela | — | Affirms | Joins Parts I and III-A; separate ordinary-process safeguards; judgment-only agreement on Parts II and III-B; emergency disposition only |
| Dissent in part, joining Parts II, III-A, III-B and IV | Stevens | — | Would grant retrospective Ex Post Facto relief; otherwise joins the indicated process disposition | Punitive-operation objection; conditional process joins when civil notification remains available |
| Concurrence in part in the judgment and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas in full; O'Connor and Kennedy on proof quantum only | Rejects punishment relief; would remove heightened proof while retaining State persuasion and advance review | Separate successive-criminal-prosecution ground and preponderance process ground; joins Parts II and IV |

#### Holdings

### I. The challenged notification is not punishment on this record

**Holding and operative rule:** For the class whose offenses preceded October 31, 1994 and who face Tier 2 or Tier 3 notification, New Jersey's civil public-protection purpose remains unrebutted by the clearest proof of punitive purpose or effect in the scheme's supported combined operation. The challenged notification therefore supplies no Ex Post Facto or Double Jeopardy ground for relief; the Court decides no same-offense question.

**Authority:** Part I of Souter's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer; six Justices directly adopt the common civil-classification ground. Scalia and Thomas support rejection on their separate grounds; Stevens dissents on Ex Post Facto punishment and does not reach independent Double Jeopardy identity.

**Controlling explanation:** Ursery supplies civil intent followed by demanding contrary proof, and Hendricks and Hudson require examination of actual combined operation. A civil label is insufficient. Here risk grading, individual exceptions and audience tailoring connect the warning to public protection. Tier 2 reaches qualifying schools, child-care facilities and organizations serving children or supporting women, subject to the state court's likely-encounter construction; Tier 3 reaches members of the public likely to encounter the registrant. The scheme directly imposes no residence, movement, employment or licensing prohibition.

The contrary shaming argument is substantial. Government actively distributes conviction and predicted-danger information, with potentially severe effects on housing, employment, relationships and safety. Those consequences cannot be dismissed categorically because some involve private acts. But the supported tailoring and causal record do not establish the clearest proof of punishment. The reported nineteen experiences, six state-compelled disclosures and separate 135-notification figure cannot be merged into one incidence calculation. Confidentiality instructions for Tier 2 and warnings against threats, vandalism and assault matter without guaranteeing perfect containment.

Hendricks found a different cumulative operation: prisonlike additional custody sustained on a future treatment structure. Its punitive result supplies no automatic classification of notification, just as its civil designation supplied no immunity there. Hudson and Kurth Ranch preserve meaningful aggregate examination; Halper's personal-monetary-penalty accounting is not a notification formula. The holding neither makes reputation irrelevant nor treats every public-safety measure as civil. Materially different punitive operation remains a different case. Registration alone, Tier 1, post-enactment offenses and every later registry are not decided.

### II. The State must justify both tier and notification plan before ordinary notification

**Holding and operative rule:** In the ordinary circumstances presented, Tier 2 or Tier 3 notification may not proceed without a meaningful advance opportunity to contest both the risk classification and the proposed notification plan, with the prosecutor bearing persuasion for each. The protected interest rests on New Jersey's substantive restriction requiring disclosure to serve a legitimate and substantial interest with minimized invasion, rather than on reputation alone or a promise of procedures.

**Authority:** Part II of Souter's opinion, joined by all seven other Associates; eight Justices directly adopt this protected-interest, hearing and State-persuasion ground. Stevens joins conditionally because civil notification remains available under the Court's judgment. Stone-Zsela supports the disposition on his stated procedural grounds without joining this distinct state-entitlement explanation.

**Controlling explanation:** Sandin directs attention to a real substantive constraint and meaningful deprivation; its prison setting supplies no universal noncustodial threshold. Paul v. Davis distinguishes reputation alone, which is not the sole interest here. The actual state disclosure restraint and irreversible distribution make erroneous classification or an excessive audience a protected deprivation.

Mathews requires consideration of the interest, error risk and practical burdens. The State selects the tier and audience and already assembles an initial case. Requiring the registrant ultimately to disprove that justification wrongly allocates the risk of an unsupported disclosure. A prosecutorial prima facie presentation does not replace the State's ultimate persuasion on both determinations. Public protection and prompt warning remain substantial interests, but do not justify making the affected person disprove the affirmative basis for disclosure.

Individual review must permit challenges to factual accuracy and calculation, atypical cases and excessive plans. A credible supported indication of likely reoffense requires high-risk treatment regardless of score; a physical condition minimizing risk, including advanced age or debilitating illness, requires low-risk treatment regardless of score. The Scale is not conclusive. This individual proceeding does not authorize a new facial attack on the Scale or its weights. No individual tier or plan is approved, and no general federal informational-privacy right is decided. The distinct proof-quantum question is addressed below.

### III. Risk classification requires clear and convincing proof; the plan injunction is retained on a narrower authority basis

**Holding and operative rule — risk classification:** Before the ordinary Tier 2 or Tier 3 notification presented here, the State must establish the factual basis and risk classification by clear and convincing evidence. Preponderance alone is constitutionally insufficient for that classification in this setting; proof beyond a reasonable doubt and certainty of future conduct are not required.

**Authority:** Part III-A of Souter's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer; five direct supporters. Stevens's join operates because the Court permits civil notification. O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas would hold State preponderance sufficient.

**Controlling explanation:** Mathews requires a practical comparison of error risks, not automatic transplantation of every civil burden. Addington and Santosky show that a civil label does not end the inquiry and that sufficiently serious erroneous deprivation can require greater assurance. The classification here combines disputed historical facts, unadjudicated circumstances and predictive judgments with disclosure that cannot effectively be recalled. Overclassification can alter housing, work, safety and status before an error is repaired.

Underclassification can endanger potential victims, and that concern is not discounted. But continuing registration and law-enforcement monitoring remain protective measures, while the State already marshals its initial factual case. A prompt adversarial determination under the heightened standard reduces erroneous classification without demanding perfect prediction or disabling warnings justified by the evidence. The required high-risk and low-risk overrides and individual factual review give the additional assurance practical work. Existing hearing procedures do not answer the separate question of ultimate persuasion and its strength. Sahhar preserves different burdens for different legal operations; this holding derives from this notification setting, not from treating its initial-commitment and discharge provisions as interchangeable. The Court imposes no universal heightened burden for every civil prediction.

**Judgment without a controlling plan-quantum rationale:** The injunction's requirement of clear and convincing State proof for the notification plan is also affirmed, 5–4, by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Only Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer join Part III-B's plan-specific error-allocation reasoning. Stone-Zsela supports retention of that injunction component without adopting a plan-specific ground. O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas would retain State persuasion but permit preponderance. The injunction binds this litigation; no five-Justice plan-quantum rationale or broader general rule is manufactured from the judgment. There is no logical-subset combination of a reasonless judgment agreement and the four-Justice explanation under Marks.

### IV. The advance-notice emergency question remains unripe

**Holding and operative rule:** The present class challenge does not decide whether a concrete emergency may justify notification without ordinary advance notice because no representative alleges such notification and no prosecutor is shown to have done or planned it. The ordinary-process injunction is retained subject to that express reservation; it neither approves every emergency bypass nor prohibits every judicially authorized exception.

**Authority:** Part IV of Souter's opinion, joined by all seven other Associates. Stone-Zsela agrees with the disposition of the separately reserved emergency component, adding no emergency rationale or new exception.

**Controlling explanation:** The described mechanism permits a prosecutor to seek a judicial order when release information arrives only after a Tier 3 offender's release or the registrant evades service despite every good-faith effort; after three days without completed service the prosecutor may apply. Permission to apply is not a command to bypass notice. Without a concrete use or planned use affecting the class, the Court cannot determine the constitutional adequacy of that exceptional procedure or any narrowing state construction. A later concrete application must stand on its own facts and legal basis. The ordinary holding therefore resolves neither that future controversy nor an imagined automatic emergency exemption.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Ursery, Ward and Mendoza-Martinez supply rebuttable civil classification and combined purpose/effect examination; neither labels nor a single indicator is conclusive.
- Hendricks retains its as-applied punitive-confinement holding and actual-operation method; its outcome is distinguished, not replaced with an approval of that custody.
- Hudson preserves aggregate examination and Halper in its separate personal-monetary field. Halper is not extended into a notification accounting test.
- Kurth Ranch retains its extraordinary-tax punishment holding; its arrest-linked operation differs from the tailored warnings here.
- Sandin's substantive-constraint principle informs the protected state interest without transplanting a prison threshold into all civil settings. Paul v. Davis's reputation-alone boundary remains intact.
- Mathews supplies the context-specific process comparison. Addington and Santosky support attention to serious and irreversible error without creating a universal civil proof quantum.
- Sahhar preserves the distinct statutory initial-commitment and discharge burdens; neither is mechanically imported.
- Blockburger and retained Grady offense-identity questions are not reached. No precedent is overruled.

#### Law After Decision

Civil classification remains rebuttable by clearest proof of punitive combined operation. A genuine substantive state privacy constraint supports ordinary advance review and State persuasion for both tier and plan. Clear and convincing proof for the factual basis and risk classification is a five-Justice rule. The plan requirement remains part of the affirmed injunction, with no five-Justice plan-specific rationale. The emergency question and separate E.B. jurisdictional judgment remain outside the merits resolution.

#### Separate Writings

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and in the judgment.** Stone-Zsela joins the civil-classification and heightened risk-classification portions. Public protection and a civil structure do not remove the need for timely notice, disclosure of the basis, an opportunity to contest, a neutral decisionmaker, written reasons and clear and convincing State proof before the broad notification at issue. The State controls the evidence and proposes the classification; an erroneous high tier can irreversibly affect housing, work, safety and status. A prompt adversarial process can reduce error without defeating protection. His express affirmance includes retention of the notification-plan injunction, but he adopts no plan-specific explanation and does not join the separate state-entitlement analysis. On the unripe emergency component he agrees only with the disposition and adds no emergency ground. His stated safeguards supply no majority adoption of a new general written-reasons or disclosure code. Confidential registration and lower-tier treatment remain subject to their own law; every internet registry, lifetime classification without review and affirmatively punitive operation remain unresolved.

**Stevens, dissenting in part.** Stevens would reverse the denial of Ex Post Facto relief and require an injunction against retrospective Tier 2/3 notification for this defined class. The affirmative official broadcast of conviction and predicted dangerousness, its criminal-history trigger and enduring cumulative burdens resemble imposed shaming. Risk and audience limits deserve weight but do not alter that combined character on his assessment. Ursery's dissenting approach and the actual-operation holdings of Hendricks and Hudson support looking beyond the civil label. He does not attribute every private attack to the State or decide independent Double Jeopardy offense identity. Because the Court leaves civil notification available, he joins the indicated process portions, including clear and convincing proof for both tier and plan.

**Souter's Part III-B, joined by Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer.** These four would apply clear and convincing persuasion to the plan as well as the classification because unnecessary geographic or audience expansion causes the same irreversible disclosure. The State prepares the plan and its initial justification; factual disputes, atypical risks and the actual likely-encounter limit make stronger assurance useful without demanding certainty. Continuing registration and monitoring reduce the comparative cost of mistaken undernotification. This is the rationale of four Justices, not a controlling general plan-quantum rule.

**Scalia, joined by Thomas; O'Connor and Kennedy join the proof-quantum portion only.** Scalia and Thomas reject the Ex Post Facto challenge because the particular risk-tailored warning scheme is genuinely civil and protective on this record. They separately reject the Double Jeopardy challenge because that civil warning process is not a successive criminal prosecution. They preserve their Hudson disagreement with a freestanding Halper civil multiple-punishment method; that view does not overrule Halper. All four proof-quantum dissenters accept the substantive state interest, advance opportunity and State persuasion for both tier and plan, but would permit preponderance. Mathews also accounts for potential victims, the danger of underclassification and existing safeguards. Noncustodial tailored warnings do not impose Addington's physical custody or Santosky's family-status severance; neither serious consequences nor a protected interest mechanically requires heightened certainty. Sahhar's different commitment and discharge rules demonstrate the need to preserve the actual legal operation. Their proposed modification would remove only the heightened quantum, preserving the remainder of the ordinary-process injunction.

#### Procedure After Action

The W.P. injunction remains in force: ordinary Tier 2/3 notification for this class requires advance opportunity to contest tier and plan and clear and convincing State persuasion for each. The expressly unripe emergency question remains reserved. Further relief is denied within the reviewed class; no individual risk assessment or notification plan is approved.

#### Source Notes

The [amended Third Circuit opinion, 119 F.3d 1077](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/119/119.F3d.1077.html), supplies the separate E.B. and W.P. appeals, class limits, scheme, individual-review rules, reported effects, process injunction and unripe emergency issue. The operative statute and Guidelines are described through that published account. The nineteen experiences, six compelled disclosures and 135 notifications are distinct populations; no combined incidence or causation finding is made.

### Caterpillar Inc. v. International Union, UAW, No. 96-1925. — 1998-03-23

#### Event

Caterpillar Inc. v. International Union, UAW, No. 96-1925. Dismissal under Rule 46.1, March 23, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 5. Argued January 20, 1998. The writ sought review of the en banc Third Circuit's judgment, 107 F.3d 1052, reversing the employer-favorable District Court judgment in a dispute about LMRA §302 and collectively bargained payments to union representatives for time spent on union business. The parties' conforming written dismissal agreement ends this review before a Supreme Court merits judgment.

#### Participation

No judicial vote is announced for this Rule-assigned act of the Clerk.

#### Public Action

The writ is dismissed by the Clerk under Rule 46.1 in accordance with the parties' agreement concerning costs. This is neither a merits affirmance nor a discretionary dismissal as improvidently granted. The judgment below is not reversed or vacated. No Court vote, merits coalition, mandate, settlement-enforcement judgment or other collateral disposition is announced.

#### Judgment & Remedy

The writ is dismissed by the Clerk under Rule 46.1 in accordance with the parties' agreement concerning costs. This is neither a merits affirmance nor a discretionary dismissal as improvidently granted. The judgment below is not reversed or vacated. No Court vote, merits coalition, mandate, settlement-enforcement judgment or other collateral disposition is announced.

#### Opinion Topology

The Clerk enters the Rule 46.1 dismissal. No opinion of the Court or separate writing is issued, and no judicial authorship or adjudicative tally is attributed to this administrative act.

#### Holdings

No new judicial holding is announced. Under Rule 46.1, when all parties file a written agreement to dismiss specifying payment of costs and pay any fees then due to the Clerk, the Clerk enters dismissal without further reference to the Court. That rule supplies the authority for this administrative termination. Rule 46.3 separately requires a Court order for a mandate or other process; no such order accompanies this dismissal.

The dismissal decides no LMRA §302 question, does not affirm the lower court's reasoning and grants no vacatur or settlement-enforcement relief. The underlying judgment remains undisturbed. Any independently presented collateral request must satisfy its own jurisdictional and remedial requirements.

#### Precedent Treatment

Bancorp's equitable requirements for vacatur remain distinct from ending review by agreement; dismissal supplies no automatic entitlement to erase the lower judgment. Kokkonen preserves the need for an independent jurisdictional basis or an otherwise lawful retained enforcement authority for a settlement controversy. Arizonans concerns a different correction of lower-court jurisdictional error. None is altered or converted into a new holding by this dismissal.

#### Law After Decision

The dismissal ends review without deciding the labor-law merits or changing any Supreme Court doctrine. The judgment below retains its existing force; agreement to end review supplies no merits approval, automatic vacatur or independent settlement-enforcement jurisdiction.

#### Separate Writings

No separate writing is issued.

#### Procedure After Action

The writ proceeding is terminated under Rule 46.1. No mandate or other process is issued under Rule 46.3, and no new lower-court proceedings are directed. Any distinct supported collateral request remains subject to its own legal requirements.

#### Source Notes

The [1997 Supreme Court Rules](https://www.supremecourt.gov/ctrules/rules/Rules_1997.pdf), effective May 1, 1997, supply the separate functions of Rules 46.1 and 46.3. The published party-agreement account and matter-specific procedural designation support the conforming dismissal predicate. The original agreement and fee receipt are not reproduced; no particular cost allocation, fee amount or substantive settlement term is adjudicated. The argument date and underlying review concern appear in the public docket and [reported proceeding at 523 U.S. 1015](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-523/pdf/USREPORTS-523.pdf).

### Hetzel v. Prince William County, No. 97-954. — 1998-03-23

#### Event

Hetzel v. Prince William County, No. 97-954. Certiorari granted, March 23, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 5. The petition seeks review of the Fourth Circuit's mandamus command requiring an unconditional reduced compensatory judgment and staying a new damages trial. The limited question is whether that command may deny the plaintiff the option of accepting a properly determined remittitur or obtaining a new jury trial. The separate appeal from a later $15,000 District Court judgment is not included in this grant. No oral argument occurs on the petition.

#### Participation

All nine Justices participate. The internal certiorari positions are not disclosed.

#### Public Action

The petition for a writ of certiorari is granted, limited to the challenged mandamus restriction on the plaintiff's remittitur election. The certiorari poll is not publicly disclosed.

#### Judgment & Remedy

The grant changes the matter to the limited merits stage. It does not vacate a judgment, restore an award, decide the separate damages appeal or itself require retrial.

#### Opinion Topology

No opinion or separate writing accompanies this grant.

#### Holdings

The grant resolves no merits question and changes no governing law. The limited mandamus/election question proceeds to the separate summary-merits action of March 23, 1998; the grant itself neither selects damages nor orders a new trial.

#### Precedent Treatment

The grant makes no precedential disposition.

#### Law After Decision

Governing law is unchanged by the grant.

#### Separate Writings

No separate writing is issued.

#### Procedure After Action

The limited question is addressed in the separate summary-merits action of March 23, 1998. No broader question or additional appeal is taken up.

#### Source Notes

The [reported proceeding, 523 U.S. 208](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-523/pdf/USREPORTS-523-208.pdf), supplies the mandamus sequence, respondents' earlier-petition objection and the distinct pending appeal. Original earlier-petition and mandamus filings are known through published recitations rather than reproduced originals. No separate damages appeal, additional claim or dollar amount is resolved by this review.

### Hetzel v. Prince William County, No. 97-954. — 1998-03-23

#### Event

Hetzel v. Prince William County, No. 97-954. Summary merits decision, March 23, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 5. Following the separate same-date limited grant, the Court reviews the Fourth Circuit's mandamus command requiring an unconditional reduced compensatory judgment and staying a damages retrial. No oral argument is held. A $750,000 jury award was first reduced to $500,000 because one supporting claim was legally insufficient; after appellate excessiveness review, the District Court set $50,000 and permitted a new damages trial when Hetzel rejected it. The challenged mandamus foreclosed that option. A later $15,000 judgment and its separate pending appeal are not the judgment reviewed here.

#### Participation

All nine Justices participate: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

#### Public Action

The challenged mandamus judgment is reversed insofar as it forecloses the remittitur election, and the case is remanded for proceedings consistent with that ruling.

#### Judgment & Remedy

**Reversed in the reviewed respect and remanded, 9–0. Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. The mandamus constraint is removed insofar as it requires an unconditional reduced compensatory award and bars the jury election. Further proceedings must permit acceptance of a properly determined remittitur or an appropriate new trial, with damages-only retrial dependent on separability. No amount is selected, original verdict restored, claim revived or separate pending appeal decided.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | Per curiam | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Reaches later mandamus challenge | Limited earlier-petition/mandate-scope ground; Stone-Zsela agrees with disposition only |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II | Per curiam | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Reverses the constraint and remands | Common remittitur election, remedy and stated limits |

No separate writing is filed. The merits joins disclose no certiorari poll.

#### Holdings

### I. The earlier-petition objection does not bar review of this later restriction

**Holding and operative rule:** Respondents' objection that the earlier petition omitted the jury-election claim does not prevent review of the later mandamus command here. The earlier mandate required recalculation without necessarily eliminating a remittitur election; the constitutional objection was before the Court of Appeals when its later order expressly required final judgment and stayed retrial.

**Authority:** Part I of the per curiam opinion, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; eight Justices directly adopt this confined threshold ground. Stone-Zsela agrees with reaching the later challenge but adds no preservation ground.

**Controlling explanation:** Recalculation and compulsory acceptance are different instructions. A reduced award can remain conditional on the plaintiff's choice, so the earlier instruction did not necessarily impose the no-election restriction now challenged. After Hetzel rejected the revised sum, the District Court addressed the Seventh Amendment question and permitted a new damages trial. That memorandum and issue were before the Court of Appeals when mandamus required an unconditional final judgment and stayed the trial.

Sanford Fork & Tool distinguishes matters actually settled by a mandate from those left open and permits examination of the opinion to determine its reach. Messenger distinguishes law-of-the-case practice from a jurisdictional limit on the rendering court's power. Neither excuses every omitted issue in an earlier petition. They support identifying what the earlier mandate actually determined; this sequence identifies the later no-election command as the challenged restriction.

Respondents correctly observe that a later enforcement order does not always reopen an earlier conclusive decision. That principle does not establish such a prior decision here. The Court does not reopen the earlier excessiveness determination, infer an unquoted contrary mandate term or find a general waiver exception. The separate pending appeal from the later $15,000 judgment neither erases this concrete restriction nor becomes part of this review.

### II. An excessiveness remittitur must preserve the new-trial election

**Holding and operative rule:** A federal court may condition retention of a jury's compensatory judgment on acceptance of a lawful reduction for excessiveness, but it may not impose that substituted valuation unconditionally while denying the plaintiff the option of a new jury trial. The challenged mandamus restriction is reversed insofar as it requires unconditional reduced recovery and forecloses that election, and the case is remanded for proceedings consistent with the choice between a properly determined remittitur and an appropriate new trial.

**Authority:** Part II of the per curiam opinion, joined by all nine Justices: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. This common election floor controls without requiring adoption of each Justice's distinct position on the broader appellate-allocation question in Gasperini.

**Controlling explanation:** Gasperini expressly preserves the federal remittitur election. The choice allows lawful correction of excessiveness while preventing a court's valuation from becoming an unconditional replacement for the jury's. Dimick preserves the traditional remittitur distinction, and Kennon v. Gilmer recognizes the need to leave the new-trial choice when a court reduces a jury's award. Calling the restriction a mandate does not remove the jury protection.

The initial reduction from $750,000 to $500,000 because one supporting claim was legally insufficient differs from the later excessiveness-based reductions. Neely's liability-insufficiency setting does not justify replacing a supported-liability jury valuation without the election. No legally fixed recovery cap is established for the challenged restriction; comparisons with awards in other cases do not create one. BMW's state punitive-damages remedy likewise supplies no exception to this federal compensatory-jury rule.

The Court does not find the original award reasonable, restore $750,000 or $500,000, select $50,000 or $15,000, revive the legally insufficient claim or reverse the earlier excessiveness determination. The scope of any new trial must follow ordinary law, including separability from liability before a damages-only trial. The later $15,000 judgment and pending separate appeal are not themselves vacated or decided. This remand removes the unlawful mandamus constraint; it does not promise a particular amount or an impermissibly partial retrial.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Gasperini's remittitur election is applied; its distinct diversity/state-compensation holding and existing federal appellate-allocation rule remain unchanged.
- Dimick v. Schiedt preserves traditional remittitur and the separate prohibition on federal additur.
- Kennon v. Gilmer, 131 U.S. 22, supplies the election principle when judicial reduction substitutes for the jury's damages valuation.
- Neely v. Martin K. Eby Construction Co., 386 U.S. 317, is distinguished as a liability-insufficiency decision, not authority for compulsory excessiveness remittitur.
- BMW v. Gore retains its state punitive-damages remedy and does not displace this federal compensatory-jury protection.
- In re Sanford Fork & Tool Co., 160 U.S. 247, supplies the settled/open mandate distinction; Messenger v. Anderson, 225 U.S. 436, distinguishes law-of-the-case practice from jurisdiction. Neither creates a general excuse for omitted earlier-petition issues. No precedent is overruled.

#### Law After Decision

The existing federal remittitur election remains enforceable against an unconditional appellate mandate. Review of this later restriction does not create a general waiver exception or reopen every earlier mandate. The original excessiveness determination, legally insufficient claim and pending separate damages appeal retain their stated boundaries.

#### Separate Writings

No separate writing is filed.

#### Procedure After Action

The matter returns for lawful proceedings preserving the choice between a properly determined remittitur and an appropriate new jury trial. Any damages-only trial requires separability from liability. No award is selected or automatically restored, and the later $15,000 judgment and separate appeal are not themselves vacated or decided.

#### Source Notes

The [reported proceeding, 523 U.S. 208](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-523/pdf/USREPORTS-523-208.pdf), supplies the mandamus sequence, respondents' earlier-petition objection and the distinct pending appeal. Original earlier-petition and mandamus filings are known through published recitations rather than reproduced originals. No separate damages appeal, additional claim or dollar amount is resolved by this review.

### United States v. Robinson; Fifth Circuit No. 96-11165. — 1998-03-23

#### Event

United States v. Robinson; Fifth Circuit No. 96-11165, judgment below 119 F.3d 1205. Plenary merits decision, March 23, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 5. On review under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) of the August 8, 1997 judgment. The grant concerns facial and as-applied Commerce Clause objections to the Hobbs Act convictions, including the proposed requirement of a substantial individual effect and the derivative firearm objection. The convictions comprise conspiracy, aiding and abetting three business robberies, and aiding and abetting one firearm offense. The Fifth Circuit affirmed the convictions but separately vacated the sentence and remanded on the vulnerable-victim enhancement; that sentencing component is outside the grant. No separate Supreme Court docket or oral-argument date is established.

#### Participation

All nine Justices participate at submission and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

#### Public Action

The judgment sustaining the convictions is affirmed insofar as the grant concerns the Hobbs Act commerce challenge and the wholly derivative firearm objection.

#### Judgment & Remedy

| Component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Remedy or limit |
|---|---|---|---|
| Facial and as-applied commerce challenge to the Hobbs Act robbery and conspiracy convictions | Affirmed in the reviewed respect, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Convictions remain effective against this commerce objection |
| Wholly derivative firearm commerce challenge | Affirmed in the reviewed respect, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Stone-Zsela agrees with disposition only; no independent firearm defect is adjudicated |

The Fifth Circuit's separate sentencing vacatur and resentencing mandate remain undisturbed. The Court does not reinstate the vacated sentence or enhancement, order a new trial or new sentencing proceeding, or adjudicate an unrelated indictment, instruction or sufficiency objection.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | Kennedy | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Rejects the Hobbs Act commerce objections | Direct commercial-interference mechanism, aggregation after connection, robbery/conspiracy application and limits |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II | Kennedy | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Rejects the derivative firearm objection | Dependent constitutional ground only; Stone-Zsela agrees with this judgment component without joining its reasoning |

No separate writing is filed.

#### Holdings

### I. Direct interference with operating commercial assets supplies the required commerce connection

**Holding and operative rule:** Congress may apply the Hobbs Act to robbery directed at the operating assets of an active business participating in interstate commerce when the taking directly impairs its commercial operations and the Act's enacted commerce element is actually established. The effect of one robbery may be slight: aggregation measures the magnitude of this directly connected economic class, but neither a business label nor generalized economic consequences of crime supplies the antecedent connection.

**Authority:** Part I of Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; all nine directly adopt the rule, application to the Hobbs Act components and limits.

**Controlling explanation:** Lopez requires the enacted class and its actual interstate mechanism before magnitude can establish federal authority. The relevant class here is robbery affecting commerce through direct interference with operating business capital, not all robbery. The targeted businesses purchased interstate inventory and cashed out-of-state, payroll and government-benefit checks. The takings removed money withdrawn for those operations and impaired restocking and check-cashing capacity; the published record also reports one business closing for lack of capital. Taking the working money in a bank drive-through lane or store parking lot does not change its established business function.

American Life League supplies the closest mechanism: conduct directed at an actual interstate market's operations can directly obstruct that market. Once that relationship exists, Wickard and Perez permit evaluation of class-wide effects without demanding a substantial effect from each individual act. The statutory commerce element still requires an actual offense-specific nexus. Its words alone would not validate a constitutionally inadequate connection, and its character as an element does not make it subject-matter jurisdiction.

The contrary argument properly rejects multiplying local losses as a substitute for a constitutional mechanism. But this record identifies that mechanism before aggregation. A theory treating every victim as a potential consumer or every dollar as eventual interstate spending would lack this boundary and is not adopted. Hale, Rybar and Kirk concern the distinct inclusion of possession necessary to a valid machinegun regime; no such anti-evasion rationale is needed here.

The facial commerce objection fails because the Act lawfully reaches this defined class; the Court does not validate every imagined application. The actual business-robbery components and conspiracy directed at those robberies likewise require no substantial individual effect. This is no universal conspiracy-elements rule. Purely personal or household robbery, remote historical movement, every asserted depletion theory, a universal dollar floor and constitutional validity without the enacted commerce connection remain undecided.

### II. The wholly derivative firearm objection fails with the predicate commerce challenge

**Holding and operative rule:** The presented constitutional objection to the firearm conviction supplies no ground for relief because it depends entirely on the rejected claim that the underlying Hobbs Act business robberies exceed the commerce power. This disposition adjudicates no independent firearm element, instruction, sufficiency, aiding-and-abetting or collateral defect.

**Authority:** Part II of Kennedy's opinion, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; eight Justices directly adopt this derivative ground. Stone-Zsela agrees with the firearm disposition only and adds no ground.

**Controlling explanation:** The lower opinion identifies the firearm constitutional challenge as dependent on invalidity of the Hobbs Act predicates. Part I rejects that asserted invalidity through the actual business-interference mechanism and proved commerce nexus. The derivative argument therefore fails on its own stated premise. Neither this conclusion nor Salinas's distinct statutory-conspiracy analysis resolves an independent defect that is not presented. The Court does not infer a new admission, revise the indictment or approve the whole jury charge. The separate lower sentencing vacatur remains effective; the commerce affirmance neither reinstates the vulnerable-victim enhancement nor creates a new resentencing or retrial order.

#### Precedent Treatment

- Lopez is applied without changing its connection-before-magnitude sequence, actual statutory-class inquiry, fit requirement or rejection of a general police power. An enacted commerce element is relevant but no automatic safe harbor.
- American Life League supplies the direct-market-interference mechanism; its service-market application is extended to the actual impairment of these businesses' commercial operations, not to all crime costs.
- Wickard and Perez preserve class-wide magnitude analysis once a genuine economic-market connection exists; neither requires substantial individual magnitude or manufactures a missing connection.
- Hale, Rybar and Kirk retain their distinct anti-evasion/material-impairment ground for the machinegun regime. That ground is not transferred to robbery.
- Parker and Mussari/Schroeder retain the distinct cross-border-payment mechanism, and Pic-A-State retains its actual interstate-transmission ground; none supplies an all-money rule.
- Salinas's agreement analysis remains statute-specific and supplies no universal conspiracy code. Robertson's direct-enterprise alternative is not reached. No precedent is overruled.

#### Law After Decision

Robbery directly impairing the actual commercial operations of an interstate business may be regulated when the enacted Hobbs Act commerce connection is established. Aggregation addresses class-wide magnitude after that connection exists; no substantial individual effect is constitutionally required for this class. Purely personal robbery, generic consumer-spending chains and other reserved applications remain distinct. The firearm result is confined to the derivative objection, and the lower sentencing vacatur remains effective.

#### Separate Writings

No separate writing is filed.

#### Procedure After Action

This Supreme Court commerce review is complete. The convictions stand against these objections. The separate lower-court resentencing mandate continues under its own terms, without reinstatement of the vacated sentence or a new Supreme Court sentencing order.

#### Source Notes

The [official Fifth Circuit opinion, 119 F.3d 1205](https://www.ca5.uscourts.gov/opinions/pub/96/96-11165.CR0.wpd.pdf), supplies the businesses' interstate inventory and check-cashing functions, targeted working-capital takings, commerce objections and separate sentencing vacatur. The [reported text](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/119/119.F3d.1205.96-11165.html) includes the complete lower analysis and footnotes. No exact dollar threshold, independent firearm defect, additional indictment finding or general instruction approval is supplied.


## Material Published Noncontrolling Positions

### City of Monroe — November 17, 1997

[Public Projection](../records/city_of_monroe_v_united_states_summary_merits_1997-11-17.md#public-projection). Souter, joined by Breyer, dissents from Part II and the judgment. He treats the practical shift from established plurality voting as the relevant local change and concludes that approval of a facial statewide default does not adequately disclose it merely because the charter was silent. City of Rome, McCain, Clark, Lopez, Young, and Foreman require identification of that practical change. The dissent agrees with Part I's approval-scope/substantive-approval boundary. It would retain prospective nonuse and the plurality injunction, leaving discrimination and completed-election consequences open. That local-change application is noncontrolling.

### Jeffries — November 17, 1997

[Public Projection](../records/jeffries_v_harleston_merits_1997-11-17.md#public-projection). Stone-Zsela's separate writing concurs in vacatur and Part II but would classify the address as citizen speech as a matter of law and categorically exclude offense, controversy, audience hostility, embarrassment, reputational pressure, and viewpoint-reaction disruption as sufficient grounds for punishment of this private public-concern speech. Title and subject matter do not convert the unaffiliated address into university speech. Assigned teaching, administration, and delegated chair speech remain controllable; independently proved nonperformance, threats, harassment, official discrimination, insubordination, misuse of authority, and other regulable misconduct remain lawful grounds. He requires proof of each defendant's substantial or motivating causal role and permits an independently lawful same-decision defense, but considers conflicting verdicts and distinct roles to require a limited new trial on those matters, immunity, and relief. He would neither reinstate inconsistent verdicts nor decide particular liability, immunity, damages, or precise prospective relief. His categorical substantive limit and compulsory-trial remedy lack a Court majority; his Part II join contributes to controlling law as identified above.

O'Connor, joined by Scalia and Thomas, would affirm on the employment balance. She gives weight to the unaffiliated public-concern setting and nonpolicymaking role, but finds that consequential administrative responsibilities and supported recruitment, fundraising, faculty, and community interests justify the chair-term action in light of the reasonable-expectation finding without waiting for actual harm. The dissent does not make sincere belief, offensive content, retained tenure, or the forecast automatically decisive, invent sponsorship or deficient performance, or adopt the nine-vote cause rationale or final immunity ruling as an alternative ground. Its balance and affirmance are noncontrolling.

### Rybar — November 24, 1997

[Public Projection](../records/united_states_v_rybar_merits_1997-11-24.md#public-projection). Stone-Zsela concurs in Parts I–II but dissents from Part III and would affirm Counts I and III through an independent arms application. Fully automatic repeated fire through one trigger function supplies the relevant characteristic; restricting additions to civilian stocks of these completed weapons directly addresses its distinctive sustained-fire risk. Registration traces arms, while licensing and records alone do not prevent stock expansion. He finds that preventive fit sufficient for this substantial burden in these transactions. Prior lawful stock is a transition limit, not proof of greater safety or an age-based exception. Government channels do not make military usefulness disqualifying or immune; available ordinary firearms describe scope but substitutes alone do not justify a protected-arm prohibition. His position relies on identified weapons, not invented casualty findings, creates no universal dangerous- or military-arms rule or equivalence to ordinary semiautomatics, and resolves no components or accessories. Dismissed transfer charges supply no alternative conviction ground. This application is noncontrolling; the Court leaves it for remand.

### Richenberg — December 8, 1997

[Public Projection](../records/richenberg_v_cohen_merits_1997-12-08.md#public-projection). Stevens, joined by Stone-Zsela, Souter, and Ginsburg, would reverse and set the discharge aside on an as-applied equality ground. The actual explanations inadequately connect ordinary attraction to likely prohibited conduct or explain rejection of the sworn undertaking and supporting evidence; receipt of rebuttal evidence is insufficient if the reasons deprive it of meaningful effect. Hypothetical lawful relationships and uncertainty about all future conduct do not supply the missing link. Military deference gives the Government's credibility reading weight but does not permit an appellate inference to replace the actual decision's required connection. The dissent preserves the policy and ordinary scrutiny, requires neither completed acts nor individual operational harm, and makes neither good service nor denial automatically dispositive. Lawful further military proceedings remain available without unconditional reinstatement, backpay, or resolution of every retention qualification. It adopts no independent APA, speech, or due-process ground.

Stone-Zsela's additional dissenting statement joins that equality ground and complete set-aside remedy, specifying that further military action must rest on a supported individualized finding of actual conduct, propensity, intent, or credibility under a lawful rule. Inability to predict every future relationship does not itself establish intent to violate military rules; additional explanation alone would not substitute for removing this discharge. Both dissenting positions are noncontrolling.

[Public Projection](../records/jefferson_v_city_of_tarrant_jurisdictional_dismissal_1997-12-09.md#public-projection). ### Chunk-5 published noncontrolling positions

#### Glendora v. Porzio, No. 97-7300. — 1998-03-09

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to action | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion and order of the Court, Part I | Per curiam | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Denies present financial leave | Particular-paper assessment and paid-compliance opportunity |
| Opinion and order of the Court, Part II | Per curiam | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Declines future restriction | Separate fit and inadequacy-of-individual-treatment ground |
| Dissent from Part I, joining Part II | Stevens | Stone-Zsela | Would grant present financial leave | Discretionary access for ordinary petition screening |

These published joins disclose no vote on the underlying certiorari petition.

**Stevens, joined by Stone-Zsela, dissenting from Part I and joining Part II.** The dissent would accept the present papers under the financial-leave rules for ordinary petition screening. The concrete service and hearing allegations warrant that discretionary course despite the serious independent obstacles. Alleged false service and judicial acceptance do not themselves establish state action by private counsel, conspiracy, or an available collateral challenge to the state judgment. Martin permits supported present denials but does not compel denial in every difficult case; the dissent's preferred exercise of discretion does not displace the Court's rule. Granting financial leave would neither excuse a jurisdictional defect nor revive the Second Circuit appeal, establish the allegations, grant certiorari, appoint counsel, or award relief. The dissent joins the separate refusal of prospective controls because present deficiencies and filing volume do not establish inadequacy of individual treatment.

#### Gray v. Maryland, No. 96-8653. — 1998-03-09

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Direct-accusation redaction rule and application |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II | O'Connor | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Preservation-qualified Chapman review and absence of an automatic retrial or acquittal |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stone-Zsela | — | Vacate and remand; would direct determination of harmless error | Joins Part I; states immediate-context application and a distinct affirmative remand instruction |
| Dissent | Scalia | Kennedy and Thomas | Would affirm on the preserved redaction question | Richardson facial-versus-inferential line, limiting instruction and separate preservation of prosecutorial-use questions |

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and in the judgment.** Stone-Zsela joins Part I. The blanks, grammar, limited named participants and prosecutor's immediate connection between Bell's information and Gray's arrest directly identify whose name was removed. The jury need not wait for later independent evidence to identify the accused, so an instruction cannot cure the uncross-examined accusation. His reliance on immediate context explains the preserved admission objection; it creates no separate ruling that the follow-up question itself was a preserved independent constitutional wrong. He would direct the state court to determine harmless error, without deciding it here, and permit consideration of materially different redactions or lawful trial-management alternatives. He does not join Part II's limitation of the remand instruction to a properly available harmless-error contention. Joint trials, genuinely neutral wording, independently admissible confessions and cross-examined testimony remain outside his prohibition.

**Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas, dissenting.** The dissent would apply Richardson's distinction between a confession that identifies the accused on its face and one that becomes incriminating through inference from other evidence. Bell's statement contains two deletions, while other testimony describes six participants and only one other defendant is at this trial. A blank does not uniquely identify Gray without a further inference. Conspicuous editing alone is not the powerfully incriminating express accusation for which Bruton displaces the ordinary presumption that jurors follow limiting instructions. The dissent objects to a rule encouraging concealed edits or substantive rewriting, and would affirm on the preserved confession-admission issue. It does not treat instructions as a cure for every confrontation problem or eliminate Rule 14 severance and evidentiary protection beyond the constitutional minimum. The prosecutor's follow-up question presents a separate issue: the State says an arrest warrant preceded the confession and characterizes the question as a transition, and no contemporaneous objection to that question is identified. The dissent would not use it to circumvent preservation or to transform the preserved redaction question into a different adjudication.

#### Quality King Distributors, Inc. v. L'anza Research International, Inc., No. 96-1470. — 1998-03-09

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | Scalia | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Rejects the categorical importation rule | Complete owner-or-authorized-person rule, statutory linkage and domestic-manufacture limit |
| Same opinion, partial join | Scalia | Stone-Zsela | Agrees with reversal | Lawful-owner reimportation proposition; no join in the authorized-person extension |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II | Scalia | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Reverses and returns for consistent further proceedings | No directed final judgment or new transaction findings |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone-Zsela | — | Agrees with reversal; would direct judgment for Quality King on the copyright-importation claim | Joins Part I only as to lawful-owner reimportation; rejects Part II's open remand in favor of the stated final relief |

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** Stone-Zsela joins Part I only insofar as it addresses copies made in the United States, sold abroad with the copyright owner's authorization and later reimported by their lawful owner. He does not join its broader rule for a person authorized by that owner. Section 602(a) invokes the §106(3) right, which §109(a) limits; crossing the border cannot recreate an exhausted distribution right, and territorial-pricing policy cannot do so either. He would direct entry of judgment for Quality King on the claim that this reimportation infringed that distribution right through §602(a), rather than leave that theory for further proceedings. Any preserved independent contract, trademark, labeling or consumer-protection claim remains governed by its own law. His disposition would not decide foreign manufacture, unauthorized reproductions, counterfeits, reproduction rights, trademark confusion or contractual duties enforceable against a contracting party.

#### Aktepe v. United States; Eleventh Circuit No. 96-2167. — 1998-03-10

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas and Breyer | Affirms | Complete claim-specific nonjusticiability ground and limits |
| Dissent | Stevens | Stone-Zsela, Souter and Ginsburg | Would vacate and remand | Issue-specific separation of execution allegations from policy choices; no guaranteed surviving claim |

**Stevens, joined by Stone-Zsela, Souter and Ginsburg, dissenting.** The dissent would vacate and remand for separate examination of the claims. The intended simulation and accidental live firing supply a concrete reason to distinguish execution from design; listing training and command purposes does not establish that every allegation requires choosing policy. Gilligan's reservation of specific-injury cases and Nixon's claim-specific limitation oppose the blanket effect of the judgment. Weiss and Loving preserve enforceable legal constraints despite military subject matter, and Dalton does not supply general executive insulation.

The dissent accepts that a court cannot invent a safer training rule, call it operational, and thereby evade the political-question doctrine. The remand would require the Eleventh Circuit to identify which allegations actually require selection of constitutionally committed military policy or lack manageable standards, without guaranteeing a surviving negligence claim, discovery, liability or compensation. No particular written protocol is found or presumed violated. Statutory coverage, sovereign immunity, Feres, causation, damages and the justiciability of any specifically identified high-level policy challenge remain unresolved. The dissent's proposed examination is not the Court's mandate.

#### W.P. v. Verniero; Third Circuit No. 96-5416. — 1998-03-16

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | Souter | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer | Rejects punishment relief | Common civil classification under both presented clauses |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II | Souter | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Preserves advance review and State persuasion for tier and plan | Substantive state constraint, hearing and burden allocation |
| Opinion of the Court, Part III-A | Souter | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer | Affirms heightened proof for risk classification | Clear and convincing factual basis and tier determination |
| Opinion announcing the judgment, Part III-B | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer | Affirms heightened proof for notification plan | Four-Justice plan-specific explanation; Stone-Zsela agrees only with this judgment component |
| Opinion of the Court, Part IV | Souter | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Leaves emergency issue unripe | No approval or categorical prohibition of exceptional notice procedure; Stone-Zsela agrees in disposition only |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stone-Zsela | — | Affirms | Joins Parts I and III-A; separate ordinary-process safeguards; judgment-only agreement on Parts II and III-B; emergency disposition only |
| Dissent in part, joining Parts II, III-A, III-B and IV | Stevens | — | Would grant retrospective Ex Post Facto relief; otherwise joins the indicated process disposition | Punitive-operation objection; conditional process joins when civil notification remains available |
| Concurrence in part in the judgment and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas in full; O'Connor and Kennedy on proof quantum only | Rejects punishment relief; would remove heightened proof while retaining State persuasion and advance review | Separate successive-criminal-prosecution ground and preponderance process ground; joins Parts II and IV |

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and in the judgment.** Stone-Zsela joins the civil-classification and heightened risk-classification portions. Public protection and a civil structure do not remove the need for timely notice, disclosure of the basis, an opportunity to contest, a neutral decisionmaker, written reasons and clear and convincing State proof before the broad notification at issue. The State controls the evidence and proposes the classification; an erroneous high tier can irreversibly affect housing, work, safety and status. A prompt adversarial process can reduce error without defeating protection. His express affirmance includes retention of the notification-plan injunction, but he adopts no plan-specific explanation and does not join the separate state-entitlement analysis. On the unripe emergency component he agrees only with the disposition and adds no emergency ground. His stated safeguards supply no majority adoption of a new general written-reasons or disclosure code. Confidential registration and lower-tier treatment remain subject to their own law; every internet registry, lifetime classification without review and affirmatively punitive operation remain unresolved.

**Stevens, dissenting in part.** Stevens would reverse the denial of Ex Post Facto relief and require an injunction against retrospective Tier 2/3 notification for this defined class. The affirmative official broadcast of conviction and predicted dangerousness, its criminal-history trigger and enduring cumulative burdens resemble imposed shaming. Risk and audience limits deserve weight but do not alter that combined character on his assessment. Ursery's dissenting approach and the actual-operation holdings of Hendricks and Hudson support looking beyond the civil label. He does not attribute every private attack to the State or decide independent Double Jeopardy offense identity. Because the Court leaves civil notification available, he joins the indicated process portions, including clear and convincing proof for both tier and plan.

**Souter's Part III-B, joined by Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer.** These four would apply clear and convincing persuasion to the plan as well as the classification because unnecessary geographic or audience expansion causes the same irreversible disclosure. The State prepares the plan and its initial justification; factual disputes, atypical risks and the actual likely-encounter limit make stronger assurance useful without demanding certainty. Continuing registration and monitoring reduce the comparative cost of mistaken undernotification. This is the rationale of four Justices, not a controlling general plan-quantum rule.

**Scalia, joined by Thomas; O'Connor and Kennedy join the proof-quantum portion only.** Scalia and Thomas reject the Ex Post Facto challenge because the particular risk-tailored warning scheme is genuinely civil and protective on this record. They separately reject the Double Jeopardy challenge because that civil warning process is not a successive criminal prosecution. They preserve their Hudson disagreement with a freestanding Halper civil multiple-punishment method; that view does not overrule Halper. All four proof-quantum dissenters accept the substantive state interest, advance opportunity and State persuasion for both tier and plan, but would permit preponderance. Mathews also accounts for potential victims, the danger of underclassification and existing safeguards. Noncustodial tailored warnings do not impose Addington's physical custody or Santosky's family-status severance; neither serious consequences nor a protected interest mechanically requires heightened certainty. Sahhar's different commitment and discharge rules demonstrate the need to preserve the actual legal operation. Their proposed modification would remove only the heightened quantum, preserving the remainder of the ordinary-process injunction.

#### Caterpillar Inc. v. International Union, UAW, No. 96-1925. — 1998-03-23

The Clerk enters the Rule 46.1 dismissal. No opinion of the Court or separate writing is issued, and no judicial authorship or adjudicative tally is attributed to this administrative act.

No separate writing is issued.

#### Hetzel v. Prince William County, No. 97-954. — 1998-03-23

No opinion or separate writing accompanies this grant.

No separate writing is issued.


## Current Procedure and Institution

The opening nine-seat roster, seniority, August 3, 1994 circuit allotments, and standing application-referral practice remain unchanged. Participation and any clerical action are stated event by event in the Records; no new recusal, oath condition, vacancy, or allotment is established.

### Court setting and standing institutional practice

The following setting is carried from the opening public projection of Standing State §§1–3, consistent with the governing [Court Composition](../../../foundation/COURT_COMPOSITION.md).

### 1. Current Court

All nine seats are occupied at the opening of OT1997. The Supreme Court Composition register controls the roster, service dates and seniority. All nine Justices are seated before OT1997 opens, subject to established case-specific nonparticipation; no vacancy or new oath-date participation condition applies.

**Allotment rule:** A membership change triggers a complete reallotment. No Justice inherits a predecessor's circuits. Under the Composition register, Stone reassesses the entire map for institutional effectiveness; an express administrative order may also change an allotment.

### 3. Standing Practices

**Referral of applications, effective October 7, 1991; signed explanations and certification, effective October 2, 1995.** By the Court's internal practice adopted at the opening of October Term 1991, an application for a stay, injunction, or other interim relief in a matter on the Term's inventory is referred by the Circuit Justice to the full Court and decided by the participating Justices under the participating-majority rule. The order issues in the Court's name and recites the presenting Circuit Justice ("presented to Justice ___ and by him or her referred to the Court"). From October 2, 1995, referral does not prevent the Circuit Justice, or the substitute the term-effective Rules designate, from taking a necessary temporary action in his or her own name while the Court considers the application. Each action is explained in short signed writings, including the Circuit Justice's own explanation at final disposition, whether prevailing or dissenting, and a statement by each participating Justice in each Court action deciding relief, and is certified for issuance by the Chief Justice or the Chief's substitute. A Justice may participate in a referred application from the date of the judicial oath. An application not on the Term's inventory remains subject to ordinary Circuit Justice practice. Source: the OT1991 opening practice carried in the opening Standing State and the OT1993 Term Continuity Note.

All fifty earlier Court events remain completed. Chunk 5 adds 11 completed events through March 23, 1998 and leaves 2 matters stopped for the exact reasons below. The 101 scheduled items still open or stopped receive no outcome here. Brown, both Arteaga petitions, Glendora and the five opening carryovers retain their separately stated pending stages. Each Public Projection retains its legal reservations and source limits.

 The outstanding legal reservations and limited mandates above remain substantive boundaries, not missing holdings to be filled by inference. Brown's next certiorari action and all five carryovers require supported dates and procedural acts. Rybar's ultimate arms application, Salinas's distinct expenditure-nexus and outer constitutional questions, Jefferson's federal remedy merits, and Trest's discretionary-initiative questions remain expressly open. Jeffries's actual claim scope, verdict materials, preservation, conduct-date immunity, and live relief govern its further proceedings; Richenberg leaves independent APA and ungranted claims unresolved.

Hudson's offense-identity, fines, and due-process questions; Bay Area Laundry's actual due dates, effective acceleration, and remaining assessment or arbitration issues; Joiner's alternative-exposure theory; Chicago's properly remaining discretion and abstention arguments; and Clanton's distinct confession, 1993 notice, factual, and ultimate liability questions retain their stated boundaries. Tanford's relief remains limited to the established official worship practice. Valot's actual personal responsibility, attribution, 1993 notice, injury, remedy, and dependent-theory questions remain open; the Board admission and ordinary economic rationality are not substitutes for their predicates. Smith's actual encounter, remaining elements and defenses, and possible fact-dependent immunity reconsideration remain open; its proposed replacement defense does not alter the governing law. Baker preserves ordinary evidence protections, other appellate grounds, genuine personal enforcement, and rendering-state effect without inventing universal nonparty exceptions; Fink adjudicates only the reviewed twenty-day enabling-loan timing defense. Rogers establishes no harmless-error, structural-error, silencer-knowledge, or waiver merits rule; the published charge readings keep their stated noncontrolling status, and no separate numerical tally is supplied. Rouse preserves accrued effects, unadjudicated independent claims, meaningful access, and remedies for actual later violations; it does not decide an amended-conjunction application that excludes an actually supported violation which the former safeguard would have preserved.

A later packet must be refreshed if any relevant earlier effective holding, new source, participation fact, or procedural action changes its entering premises. Preserve the common baseline for independent same-day events. The record-specific source limits above—including unreproduced filings or instructions, assumed rather than adjudicated predicates, and analytical rather than quoted questions—must travel with affected propositions.

The [case index](../case-list.md), as reflected in the current [manifest](manifest.md), contains 161 scheduled matters across 13 chunks: 151 MERITS, one ORIGINAL, four APPLICATION, and five DISMISSAL, with dates October 20, 1997–September 28, 1998. Chunk 13 contains fifteen matters, scheduled five each on September 14, 21, and 28, 1998. No event is scheduled after September 28. ## Source and Research Cutoff

This cumulative replacement carries the identified opening public workspace state and the authoritative Public Projections of all 61 Court-action Records: twelve in chunk 1, twelve in chunk 2, thirteen in chunk 3, thirteen in chunk 4 and 11 in chunk 5. All thirteen chunk-4 Public Projections are copied in full above, and any separately identified additional published continuity is retained. Their linked Record locations identify the authoritative public sections. The additional statutory source is carried from its admitted public projection, with its application in Rouse supplied only by that public decision; no other judicial construction or individual procedural effect is inferred.

Each current-term holding is effective from its own listed event date. The research and source boundary is event-specific: October 20; November 4, 17, and 24; and December 1, 2, 8, 9, 10, 15, 22, and 29, 1997; and January 5, 12, 13, 14, 15, 20, 21, 26 and 29, and February 2, 9, 12, 13, 14, 17, 23, 24, 25 and 26, and March 2, 3, 4, 9, 10, 16 and 23, 1998, with the statutory source effective November 26, 1997. Later information does not retroactively enter an earlier event or an uncoordinated same-day peer. The latest covered Court event date is March 23, 1998; that cursor is not a claim that every connected external event has been comprehensively searched through that date. This projection conducts no independent external-event sweep. Any newly recovered intervening event must be checked for source support, legal effect, and chronology before the next affected event.

For continuation, use the opening publications, the public holdings and separate writings linked above, the [manifest](manifest.md), and the dated neutral case packet and entering-law reading slice for each later item, beginning with Cohen v. de la Cruz on March 24, 1998. Refresh affected packets against every earlier effective current-term change and its reservations. The full opinion and source limits represented by a Public Projection remain controlling over a shorthand summary; no unreported finding, quotation, preservation, waiver, or deadline is supplied here.

Chunk-5 source coverage extends only through each decision's own March 1998 effective date, latest March 23. This is not an assertion that all external events were searched. The twelve supplied matters and exact open-stage conditions are accounted for above. The next supplied event is Cohen on March 24; prior dated source excerpts are not current pending-item instructions.
